How to use this page

This is a study companion for the official CrossFit Level 1 Training Guide (258 pages) — it does not replace reading the guide. Work it the same way as the article guide: read a section's chapter here first, then open the official guide to that section and read it deeply — hunt down each Find & Highlight item, highlight it in the guide, and check it off (your progress saves in this browser).

The Level 1 & Level 2 guides are the CORE of the CCFT material — they are the methodology the whole exam is built on. Give them weight.

L1 · Section 1 of 27 Tier: ___

Understanding CrossFit

Open the official guide

Lays out what makes CrossFit unique through five lenses — its aims, prescription, methodology, implementation, and adaptations.

Key ideas to understand

  • CrossFit is defined by five things that are unique both individually and together: its aims, prescription, methodology, implementation, and adaptations.
  • AIMS: From the start, the aim has been to forge a broad, general, and inclusive fitness — to prepare people for any physical contingency, 'not only for the unknown but for the unknowable.'
  • The aim was reverse-engineered by looking at all sport and physical tasks together and asking which skills and adaptations would most universally improve performance — capacity at the intersection of all sports logically serves all sport. In short: 'our specialty is not specializing.'
  • PRESCRIPTION: CrossFit is 'constantly varied, high-intensity functional movement.' That one phrase is the prescription.
  • Functional movements are universal motor recruitment patterns; they move in a wave of contraction from core to extremity; and they are compound (multi-joint). They're natural, effective, efficient ways to move your body and outside objects.
  • The most important trait of functional movements is their capacity to move large loads over long distances quickly — those three attributes (load, distance, speed) uniquely qualify them to produce high power.
  • Intensity is defined exactly as power. And intensity is the independent variable most associated with maximizing the rate of return of favorable adaptation — i.e., results come fastest from intensity.
  • The breadth and depth of the program's stimulus determines the breadth and depth of the adaptation — so the prescription is constantly varied, because preparing for random (unknown and unknowable) challenges is at odds with fixed, predictable, routine programs.
  • METHODOLOGY: CrossFit's methodology is entirely empirical. Meaningful claims about the three most important, interdependent facets — safety, efficacy, and efficiency — can only be supported by measurable, observable, repeatable data. This is 'evidence-based fitness.'
  • The methodology depends on full disclosure of methods, results, and criticisms, delivered through the internet. The charter is open source, turning participating coaches, athletes, and trainers into co-developers through an online community. CrossFit is empirically driven, clinically tested, and community developed.
  • IMPLEMENTATION: In practice, CrossFit is a sport — the Sport of Fitness. Harnessing the camaraderie, competition, and fun of sport produces an intensity no other method matches.
  • Col. Jeff Cooper's line — 'the fear of sporting failure is worse than the fear of death' — captures it; the observation is that 'men will die for points.'
  • The tools of sport — whiteboards as scoreboards, accurate scores and records, a running clock, and precisely defined rules and standards — don't just motivate huge output, they also produce relative and absolute metrics at every workout, and that data has value far beyond motivation.
  • ADAPTATIONS: The commitment to evidence-based fitness, public data, collaborative development, and an open-source charter let CrossFit learn precisely what adaptations its programming produces.
  • The key discovery: CrossFit increases work capacity across broad time and modal domains. This is treated as a discovery of great importance that now drives the programming.
  • This far-reaching rise in work capacity supports the original aim of broad, general, inclusive fitness, and explains CrossFit's success across diverse sports.
  • Increased work capacity is seen as the 'Holy Grail' of performance improvement; other common metrics — VO2 max, lactate threshold, body composition, even strength and flexibility — are treated as correlates or derivatives. CrossFit would not trade improvement in any other metric for a decrease in work capacity.
  • CONCLUSIONS: Publicly posting daily workouts on the internet (started 2001) grew into a community where human performance is measured and publicly recorded against multiple, diverse, fixed workloads.
  • CrossFit is an open-source engine: inputs from anywhere can be publicly given to demonstrate fitness and programming, and coaches, athletes, and trainers collectively advance the art and science of optimizing human performance.

What you should get from it

These five headings ARE the structure of the whole article — each one is a distinct lens on what makes CrossFit unique.

What you should get from it

The aim is readiness for anything life throws at you — not just events you can predict, but ones you can't even imagine.

What you should get from it

CrossFit deliberately refuses to specialize — broad capacity across all sports is the whole point.

What you should get from it

Memorize this — it's the one-line definition of what CrossFit prescribes.

What you should get from it

Functional movements have three signatures: they're universal patterns, they fire from core out to the limbs, and they cross multiple joints.

What you should get from it

Move a big load, a long way, fast — that combination is what produces power, which is the whole game.

What you should get from it

Intensity = power, and intensity is the single biggest driver of how fast you get results.

What you should get from it

You can't prepare for the unpredictable with a predictable routine — so the program is constantly varied on purpose.

What you should get from it

Judge any program (including yours) on these three: is it safe, does it work, and is it efficient — and only data can answer.

What you should get from it

CrossFit's claims rest on measurable, repeatable data and an open community — not opinion or tradition.

What you should get from it

Turning fitness into a sport unlocks an intensity nothing else can — competition makes people work harder than they otherwise would.

What you should get from it

Scoring every workout both drives output AND generates the measurable data the whole methodology depends on.

What you should get from it

This is the headline finding — and it's the eventual definition of fitness itself (Part 2). Broad work capacity is what CrossFit produces.

What you should get from it

Work capacity is the master metric; the familiar fitness numbers are downstream of it — CrossFit wouldn't trade work capacity for any of them.

What you should get from it

CrossFit improves by being open — anyone can contribute, and the whole community advances the science of human performance together.

Words to know (plain English)

Aims (of CrossFit)
The goal the whole program is built toward: a broad, general, and inclusive fitness that prepares people for any physical challenge, known or unknowable.
Prescription
CrossFit's one-line formula for what to do: 'constantly varied, high-intensity functional movement.'
Functional movement
A natural, multi-joint (compound) movement that uses universal motor recruitment patterns and fires in a wave from core to extremity — the efficient way to move your body and external objects.
Core-to-extremity
The principle that powerful, functional movement starts at the center of the body (the core) and travels outward to the limbs in a wave of contraction.
Power (intensity)
Work done per unit of time — moving a load over a distance quickly. In CrossFit, intensity is defined exactly as power, and it's the main driver of fast results.
Constant variance
Deliberately changing the workouts so the program never becomes fixed or routine — necessary because you're preparing for unpredictable demands.
Evidence-based (empirical) fitness
Basing claims about a program only on measurable, observable, repeatable data — what can actually be tested and recorded.
Safety, efficacy, efficiency
The three most important and interdependent things to evaluate in any fitness program: is it safe, does it work, and does it get results without wasted effort.
Sport of Fitness
CrossFit implemented as a sport — using scoring, clocks, records, and competition to generate intensity and data that other training methods can't match.
Work capacity across broad time and modal domains
The ability to do work across many different durations (time domains) and many different types of activity (modal domains). CrossFit's central discovery is that it increases this — and it becomes the working definition of fitness.
Open source
An approach where methods, results, and data are fully disclosed publicly so anyone — coaches, athletes, trainers — can contribute and improve the program together.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A potential client asks why your gym programs something different every day instead of a fixed, repeatable routine. Which answer best reflects CrossFit's reasoning?

  1. Because we don't have the equipment to repeat workouts.
  2. Variety keeps members from getting bored, which is the only reason.
  3. Because the aim is fitness for the unknown and unknowable, and a fixed, predictable routine is at odds with preparing for random physical challenges.
  4. Because fixed routines are against the rules.
  5. Because variety burns more calories than anything else.
Show answer

Answer: C — Because the aim is fitness for the unknown and unknowable, and a fixed, predictable routine is at odds with preparing for random physical challenges.. C is the guide's actual logic — constant variance exists because you can't prepare for unpredictable demands with a predictable program. B names a side benefit, not the reason; D, A, and E are invented justifications with no basis in the text.

Scenario 2. Another coach claims his bootcamp is 'just as good as CrossFit' but keeps no scores, no clock, and no records. Based on Understanding CrossFit, what's the strongest critique?

  1. He should add more variety and that's the only gap.
  2. None — scoring doesn't matter as long as people sweat.
  3. Without measurable, repeatable data he can't actually support claims about safety, efficacy, or efficiency, and he loses the metrics that drive intensity and learning.
  4. His program is fine; CrossFit just markets better.
  5. He needs a bigger facility.
Show answer

Answer: C — Without measurable, repeatable data he can't actually support claims about safety, efficacy, or efficiency, and he loses the metrics that drive intensity and learning.. C captures two pillars at once: the empirical methodology (claims need data on safety/efficacy/efficiency) and implementation (scoreboards, clocks, and records both motivate and produce that data). B dismisses the core of evidence-based fitness; A and E miss the methodology point entirely; D ignores the substantive difference the article describes.

Scenario 3. A member asks whether she should worry that her VO2 max number hasn't improved much, even though she's getting much stronger and finishing workouts faster. How does CrossFit frame this?

  1. She should only track body composition instead.
  2. Increased work capacity across broad time and modal domains is the 'Holy Grail,' and metrics like VO2 max are correlates/derivatives — improving work capacity is what counts.
  3. She should stop CrossFit and switch to pure endurance training.
  4. Strength and speed are meaningless without VO2 max.
  5. VO2 max is the only metric that matters; she should be concerned.
Show answer

Answer: B — Increased work capacity across broad time and modal domains is the 'Holy Grail,' and metrics like VO2 max are correlates/derivatives — improving work capacity is what counts.. B reflects the Adaptations section — work capacity is the master metric and VO2 max, lactate threshold, body composition, strength, and flexibility are treated as correlates; CrossFit wouldn't trade work capacity for any single one. E and D overrate one isolated number; C abandons the broad approach for a specialty; A just swaps one narrow metric for another.

L1 · Section 2 of 27 Tier: ___

Foundations — The Case for Broad, Inclusive Fitness

Open the official guide

This is the founding argument for why CrossFit trains everything — all 10 physical skills, all three energy systems, the core lifts and a real diet — and why that broad approach beats specialization for everybody from your grandma to a pro.

Key ideas to understand

  • CrossFit is core strength and conditioning — not a niche program. The whole point is to build competence across 10 fitness domains at once: cardiovascular/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy. Know all 10 cold.
  • We chase the broadest possible adaptation. On top of that breadth, the program is distinctive for five things: maximizing neuroendocrine response, developing power, cross-training across modalities, constant practice with functional movements, and a sound diet.
  • Three training modalities cover the bases: metabolic conditioning (bike/run/swim/row at short, middle, long distances to hit all three energy systems), gymnastics (bodyweight — builds strength-to-weight ratio plus coordination/balance/agility/accuracy/flexibility), and weightlifting (the Olympic lifts — explosive power and motor recruitment).
  • We work compound movements and short, high-intensity cardio instead of isolation movements and long aerobic grinds. Push press replaces the lateral raise, pull-up replaces the curl, squat replaces the leg extension. For every long effort, do five or six short ones.
  • 'Is this for me?' Yes — your needs and an Olympic athlete's differ by degree, not kind. Same methods, scaled load. Grandma and the elite skier both need to squat; you just don't load them the same.
  • 'Bracketing': CrossFit proved its methods on the sedentary, overweight, pathological, and elderly AND on elite athletes. If it works at both ends of the bracket, it works for everyone in between.
  • Sickness–wellness–fitness is one continuum, not three separate things. Blood pressure, cholesterol, heart rate, body fat, muscle mass, flexibility, strength — all run from sick (pathological) to well (normal) to fit (better than normal). CrossFit holds that fitness and health are the same thing.
  • 'Core' has two meanings here. One: the fitness we build is foundational to everything else (like a college 'core curriculum' — the stuff everyone needs). Two: the literal center of the body — we train core to extremity, built around extension and flexion of the hips and trunk.
  • Powerful hip extension is 'necessary and nearly sufficient' for elite athletic performance. Running, jumping, punching, throwing all start at the core and recruit out to the extremities.
  • An hour a day, six days a week is enough for world-class age-group conditioning. The intensity that optimizes conditioning isn't sustainable past 45–60 minutes — past an hour, more is NOT better.
  • 'Fringe athletes' — marathoners, triathletes, sumo wrestlers, powerlifters — are so specialized their fitness is lopsided. Endurance athletes especially have trained past any cardiovascular health benefit and given up strength, speed, power. Elite conditioning is a compromise across all 10 skills; specialists don't balance that compromise.
  • Three energy systems fuel everything: the phosphagen system and the glycolytic (lactic acid) system are the two anaerobic ones; the oxidative (aerobic) system is the third. Properly structured anaerobic work builds high aerobic fitness WITHOUT the muscle wasting of high-volume aerobic training. All three are always working, but one dominates depending on duration and power output.
  • The two Olympic lifts — the clean and jerk and the snatch — are the centerpiece of weightlifting. They teach you to fire more muscle fibers faster, apply force core to extremity, and both DELIVER and RECEIVE force safely. They're the only lifts shown to raise max oxygen uptake.
  • 'There is no ideal routine.' The chief value of any routine is abandoning it for another. Whatever a routine leaves out is exactly what you won't adapt to — 'the breadth of adaptation will exactly match the breadth of the stimulus.' So vary constantly.
  • Neuroendocrine adaptation = a neurological or hormonal change from training. Heavy loads, short rest, high heart rates, and high intensity drive a big response (more testosterone, IGF, growth hormone); isolation movements drive almost none, which is a big reason they're weak.
  • Power = the time rate of doing work — 'hard and fast.' Power IS intensity, and intensity drives nearly every positive fitness result. Increasing power is necessary and nearly sufficient for elite athleticism.
  • Cross-training here means more than playing multiple sports — it means training past the normal motions, metabolic pathways, and modes of your sport. Athletes are weakest at the margins of their exposure, so we deliberately expand those margins.
  • Functional movements mimic real-life motor recruitment patterns, move multiple joints, are mechanically sound (and therefore safe), and elicit a high neuroendocrine response. Squatting = standing up from a seat; deadlifting = picking anything off the ground. Isolation movements (leg curl, leg extension) have no equivalent in nature.
  • The diet prescription: ~30% protein (lean, varied), ~40% carbohydrate (predominantly low-glycemic), ~30% fat (whole-food sources). Set total calories off protein needs — 0.7 g/lb lean body mass for moderate loads up to 1.0 g/lb for hardcore athletes.
  • In plain terms: eat meat and vegetables (especially greens), nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar. Shop the perimeter of the store; long shelf life is suspect. This lines up with the Paleolithic/caveman model.
  • The nutritional villain is excess high-glycemic carbohydrate. It spikes blood sugar, drives an outsized insulin response, and chronic elevation leads to hyperinsulinism — linked to obesity, high cholesterol, high blood pressure, mood problems, and a Pandora's box of disease.

What you should get from it

The mission is broad competence across 10 specific domains — memorize the list (CV/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, accuracy).

What you should get from it

Maximizing neuroendocrine response, developing power, cross-training, constant functional-movement practice, and sound diet — the five pillars layered on top of breadth.

What you should get from it

Compound functional movements at high intensity beat isolation work for nearly any fitness goal — and that's framed as scientific fact, not opinion.

What you should get from it

Needs differ by degree, not kind. Everyone needs to squat; you only change the load, not the movement.

What you should get from it

Proven at both extremes (elite athletes and sedentary/overweight/elderly), so it covers everyone in the middle.

What you should get from it

Fitness, wellness, and pathology are points on one continuum — the same measurable health, just at different levels. CrossFit treats fitness and health as the same thing.

What you should get from it

Core as 'foundational curriculum everyone needs' AND core as the literal center of the body — the hips and trunk. We train inside out, core to extremity.

What you should get from it

Hip extension is the engine of athleticism — running, jumping, punching, throwing all originate there.

What you should get from it

Optimal conditioning lives in 45–60 minutes of real intensity; longer sessions are skill/sport training, not better conditioning.

What you should get from it

Specialists are lopsided. Elite conditioning is a compromise across all 10 skills, and endurance athletes don't balance it — long-distance training is NOT the prerequisite fitness for most sports.

What you should get from it

Two anaerobic systems plus the aerobic system = three energy systems. Anaerobic work, done right, builds aerobic fitness without muscle wasting.

What you should get from it

Clean and jerk + snatch — unmatched for explosive power, core-to-extremity force, receiving force safely, and even raising VO2 max.

What you should get from it

Constant variance is the design principle. Whatever you leave out is exactly what you won't adapt to.

What you should get from it

These conditions drive the hormonal/neurological changes that produce champions — and isolation movements trigger almost none of it.

What you should get from it

Power is king. Power = intensity, and intensity drives nearly every positive fitness adaptation.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

The macro split and how to anchor total calories off protein needs by activity level.

What you should get from it

The simplest possible diet rule, consistent with the Paleolithic model.

What you should get from it

Excess high-glycemic carbohydrate is the main nutritional culprit; chronically elevated insulin (hyperinsulinism) is the mechanism behind the disease cluster.

Words to know (plain English)

Core strength and conditioning
Foundational, general fitness that underlies all athletic needs — the 'stuff everyone needs,' as opposed to sport-specific training. Also refers literally to training the body's center (hips and trunk).
The 10 general physical skills
The full menu of fitness CrossFit develops: cardiovascular/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy.
Bracketing
Proving a method works at both extremes of a population (elite athletes and the sedentary/overweight/elderly) so it's trusted to work for everyone in between.
Sickness–wellness–fitness continuum
The idea that health markers run on one scale from pathological (sick) to normal (well) to better-than-normal (fit) — so fitness and health are the same thing measured at different levels.
Core to extremity
The natural order in which sound functional movements recruit muscle — from the center of the body outward to the limbs — and the way we develop athletes.
Hip extension
Opening the hip from a bent to a straight position; the primary power-producing action of the body and 'necessary and nearly sufficient' for elite athleticism.
Fringe athletes
Highly specialized athletes (marathoners, triathletes, sumo wrestlers, powerlifters) whose fitness demands are so narrow they sacrifice broad physical competence.
Three energy systems / metabolic pathways
The phosphagen (phosphocreatine) system and the glycolytic (lactic acid) system — both anaerobic — plus the oxidative (aerobic) system. All three run at once, but one dominates by duration and intensity.
Aerobic vs. anaerobic
Aerobic = energy produced with oxygen, usually efforts longer than ~90 seconds at low-to-moderate power. Anaerobic = energy produced without oxygen, efforts under ~2 minutes at moderate-to-high power.
The Olympic lifts (weightlifting)
The two competition lifts — the clean and jerk and the snatch — prized for developing explosive power, core-to-extremity force, and the ability to receive force safely.
Neuroendocrine adaptation
A training-driven change that affects you neurologically or hormonally (e.g., testosterone, IGF, growth hormone). Heavy load, short rest, high heart rate, and high intensity maximize it.
Power
The time rate of doing work — 'hard and fast.' In CrossFit, power is the definition of intensity and the king of performance.
Cross-training (CrossFit sense)
Training beyond the normal motions, metabolic pathways, and modes of your usual sport — functional, metabolic, and modal cross-training — to cover your weak margins.
Functional movements
Multi-joint, compound movements that mimic real-life motor recruitment patterns (squat, deadlift), are mechanically sound and safe, and elicit a high neuroendocrine response.
Glycemic index
A measure of how quickly a carbohydrate raises blood sugar. High-glycemic carbs (rice, bread, candy, potato, soda, most processed carbs) spike it fastest; processing raises the index.
Hyperinsulinism
Chronically and acutely elevated insulin, driven by excess high-glycemic carbohydrate, linked to obesity, elevated cholesterol and blood pressure, mood dysfunction, and chronic disease.
Paleolithic / caveman model
Eating in line with what our genetics evolved for — meats, vegetables, nuts/seeds, some fruit, minimal starch, no sugar — because modern processed diets outpaced our evolution.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Don't let a client believe long-distance endurance work alone makes them 'fit.' The guide is explicit that endurance specialists trade away strength, speed, and power and aren't in shape for most sports — fitness is broad, not specialized.
  • More time is not more fitness. Coaching past 45–60 minutes of real intensity isn't 'extra credit' — it shifts into skill/sport work and can cost conditioning. 'Past one hour, more is not better.'
  • Never load every client the same. The grandma-and-Olympic-skier point cuts both ways: same movements, but load must be scaled to the individual — incremental, painstaking load assignment is what makes it safe.
  • Stay in your lane on health claims. The continuum framing ('side benefit vs. side effect') is a training philosophy, not a license to diagnose, treat, or tell clients to drop medical care. Fitness complements medical care — it doesn't replace a physician.
  • Nutrition guidance here is general dietary advice (macro split, whole foods, low-glycemic). Don't present it as a medical prescription for clients with diabetes or other conditions — those need a physician/dietitian.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A prospective member who has run marathons for a decade tells you she's 'already extremely fit' and questions why she'd squat or do short workouts. Using the Foundations argument, what's the best response?

  1. Explain that endurance training has built deep CV fitness but left gaps in strength, speed, power, and the other physical skills — broad competence across all 10 is the goal, and that includes squatting
  2. Tell her marathon training is a waste of time and she should stop
  3. Put her straight into a max-effort heavy day to prove a point
  4. Recommend she keep doing exactly what she's doing
  5. Agree she's at peak fitness and just add some mobility work
Show answer

Answer: A — Explain that endurance training has built deep CV fitness but left gaps in strength, speed, power, and the other physical skills — broad competence across all 10 is the goal, and that includes squatting. The guide calls specialists 'fringe athletes' who sacrifice breadth; the move is to respect what she's built while explaining fitness is competence across all 10 skills. E and D ignore the gaps; B is needlessly dismissive (the guide says endurance work isn't bad, just not complete); C ignores incremental load assignment and safety.

Scenario 2. A new client asks, 'I don't care about being an athlete — I just want to be healthy.' Which response best reflects the Foundations view?

  1. Health is purely genetic, so training won't change it
  2. Health and fitness are separate; we'll focus only on health markers and skip the training
  3. Just take supplements and you'll be healthy
  4. Then CrossFit isn't for you — try a walking program
  5. Fitness, wellness, and sickness are points on one continuum — the same measurable health — so getting fitter literally moves your health markers in the right direction
Show answer

Answer: E — Fitness, wellness, and sickness are points on one continuum — the same measurable health — so getting fitter literally moves your health markers in the right direction. The guide frames sickness–wellness–fitness as one continuum and holds fitness and health as the same thing. D and B falsely separate them; C and A contradict the entire premise that training drives health markers from normal toward exceptional.

Scenario 3. You're designing a week of training and a member asks why you don't just lock in one proven 'best' routine and repeat it. What's the most accurate answer from this section?

  1. There is a single ideal routine; we just haven't found it yet
  2. There is no ideal routine — whatever a routine omits is exactly what you won't adapt to, so we vary constantly to keep adaptation broad
  3. Variety only matters for advanced athletes
  4. We change routines just to keep members from getting bored
  5. Repeating one routine is fine as long as it's hard enough
Show answer

Answer: B — There is no ideal routine — whatever a routine omits is exactly what you won't adapt to, so we vary constantly to keep adaptation broad. The guide states 'there is no ideal routine' and that 'the breadth of adaptation will exactly match the breadth of the stimulus.' A and E contradict that; C is false (variance applies to everyone); D understates it — variance is a physiological design principle, not just engagement.

Scenario 4. A member spends 90+ minutes a day grinding extra long cardio sessions and asks how to add even more volume to get fitter faster. Based on Foundations, what do you tell them?

  1. Switch entirely to isolation movements to recover
  2. The intensity that optimizes conditioning isn't sustainable past 45–60 minutes; past an hour you're into skill/sport work, not better conditioning — train hard and short, not long
  3. Double the volume — more is always better
  4. Stop training six days a week and only train twice
  5. Add a second long session in the evening
Show answer

Answer: B — The intensity that optimizes conditioning isn't sustainable past 45–60 minutes; past an hour you're into skill/sport work, not better conditioning — train hard and short, not long. The guide says world-class conditioning comes from about an hour a day, six days a week, and 'past one hour, more is not better.' C and E pile on volume the guide warns against; A contradicts the preference for functional compound movements; D throws out the proven frequency unnecessarily.

Scenario 5. A client wants to clean up their diet and asks for the simplest rule that fits the CrossFit prescription. Which best matches this section?

  1. Eat whatever fits the macros, including plenty of sugar
  2. Eat low-fat processed foods and count only calories
  3. Maximize high-glycemic carbs for energy before workouts
  4. Base the diet on vegetables (especially greens), meats, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch and no sugar — shop the perimeter of the store
  5. Eliminate all fat and all protein
Show answer

Answer: D — Base the diet on vegetables (especially greens), meats, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch and no sugar — shop the perimeter of the store. That's the guide's plain-language rule, consistent with the ~30/40/30 split and the Paleolithic model. B and C run straight into the high-glycemic/hyperinsulinism warning; E ignores that fat (~30%) and protein (~30%) anchor the prescription; A contradicts 'no sugar.'

L1 · Section 3 of 27 Tier: ___

What Is Fitness? (Part 1): The Four Models

Open the official guide

CrossFit's four models for defining and measuring fitness, the 10 general physical skills, the hopper, the metabolic pathways, and the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum, plus how cardio, gymnastics, and weightlifting build it.

Key ideas to understand

  • The whole reason this exists: most authorities won't even define fitness (the NSCA's flagship text doesn't try; the dictionary is no help). CrossFit refuses to champion a program without defining what it delivers, so it built its own definition. This definition is the key to understanding CrossFit's methods and successes.
  • CrossFit's view is contrarian: the public holds endurance athletes (like a triathlete) up as the model of fitness. CrossFit does not. The Mark Allen vs. decathlete Simon Poelman example makes the point, an athlete who also has strength, power, speed, and coordination beats the pure endurance athlete in any broad comparison.
  • There are FOUR models, used together. Model 1 = the 10 general physical skills. Model 2 = the hopper (performing athletic tasks). Model 3 = the three metabolic pathways (energy systems). Model 4 = the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum (health markers). Each has distinct utility for evaluating an athlete or a program's effectiveness.
  • Important caveat: CrossFit isn't claiming scientific proof of legitimacy. It's sharing the methods of a program whose legitimacy was established by the testimony of athletes, soldiers, cops, and others whose lives depend on fitness.
  • MODEL 1, the 10 general physical skills: cardiovascular/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy. You are as fit as you are competent in all 10, and a program develops fitness to the degree it improves all 10.
  • Training vs. practice (a key distinction inside Model 1): endurance, stamina, strength, and flexibility improve through TRAINING (a measurable organic change in the body). Coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy improve through PRACTICE (changes in the nervous system). Power and speed are adaptations of BOTH training and practice.
  • Know the 10 definitions cold: Endurance = body systems gather, process, and deliver oxygen. Stamina = process, deliver, store, and utilize energy. Strength = a muscular unit applies force. Flexibility = maximize range of motion at a joint. Power = apply maximum force in minimum time. Speed = minimize the time cycle of a repeated movement. Coordination = combine distinct movement patterns into one. Agility = minimize transition time between movement patterns. Balance = control the center of gravity over the base of support. Accuracy = control movement in a given direction or at a given intensity.
  • MODEL 2, the hopper: imagine a hopper holding infinite physical challenges, drawn at random with no selective mechanism. Your fitness is your capacity to perform well at these tasks relative to others. The implication: fitness requires performing well at ALL tasks, even unfamiliar ones and infinitely varied combinations. Practically, this means disinvesting from set notions of reps, rest, exercises, order, routines, periodization, etc. Nature throws unforeseeable challenges, so train broad and constantly varied.
  • MODEL 3, the three metabolic pathways (the 'metabolic engines') that power all human action: phosphagen (phosphocreatine), glycolytic (lactate), and oxidative (aerobic). Phosphagen dominates the highest-power efforts under ~10 seconds; glycolytic dominates moderate efforts up to several minutes; oxidative dominates low-power efforts beyond several minutes. Total fitness requires training all three; balancing them is the how and why of CrossFit 'cardio.'
  • The two most common faults in fitness training (from Model 3): favoring one or two pathways to the exclusion of the others, and not recognizing the harm of excessive oxidative (aerobic) training.
  • MODEL 4, the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum: nearly every measurable marker of health sits on a continuum from sickness to wellness to fitness. Example: blood pressure 160/95 is pathological, 120/70 healthy, 105/55 athletic; 40% body fat pathological, 20% healthy, 10% fit. Same ordering for bone density, triglycerides, muscle mass, flexibility, HDL, resting heart rate, and more. Even mental health fits, depression is mitigated by proper diet and exercise.
  • The continuum's punchline: fitness is 'super-wellness.' Sickness, wellness, and fitness are measures of the same entity. Done right, fitness gives a great margin of protection against time and disease. A fitness regimen that does NOT support health is not CrossFit. Studies claiming the fit aren't health-protected almost always studied endurance athletes, often on a dangerous high-carb/low-fat/low-protein fad diet.
  • Common ground across the four models: all four exist simply to ensure the broadest, most general fitness possible, full range of adaptations (Model 1), breadth and depth of performance (Model 2), time/power/energy systems (Model 3), and health markers (Model 4). The fitness is deliberately broad, general, and inclusive. CrossFit's specialty is not specializing; combat, survival, sport, and life reward this and punish the specialist.
  • IMPLEMENTATION, you forge this fitness by molding athletes who are equal parts gymnast, Olympic weightlifter, and multi-modal sprinter. Develop the capacity of a novice 800m runner, gymnast, and weightlifter and you'll be fitter than any world-class specialist in any one of them. The tools are metabolic conditioning ('cardio'), gymnastics, and weightlifting.
  • Metabolic conditioning / 'cardio': of the three pathways, phosphagen and glycolytic are anaerobic; oxidative is aerobic. Aerobic training helps cardiovascular function and lowers body fat, BUT too much aerobic work decreases muscle mass, strength, speed, and power, and reduces anaerobic capacity. Anaerobic training ALSO helps cardiovascular function and is actually superior for fat loss, and it uniquely improves power, speed, strength, and muscle mass without harming aerobic capacity.
  • Interval training is the key to building the cardiovascular system without losing strength, speed, and power. It mixes timed bouts of work and rest; varying work duration, rest duration, and number of intervals controls which pathway you condition. The Tabata interval (20 sec work / 10 sec rest x 8) is a regular example shown to boost both anaerobic and aerobic capacity. Interval guidelines: phosphagen ~10-30s work / 30-90s rest (1:3) x 25-30; glycolytic ~30-120s work / 60-240s rest (1:2) x 10-20; oxidative ~120-300s work / 120-300s rest (1:1) x 3-5.
  • Dr. Seiler's work backs the interval reliance: there are three waves of adaptation to endurance training, increased max oxygen consumption (wave 1), increased lactate threshold (wave 2), increased efficiency (wave 3). CrossFit maximizes wave 1, gets wave 2 systemically through many modalities, and deliberately avoids wave 3 efficiency, since waves 2 and 3 are highly specific and can hurt broad fitness.
  • Gymnastics (broadly defined, climbing, yoga, calisthenics, dance, anything where the aim is body control) develops strength (especially upper body and trunk), flexibility, coordination, balance, agility, and accuracy. Start with the calisthenics core: pull-ups, push-ups, dips, rope climbs (with benchmark goals). At 15 pull-ups and dips, start the muscle-up. Develop the handstand, then pirouettes, handstand walking, and handstand presses; build trunk strength with sit-ups and the L-hold.
  • Weightlifting (the Olympic sport, clean and jerk and snatch) develops strength (especially hips), speed, power, flexibility, coordination, agility, accuracy, and balance like no other modality. The Olympic lifts are based on the deadlift, clean, squat, and jerk, which should be the core of resistance training because they elicit a profound neuroendocrine response (they change you hormonally and neurologically). Isolation/bodybuilding moves (curls, leg extensions, flyes) have a blunted neuroendocrine response, no functional analog, and work one joint at a time, so they have no place in serious strength and conditioning.
  • Throwing (medicine-ball work, especially the Dynamax ball) adds a potent stimulus for strength, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy, plus general movement practice.
  • Nutrition can amplify or diminish your training. Effective nutrition is moderate in protein, carbohydrate, and fat (roughly 40% carb / 30% protein / 30% fat), NOT the high-carb/low-fat/low-protein fad. The Zone Diet offers the greatest precision and health benefit. Forget the fad diet.
  • Sport is the application of fitness in an atmosphere of competition and mastery. Sport is better at EXPRESSING and testing the 10 skills than at developing them; training develops them faster but with more predictable movements. Both expression and development matter, so athletes should play sports on top of their strength and conditioning.
  • The theoretical hierarchy of athlete development (a pyramid): nutrition at the base, then metabolic conditioning, gymnastics, weightlifting, and sport at the top. It reflects foundational dependence and skill ordering, from molecular foundations up to mastery and application. A deficiency at any level makes everything above it suffer. Its best use is diagnosing an athlete's shortcomings.
  • Integration: every routine contains a blueprint for its own deficiency. Work only low reps and you miss localized muscular endurance; only high reps and you miss strength and power. For the broad fitness CrossFit seeks, modulate every parameter you control, routine is the enemy of progress. The body only responds to an unaccustomed stressor; strive for variance.
  • Scalability and applicability: the needs of an Olympic athlete and your grandparents differ by DEGREE, not KIND, one wants functional dominance, the other functional competence, but both manifest through identical physiological mechanisms. CrossFit scales load and intensity; it does not change the program. The same routines serve elderly heart-disease patients and cage fighters. Sport-specific training is mostly ineffective; specificity is met by practicing the sport itself, not in the strength and conditioning environment.

What you should get from it

The opening jab at the endurance-athlete-as-fittest idea; broad capability beats narrow endurance, which sets up CrossFit's contrarian definition.

What you should get from it

Four models together form the definition: the 10 skills, the hopper, the metabolic pathways, and the health continuum.

What you should get from it

Model 1: fitness is competence across all 10 skills, and a program is judged by whether it improves all 10.

What you should get from it

Endurance/stamina/strength/flexibility come from training; coordination/agility/balance/accuracy from practice; power and speed from both.

What you should get from it

These exact definitions are testable, e.g., power = max force in minimum time, agility = minimize transition time between movement patterns.

What you should get from it

Model 2: random, infinite challenges mean you must train broad and constantly varied, and disinvest from fixed routines and periodization.

What you should get from it

Model 3: phosphagen under ~10s, glycolytic up to ~minutes, oxidative beyond, and total fitness needs all three trained.

What you should get from it

Over-relying on a pathway, especially too much aerobic work, is the classic programming mistake.

What you should get from it

Model 4: health markers fall on one continuum, and fitness is 'super-wellness,' the far healthy end of the same scale.

What you should get from it

Fitness must support health; if it doesn't, examine the protocol (especially diet), it isn't real CrossFit fitness.

What you should get from it

All four models exist to maximize broad, general, inclusive fitness; combat, survival, sport, and life punish the specialist.

What you should get from it

Build novice-level capacity across all three and you out-fitness any world-class single-modality specialist.

What you should get from it

Too much aerobic work costs strength, speed, power, and muscle; anaerobic work builds them AND can develop aerobic fitness via intervals.

What you should get from it

Intervals are how you build cardio without losing strength/speed/power; work:rest ratios target each pathway (1:3, 1:2, 1:1).

What you should get from it

CrossFit maximizes wave 1, gets wave 2 across modalities, and deliberately avoids the activity-specific efficiency of wave 3.

What you should get from it

Functional, multi-joint lifts change you hormonally and neurologically; single-joint bodybuilding moves have a blunted response and no functional analog.

What you should get from it

Balanced macronutrients beat the fad diet; the Zone offers the most precise, health-promoting protocol.

What you should get from it

A pyramid of foundational dependence, a deficiency at any level undermines everything above it, useful for diagnosing shortcomings.

What you should get from it

Universal scalability: dominance vs. competence run through identical physiology, so you scale the load, not swap the program.

Words to know (plain English)

The four models of fitness
CrossFit's combined definition of fitness: the 10 general physical skills, the hopper, the three metabolic pathways, and the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum.
10 general physical skills
Cardiovascular/respiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy; you're as fit as you're competent in all 10.
Training vs. practice
Training improves performance through a measurable organic change in the body (endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility); practice improves it through nervous-system changes (coordination, agility, balance, accuracy). Power and speed need both.
The hopper
A thought model: an infinite hopper of random physical challenges. Fitness is your ability to perform well at any task drawn from it, even unfamiliar ones.
Phosphagen pathway
The anaerobic 'phosphocreatine' energy engine that dominates the highest-power efforts lasting under about 10 seconds.
Glycolytic pathway
The anaerobic 'lactate' energy engine that dominates moderate-power efforts lasting up to a few minutes.
Oxidative pathway
The aerobic energy engine that dominates low-power efforts lasting beyond a few minutes; over-training it is a common fault.
Sickness-wellness-fitness continuum
The idea that health markers (blood pressure, body fat, HDL, etc.) fall on one continuum, and fitness is 'super-wellness,' the far healthy end.
Interval training
Mixing timed bouts of work and rest; adjusting work/rest duration and repetitions targets a specific metabolic pathway and builds cardio without losing strength, speed, or power.
Tabata interval
20 seconds of work followed by 10 seconds of rest, repeated eight times; shown to boost both anaerobic and aerobic capacity.
Neuroendocrine response
Hormonal and neurological change from exercise. Functional, multi-joint lifts (deadlift, clean, squat, jerk) produce a profound one; isolation moves produce a blunted one.
Theoretical hierarchy of development
The athlete-development pyramid: nutrition, then metabolic conditioning, gymnastics, weightlifting, and sport, where a deficiency at any level weakens everything above it.
Differ by degree, not kind
An Olympic athlete (functional dominance) and a grandparent (functional competence) need the same kind of training through identical physiology; you scale load and intensity, not the program.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Specializing, building a single capacity (like endurance) while neglecting the others, is exactly what these four models are designed to prevent; nature and life punish the specialist.
  • Favoring one or two metabolic pathways to the exclusion of the others, or doing excessive oxidative (aerobic) training, are named as the two most common faults in fitness training and cost you strength, speed, power, and muscle.
  • Running a 'fitness' program that doesn't support health markers: a regimen that doesn't support health is not CrossFit; check the protocol, especially diet.
  • Eating the high-carbohydrate, low-fat, low-protein fad diet; it's explicitly rejected in favor of balanced macros / the Zone.
  • Filling a strength program with isolation/bodybuilding moves (curls, leg extensions, flyes), they have a blunted neuroendocrine response, no functional analog, and work one joint at a time.
  • Falling into routine: every fixed routine contains a blueprint for its own deficiency; the body only adapts to an unaccustomed stressor, so chase variance.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new member who just finished a marathon says they're already 'fully fit' and don't need strength or skill work. Using the four models, what's the best coaching response?

  1. Tell them body weight is all that matters
  2. Put them straight into a 1-rep-max test to prove a point
  3. Explain that fitness is competence across all 10 general physical skills and broad performance across tasks and energy systems; pure endurance neglects strength, power, speed, and skill
  4. Tell them to just keep running
  5. Agree, endurance is the truest measure of fitness
Show answer

Answer: C — Explain that fitness is competence across all 10 general physical skills and broad performance across tasks and energy systems; pure endurance neglects strength, power, speed, and skill. The four models define fitness as broad and inclusive, competence in all 10 skills, the hopper's any-task performance, all three energy systems, and health markers. E and D reinforce specialization the models reject; B is unsafe and misses the teaching point; A ignores the models entirely.

Scenario 2. You want to build an athlete's cardiovascular capacity without eroding their strength, speed, and power. What does this section prescribe?

  1. Maximize wave-3 efficiency adaptations in one modality
  2. Eliminate all conditioning and only lift
  3. Interval training, mixing timed work and rest bouts, which delivers the cardiovascular benefit of endurance work without the loss of strength, speed, and power
  4. Train the oxidative pathway exclusively
  5. Long, slow distance running most days
Show answer

Answer: C — Interval training, mixing timed work and rest bouts, which delivers the cardiovascular benefit of endurance work without the loss of strength, speed, and power. Interval training is named as the key to developing the cardiovascular system without an unacceptable loss of strength, speed, and power. E and D over-train the oxidative pathway (a named fault); B abandons conditioning; A pursues the activity-specific efficiency CrossFit deliberately avoids.

Scenario 3. A 72-year-old with manageable heart disease (cleared to train) and a 25-year-old competitor both join your gym. How does this section say to program for them?

  1. Give the older athlete only walking and stretching
  2. Give them completely different programs since their needs differ in kind
  3. Give the younger athlete the real program and the older one a separate 'senior' system
  4. Avoid all weightlifting for the older athlete
  5. Use the same program and scale load and intensity, because their needs differ by degree, not kind, and run through identical physiological mechanisms
Show answer

Answer: E — Use the same program and scale load and intensity, because their needs differ by degree, not kind, and run through identical physiological mechanisms. The section states the needs of an Olympic athlete and our grandparents differ by degree not kind, and that CrossFit scales load and intensity without changing programs. B, A, C, and D all change the program rather than scale it, contradicting universal scalability.

Scenario 4. Why does this section center resistance training on the deadlift, clean, squat, and jerk rather than curls, leg extensions, and flyes?

  1. Because bodybuilding moves take too long
  2. Because the deadlift, clean, squat, and jerk are functional, multi-joint movements that elicit a profound neuroendocrine (hormonal and neurological) response, while isolation moves have a blunted response and no functional analog
  3. Because the big lifts are easier to learn
  4. Because the big lifts only build strength and nothing else
  5. Because isolation moves are dangerous to perform
Show answer

Answer: B — Because the deadlift, clean, squat, and jerk are functional, multi-joint movements that elicit a profound neuroendocrine (hormonal and neurological) response, while isolation moves have a blunted response and no functional analog. The section explains the core lifts are chosen for their profound neuroendocrine response and functional, multi-joint nature, whereas isolation moves have a blunted response, work one joint at a time, and lack a functional analog. C and A aren't the stated reasons; E is not the argument made; D is false since the lifts develop many skills.

L1 · Section 4 of 27 Tier: ___

What Is Fitness? (Part 2): Defining Fitness and Health

Open the official guide

Coach Glassman's lecture deriving the measurable definitions of fitness (work capacity across broad time and modal domains) and health (that capacity held across your whole life).

Key ideas to understand

  • This starts with 'a belief in fitness' — that there's a physical capacity that serves you generally well in any contingency: the likely and unlikely, the known and unknown. That's different from sport fitness, where we already know the event's physiological demands.
  • When CrossFit went to the literature for a definition of fitness, what existed was esoteric, irrelevant, or logically/scientifically flawed. The key test: even bodies like the ACSM give definitions that contain nothing measurable — and if it isn't measurable, it isn't a valid definition.
  • So CrossFit built three clumsy-but-useful operational models that kept it pointed at this broad fitness, and later added a fourth.
  • MODEL 1 — the 10 general physical skills (from Cawley and Evans of Dynamax): cardiorespiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, accuracy, agility, balance. You are as fit as you are developed in breadth and depth across all 10; a deficiency in any one relative to a cohort makes you less fit. These distinctions are manmade abstractions — nature isn't obligated to recognize them — but they help us understand fitness.
  • MODEL 2 — the hopper: a statistical model based on training modality. Load a lottery-style hopper with every skill and drill from every sport and regimen imaginable (track agility, a football bench-press max, Fran/Helen/Diane, Pilates, yoga). Pull tasks at random and test everyone. Whoever performs best at randomly assigned tasks is the fittest.
  • A key hopper insight: the fittest person might be only 75th percentile at each event. Being BEST at many things actually tells you you're NOT as fit as you could be. A 4-minute mile, for example, comes packaged with a roughly half-bodyweight bench and a 3-4 inch vertical — that's a narrow, specialized bandwidth, not advancing fitness. No value judgment, just physiology.
  • The biggest payoff is in the event you do NOT want to see come out of the hopper — that's your chink in the armor, a glaring deficiency in your general physical preparedness (GPP). Fixing it gives advantage in places that don't always make obvious mechanical or metabolic sense.
  • There's more opportunity to advance athletic performance by raising GPP than by adding sport-specific work. Example: more pull-ups make for better skiers, and we don't need to know the exact mechanism — we're focused on advancing performance.
  • MODEL 3 — the three metabolic pathways (the three engines that make ATP, the currency of all effort), plotted as power on the Y-axis and duration on the X-axis: (1) phosphagen/phosphocreatine — high power, short duration, ~100% of max output, taps out around 10 seconds; (2) glycolytic/lactate — moderate power, moderate duration, ~70% of max, peaks around 60 seconds, ends around 120; (3) oxidative/aerobic — low power, long duration, ~40% of max, doesn't fade in any reasonable time. Phosphagen and glycolytic are anaerobic; oxidative is aerobic.
  • All three engines run all the time to some degree; how active each is depends on the activity. You're as fit as you're balanced across all three — a human is a vehicle with three engines, and we build all of them with constantly varied functional movement at high intensity (a balance in bioenergetics).
  • THE 2002-2008 DEFINITION came from collecting WOD data and asking what it really means when your Fran time drops from 7 to 6 to 5 to 4 minutes.
  • The work to do a benchmark like Fran is CONSTANT (force times distance) — it only changes if your height, the range of motion, the load, or your bodyweight change. So every time you repeat the workout, the work is the same.
  • Power = force x distance (work) / time. Do Fran at Time 1, then a year later at Time 2: the constant work cancels, and the difference in time IS the difference in power. That ratio (T2/T1) describes your progress to the precision of the stopwatch — your most accurate tool, more so than the tape measure or scale.
  • Because the same measurement error happens on every effort and cancels out in comparison (zero-order error), tracking the change in time between attempts is really tracking change in power. Your collection of workout data points is your work capacity across broad time and modal domains — THAT is your fitness.
  • Graph it: plot average power output for efforts of ~10 sec, 30 sec, 2 min, 10 min, 60 min, etc. With scientific accuracy and precision, the result is a curve — your work capacity across broad time and modal domains (Figure 1). Fitness is the area under that curve.
  • MODEL 4 and HEALTH (2008): along the way they saw a continuum from sickness to wellness to fitness. Any quantifiable metric a physician or physiologist cares about sits well-ordered on this pattern — body fat (40% morbidly obese, ~15% normal, ~5% elite), bone density (osteoporosis → normal → 3-5x normal in gymnasts), resting heart rate, flexibility, even mood. No known metric runs counter to it.
  • That means fitness and health are different measures of the same reality — and if you're fit, you'd first have to become merely well before you could become pathologically sick. Fitness is a hedge against sickness, with wellness as the intermediate value. If any lifestyle, training, or recreational metric is moving the wrong way, entertain that you're doing something profoundly wrong.
  • When you do CrossFit, eat meat and vegetables/nuts and seeds/some fruit/little starch/no sugar, and sleep plenty, you don't get the divergent side effect where everything improves except one value — so this observation itself becomes a test of your regimen.
  • Add a third axis — age (Z-axis) — and extend fitness across your lifetime, and the area-under-the-curve becomes a three-dimensional solid (Figure 2). That solid IS health. Health is maximized by maximizing your area under the curve and holding that work capacity for as long as you can.
  • These definitions differ from exercise science in two ways: first, they're MEASURABLE (exercise science rarely meets the rigor of chemistry, physics, or engineering); second, it's almost never really about exercise — VO2 max and lactate threshold are correlates or components but are subordinate to work capacity. Nobody trades work capacity for a higher VO2 max.
  • No one has ever built a great athlete by advancing isolated metrics one at a time. You move them best by doing constantly varied, high-intensity functional movement — turning fitness into sport with fixed workloads and minimized times in a competitive cohort — and then those metrics do spectacular things.
  • Longevity alone isn't the goal: a 90-year-old running stairs and playing with grandkids matters more than 'good cholesterol,' and a long life with very low work capacity is not what CrossFit is about. It's about vitality and capacity — what can you DO?
  • Bottom line for making meaningful training assertions: fitness and health must be measurable. Area (or volume) under the curve gives a scientifically accurate, precise, valid measure — and that's assessed the same way you'd assess a rocket, motorcycle, or truck: how much it weighs, how far it moves, how long it takes. Everything else is irrelevant.

What you should get from it

CrossFit chases a broad, general, inclusive adaptive capacity for the unknown — not the known demands of a specific sport.

What you should get from it

Measurability is the gate — a definition of fitness that can't be measured doesn't count.

What you should get from it

Fitness as balanced development across 10 named skills — a useful manmade map, not a law of nature.

What you should get from it

The hopper defines fitness as performance across random, unpredictable tasks — the more variety, the better the test.

What you should get from it

Over-specialization is a cost: elite at one thing usually means a narrow capacity, not maximal fitness.

What you should get from it

Your worst event is your highest-leverage training target — attack the weakness, not the strength.

What you should get from it

Three energy engines with distinct power/time signatures — fitness means balance across all three, all of which run all the time.

What you should get from it

Repeat a benchmark and the WORK is fixed — so any change in your time is a change in your power.

What you should get from it

Time on a repeated benchmark is a clean proxy for power output, because the measurement errors cancel (zero-order error).

What you should get from it

Fitness = work capacity across broad time and modal domains, graphed as the area under your power-vs-duration curve.

What you should get from it

Health and fitness are the same reality measured differently — virtually every meaningful metric lines up sickness → wellness → fitness.

What you should get from it

Being fit buys you a buffer against disease; a metric drifting the wrong direction is a red flag about your regimen.

What you should get from it

Health is fitness sustained across your entire lifespan — the 3-D volume under the curve, maximized and held as long as possible.

What you should get from it

CrossFit's definitions are rigorous because they're measurable, and they prize work capacity over lab correlates like VO2 max.

What you should get from it

The goal isn't just a long life; it's a long life with high work capacity — vitality and capacity, not just years.

What you should get from it

Fitness/health is an engineering measurement: mass, distance, time. That trio is all you need to measure capacity.

What you should get from it

CrossFit invites critique, but only on scientific terms: give performance data, or the argument doesn't count.

Words to know (plain English)

Fitness (CrossFit's definition)
Work capacity across broad time and modal domains — measured as the area under a curve of power output plotted against duration of effort. The more capacity across more time frames and more kinds of movement, the fitter you are.
Health (CrossFit's definition)
That same work capacity held across your entire lifespan — fitness with age added as a third dimension, forming a 3-D solid. Maximized by maximizing the area under the curve and holding it as long as possible.
10 general physical skills
Model 1: cardiorespiratory endurance, stamina, strength, flexibility, power, speed, coordination, accuracy, agility, and balance. You're as fit as you're developed across all ten, in breadth and depth.
The hopper
Model 2, a statistical model: a lottery-style drum loaded with every skill and drill from every sport and regimen. Pull tasks at random; the fittest person performs best across randomly assigned tasks.
General physical preparedness (GPP)
Your broad, all-around physical readiness. CrossFit argues raising GPP advances performance more than sport-specific training — and your worst hopper event marks the biggest GPP gap to attack.
Metabolic pathways (the three engines)
Model 3, the three systems that make ATP: phosphagen (high power, ~10 sec), glycolytic/lactate (moderate power, ~60-120 sec), and oxidative/aerobic (low power, long duration). Phosphagen and glycolytic are anaerobic; oxidative is aerobic. Fitness is balance across all three.
ATP (adenosine triphosphate)
The body's energy currency — the molecule the three metabolic pathways produce to fuel all effort.
Work and power
Work = force x distance. Power = work / time. Because a benchmark's work is constant when you repeat it, a faster time directly means more power.
Zero-order error
When the same measurement error occurs on every effort, it cancels out when you compare one effort to another — so the ratio of times is accurate to the precision of the stopwatch.
Sickness-wellness-fitness continuum
The observation that nearly every health metric (body fat, bone density, resting heart rate, etc.) lines up in order from sickness through wellness to fitness — evidence that health and fitness are the same reality measured differently.
VO2 max / lactate threshold
Common exercise-science metrics. CrossFit treats them as correlates or components of fitness but subordinate to work capacity — nobody would trade work capacity for a better VO2 max.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Don't accept any definition of fitness or claim about a program that isn't measurable — if it can't be expressed in distance, time, load, velocity, work, or power, it doesn't count.
  • Don't mistake being elite at one thing for being fit. Specialization buys a narrow bandwidth; the data may show thousands of people fitter than the specialist overall.
  • Don't pour training into your strengths. The event you least want out of the hopper is the chink in your armor and the highest-leverage thing to fix.
  • Don't chase isolated lab numbers (VO2 max, lactate threshold, a single cholesterol value) one at a time — no one has ever built a great athlete that way, and work capacity is what actually moves them.
  • Don't optimize for longevity alone. A long life with low work capacity misses the point; CrossFit is about vitality and capacity — what you can actually do.
  • If any lifestyle, training, or recreational metric is trending the wrong way, treat it as a signal you may be doing something profoundly wrong rather than ignoring it.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A competitive marathoner asks why your gym has him doing pull-ups, cleans, and short sprints instead of more running. Using the 'What Is Fitness?' logic, what's the best explanation?

  1. Running more is actually a waste of time for everyone
  2. His marathon ability is a narrow specialized bandwidth; advancing his general physical preparedness across the 10 skills and three engines will broaden his work capacity, and attacking his weakest domains yields the biggest gains
  3. Pull-ups and cleans directly make you run faster, which is why we drop the running
  4. The 10 general physical skills don't apply to endurance athletes
  5. We only care about his VO2 max, and lifting raises it fastest
Show answer

Answer: B — His marathon ability is a narrow specialized bandwidth; advancing his general physical preparedness across the 10 skills and three engines will broaden his work capacity, and attacking his weakest domains yields the biggest gains. The section frames sport specialization as a narrow bandwidth, says there's more opportunity in raising GPP than in sport-specific work, and identifies your worst hopper events as the highest-leverage targets. A overstates the point (running isn't worthless, it's just narrow when it's all you do); C claims a direct mechanism the text deliberately avoids ('we do not actually need to know the mechanism'); D contradicts the universality of the 10 skills; E inverts the lesson — VO2 max is subordinate to work capacity.

Scenario 2. An athlete did Fran in 5:00 last year and 4:00 today, same Rx weight and same height. He asks what actually improved. Best answer?

  1. Nothing measurable — time is too noisy to compare
  2. His work increased by about 20%
  3. Only his VO2 max improved; power can't be inferred from a workout
  4. We can't say anything unless we re-measure his bench press and vertical
  5. The work was constant, so the drop in time is a direct increase in power output — roughly a 20% improvement, accurate to the precision of the stopwatch
Show answer

Answer: E — The work was constant, so the drop in time is a direct increase in power output — roughly a 20% improvement, accurate to the precision of the stopwatch. Because a benchmark's work (force x distance) is constant when load, height, range of motion, and bodyweight are unchanged, the work cancels between attempts and the difference in time IS the difference in power — and the comparison is accurate to the stopwatch via zero-order error. A and C contradict the central derivation; B is wrong because the WORK didn't change, the power did; D confuses Model 1 add-ons with the work/power calculation this section actually uses.

Scenario 3. A 78-year-old client's doctor flagged 'borderline high cholesterol.' She trains hard, eats meat and vegetables/nuts and seeds/some fruit/little starch/no sugar, sleeps well, and is getting measurably stronger and faster across the board. How should you think about it?

  1. Switch her to isolated metric-chasing — fix cholesterol first, fitness later
  2. Stop training immediately until the cholesterol is medicated to normal
  3. Health is the work capacity she holds across her life; with nearly every metric improving and her capacity rising, the priority is maximizing and holding area under the curve — and a single number out of context isn't the alarm a divergent, wrong-way trend would be
  4. Cut all fat from her diet to fix the number, even if performance drops
  5. Her age means work capacity no longer matters; only longevity does
Show answer

Answer: C — Health is the work capacity she holds across her life; with nearly every metric improving and her capacity rising, the priority is maximizing and holding area under the curve — and a single number out of context isn't the alarm a divergent, wrong-way trend would be. The section defines health as fitness held across the lifespan (maximize and hold the area under the curve), notes the regimen described avoids the divergent side effect where one metric drifts wrong, and uses the independent-90-year-old example to argue vitality and capacity over a lone longevity/lab number. Note this is methodology framing, not medical advice — the coach doesn't override her physician. B and D sacrifice capacity to chase one number; E inverts the 'vitality and capacity' point; A is exactly the one-metric-at-a-time approach the text says never built a great athlete.

Scenario 4. Someone argues their training program is superior to CrossFit. On what terms does this section say that claim can be evaluated?

  1. On popularity and number of participants
  2. On measurable, observable, repeatable data — distance, time, load, velocity, work, and power related to movements, skills, and drills
  3. On which program has more lab markers like VO2 max
  4. On testimonials and how athletes say they feel
  5. On the credentials of the program's founder
Show answer

Answer: B — On measurable, observable, repeatable data — distance, time, load, velocity, work, and power related to movements, skills, and drills. The Coach Glassman sidebar states that valid criticisms must speak to measurable, observable, repeatable data, and an alternative worthy of consideration should be presented in distance, time, load, velocity, work, and power — 'give me performance data.' D, E, and A aren't measurable; C leans on correlates the section explicitly subordinates to work capacity.

L1 · Section 5 of 27 Tier: ___

Technique: Mechanics, Consistency, Intensity

Open the official guide

This section explains why technique is what maximizes work for the energy you spend, and how threshold training builds real intensity on a foundation of sound mechanics.

Key ideas to understand

  • Behind the whole program is the quantification of fitness: putting a number on fitness as work capacity across broad time and modal domains. You can assess someone's fitness by the area under their work-capacity curve, imagine ranking athletes across 25-30 varied workouts (from short rower efforts to a 10-mile run) and compiling their overall placings for a real metric of total capacity.
  • This sits inside a bigger idea: evidence-based fitness, using measurable, observable, repeatable data to analyze and assess a program. There are three meaningful components to that analysis: safety, efficacy, and efficiency.
  • Efficacy means 'What is the return?', the tangible result, the adaptation the program actually induces. For CrossFit, the efficacy is increased work capacity across broad time and modal domains. If a program claims it makes you a better soccer player, there must be measurable evidence.
  • Efficiency is the time rate of that adaptation, how fast the result comes. Delivering 50 pull-ups in six months versus nine years is a huge difference in efficiency.
  • Safety is how many people end up at the finish line. A program that turns 2 of 10 people into the fittest on Earth while the other 8 die is not something you can judge without knowing the safety numbers, and the real tragedy is not knowing them.
  • Safety, efficacy, and efficiency point in the same direction and aren't fully at odds. You can crank safety up by turning efficacy and efficiency to zero; you can boost efficiency by turning intensity up and risking safety; you can wreck efficacy by losing people. Together, these three vectors give you everything you need to assess a program.
  • Choosing work capacity as the standard for efficacy forces the qualification of movement, that is, judging HOW you move, not just how much output you produce.
  • Four common terms qualify movement: mechanics, technique, form, and style. The exact distinctions don't matter much, and technique and form get used somewhat interchangeably.
  • Mechanics is the physics of movement: angular velocity, momentum, leverage, muscle origin/insertion, torque, force, power, relative angles, mostly the statics, less the dynamics.
  • Technique is the method for completing a movement, the macro 'how do you complete the movement' without the physics (e.g., for a twisting ring dismount: pull, let go, look, arm up, turn, shoulder drop). It includes head and body posture, and some techniques are more effective than others.
  • Form is the normative value, the 'good/bad,' 'should/shouldn't' judgment applied to mechanics and technique.
  • Style is the signature unique to you, like a thumbprint (a coach can spot a lifter by bar path). To be pure style, it has no bearing on form, technique, or mechanics, and doesn't change the physics.
  • By taking power (work capacity) as the primary value and relying on functional movement, you land in an interesting position: power becomes the successful completion of functional movement.
  • This is NOT about energy merely exerted. Plot work completed against energy expended: someone can burn a lot of energy and do little work by being inefficient. The ideal is little energy spent for maximum work. Technique is what maximizes the work completed for the energy expended. For any given metabolic capacity, the person who knows the technique does the most work.
  • Concrete example: take two people loading a truck with sandbags, one knows how to lift and transport large objects, one doesn't. The skilled one does far more work for the same energy.
  • CrossFit does NOT take contractile potential as the gold standard for strength. Strength is the productive application of force. Huge biceps and quads are useless if you can't run, jump, lift, throw, or press. If strength can't be expressed as power, as a productive result, it doesn't count.
  • Technique ties directly to safety, efficacy, and efficiency. Safety: two people lift a heavy object, one pops the hip and gets under it (clean), one pulls with a rounded back, you can guess who gets hurt. Efficacy: for any given contractile potential or metabolic limit, the one with good technique gets more work done and develops faster. If your clean still looks wrong after six months, you won't hit twice-bodyweight overhead as fast as someone who looks natural.
  • Across domains where speed matters, technique is at the heart of it: typing, shooting, violin, NASCAR, CrossFit. In all of them, marked proficiency shows up as speed. A flawless typist at 20 words a minute on two fingers won't get hired; a 12-minute 'Flight of the Bumblebee' isn't virtuosity; a perfectly exquisite Fran that takes 32 minutes is worthless.
  • The false choice: coaches get asked 'good form OR do it quickly?' Both are impossible without the other, technique and speed only seem at odds. It's a misapprehension, an illusion.
  • You learn to go fast by going fast, making lots of errors, and then pulling the errors back in at that speed, then going faster and pulling them back in again. A race driver who's never spun out isn't very good; a typist who's never made a mistake is very slow; in CrossFit, if your technique is always perfect, your intensity is always low.
  • The hard part to understand: you won't maximize intensity/speed without mistakes, BUT it's not the mistakes that make you faster. Hitting the wrong note or missing the target didn't make you better, the errors are just an unavoidable consequence of development.
  • This iterative process, letting the scope of errors broaden, then reducing them WITHOUT reducing the speed, is called threshold training.
  • How it works in a workout: if you're moving well, the coach tells you to pick up speed; if it still looks good, go faster; eventually the movement starts to fall apart. The coach does NOT want you to slow down yet, first, at that speed, fix the technique. You continuously advance the margin at which form falters (e.g., perfect at 10,000 ft-lb/min but breaking at 12,000; work that 10,000-12,000 zone to clean it up, then push to 14,000, and repeat). The process is ineluctable, unavoidable.
  • CrossFit is 'the technique people', drill technique incessantly while simultaneously pushing for more speed. You only learn to work at high intensity with good technique by ratcheting intensity to the point where good technique is momentarily impossible. At the limits of your capacity, you can't obey every nuance of technique, and some refined motor-recruitment patterns won't always look perfect.
  • There's no domain where speed matters and technique isn't central. If someone set a record with poor technique, it means either better technique would've gone farther, or we were wrong about what good technique is. Technique is everything, you won't express power in significant measure without it, won't see productive force, won't complete tasks efficiently, and won't be safe trying.
  • The order is the key to implementation: learn the Mechanics of fundamental movements, build a Consistent pattern of practicing them, and only then ratchet up Intensity. Mechanics, then Consistency, then Intensity.

What you should get from it

Evidence-based fitness assesses any program by three meaningful vectors: safety, efficacy, and efficiency.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it
