Words to know (plain English)

Work capacity across broad time and modal domains
CrossFit's measurable definition of fitness: the ability to do work across many durations and many kinds of activity.
Evidence-based fitness
Analyzing and assessing a program using measurable, observable, repeatable data rather than opinion.
Efficacy
The return on a program, the actual adaptation or result it produces (for CrossFit, increased work capacity).
Efficiency
The time rate of adaptation, how quickly the program delivers the result.
Safety
How many people make it to the finish line; a program is incomplete without knowing its safety numbers.
Mechanics
The physics of a movement, leverage, torque, force, momentum, relative angles.
Technique
The method for completing a movement, the macro 'how,' including posture, without reducing it to the physics.
Form
The normative judgment ('good/bad,' 'should/shouldn't') applied to mechanics and technique.
Style
The signature unique to an athlete that doesn't affect form, technique, or the physics, like a thumbprint on the movement.
Strength (CrossFit definition)
The productive application of force, force that produces real work/power, not just contractile potential.
Threshold training
Pushing intensity until errors appear, then cleaning up technique at that speed without slowing down, then repeating at a higher speed.
Mechanics, Consistency, Intensity
The required order for safely and effectively implementing CrossFit: learn sound mechanics, make them consistent, then add intensity.
Power
Here, the successful completion of functional movement, the productive expression of strength as work over time.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Adding intensity before mechanics and consistency are in place. The whole section insists the order is Mechanics, then Consistency, then Intensity, reversing it compromises safety and results.
  • Forcing the false choice 'good form OR speed.' They aren't opposites; demanding one at the total expense of the other misunderstands the model.
  • Judging strength by contractile potential (big muscles) instead of productive output. If it can't be expressed as work/power, it doesn't count.
  • Keeping an athlete's technique 'always perfect' by keeping intensity low. If form never falters, intensity is too low and threshold training isn't happening.
  • Letting form fall apart and just telling the athlete to slow down. The section says first fix the technique at that speed and advance the margin where form breaks, rather than only backing off.
  • Chasing errors as if mistakes themselves build speed. Errors are an unavoidable byproduct of development, not the cause of improvement.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. An athlete is moving a barbell with flawless technique on every single rep, every workout, but their times and loads never improve. Based on this section, what is the most likely problem?

  1. They need to add more rest between sets
  2. Their intensity is too low, perfect technique on every rep means they're never pushing the threshold where form falters
  3. Their mechanics are wrong and need to be retaught from scratch
  4. They should switch to machines
  5. They have a mobility restriction
Show answer

Answer: B — Their intensity is too low, perfect technique on every rep means they're never pushing the threshold where form falters. The text says if your technique is always perfect, your intensity is always low, and a race driver who's never spun out isn't very good. Improvement at higher intensity comes from threshold training: pushing to where form starts to break, then cleaning it up at that speed. The athlete is moving well, so the issue isn't faulty mechanics (C) or mobility (E); the missing ingredient is intensity.

Scenario 2. During a workout, an athlete's technique is solid, so you tell them to speed up; at the faster pace the movement starts breaking down. What does this section say to do FIRST?

  1. Immediately tell them to slow back down to protect form
  2. End the workout to avoid injury
  3. Keep them at that speed and coach them to fix the technique there, advancing the margin at which form falters
  4. Add load to challenge them more
  5. Ignore the form breakdown since intensity is all that matters
Show answer

Answer: C — Keep them at that speed and coach them to fix the technique there, advancing the margin at which form falters. The section explicitly says, when form starts falling apart at higher speed, 'I do not want you to slow down yet. First, at that speed I want you to fix your technique,' continuously advancing the margin where form falters. Slowing down immediately (A) or stopping (B) skips the threshold work; adding load (D) or ignoring breakdown entirely (E) isn't the prescribed first step.

Scenario 3. A prospective client says they care about being 'strong' and points to their large arms and big bench press on a machine, but they can't perform a clean, run, or jump well. How does this section frame strength?

  1. Strength is style, unique to each person
  2. Strength is purely about how much weight you can move on any single machine
  3. Strength can't be measured at all

Answer: E — Strength is the productive application of force, if it can't be expressed as productive work like running, jumping, lifting, or pressing, it doesn't count. The text rejects contractile potential as the gold standard and defines strength as the productive application of force, stating that enormous biceps and quads are useless if you cannot run, jump, lift, throw, or press. That makes D and B wrong. Style (A) is the signature, not strength, and the section is built on quantifying and measuring (C is false).

  1. All three at once from day one
  2. Mechanics first, then a consistent pattern of practicing those movements, and only then ratcheting up intensity
  3. Intensity first to build work capacity fast, then clean up mechanics later
  4. Style first so the athlete feels comfortable

Glassman's guidance is explicit: learn the mechanics of fundamental movements, establish a consistent pattern of practicing them, and only then ratchet up intensity, 'Mechanics, then Consistency, and then Intensity.' Leading with intensity (C, A) or reordering the steps (E, D) contradicts the section's central rule.

L1 · Section 6 of 27 Tier: ___

Nutrition: Avoiding Disease and Optimizing Performance

Open the official guide

Why too much insulin — driven by too much carbohydrate — is the root of chronic disease, and how the CrossFit diet prevents it and fuels elite performance.

Key ideas to understand

  • Norman Kaplan's July 1989 Archives of Internal Medicine paper (still unchallenged) showed — by mechanism, by correlation, and causally — that hyperinsulinism (too much insulin) is the root of the 'deadly quartet': upper-body obesity, glucose intolerance, hypertriglyceridemia, and hypertension.
  • Insulin is normal and essential — a hormone from the pancreas that you can't live without; it stores energy in cells (including storing fat). You either produce it, inject it, or die. Glucagon is its counter-regulatory partner — it releases energy out of cells.
  • The way you get insulin too high (hyperinsulinism) is eating too much carbohydrate. 'Too much' is qualitative: if your insulin is driving up blood pressure, making you fat, or hurting your ability to clear blood sugar after eating, you're getting too much insulin — and therefore too much carb. Glucose intolerance, hypertension, and high triglycerides are the tells.
  • These are risk factors for heart disease and the process that builds atherosclerotic disease — arteries paved over with plaque — leading to thrombosis, occlusion, myocardial infarction, debilitation, and death.
  • When physicians are asked what they least want to get, Type 2 diabetes ranks higher than cancer or heart disease.
  • Type 2 diabetes is caused by a receptor downgrade phenomenon on the liver, muscle, and fat cells. Insulin binds to a receptor like a key in a lock; when the cell binds insulin, it can take in good things including amino acids (protein) and fat.
  • Expose yourself to too much insulin and the receptors go 'blind' — the key stops working in the lock (receptor downgrade). It's like staring at the sun: at first you see light, but burn out the receptors and you won't see light again. That's Type 2 diabetes.
  • Kaplan's work overturned the accepted model. The old story said people first got fat, then high cholesterol (hypercholesterolemia), then high blood pressure, then diabetic — and assumed the order proved causation. That's the post hoc, ergo propter hoc fallacy: order of events does NOT prove causality. Kaplan showed hyperinsulinism was the actual cause of all of it, of atherosclerotic disease, and of cardiac death — collectively coronary heart disease (CHD).
  • The big shift: heart disease is driven not by dietary fat but by excessive carbohydrate. The 'French paradox' is no paradox — the French eat many times the fat Americans do, have far less heart disease, and eat just under 5% of the refined sugar we do. Americans eat about 150 lb. of sugar per person per year.
  • The craving for carbohydrate is no different than an interest in beer or opiates — sugar tickles the brain and feels good, and people make wild excuses to get the high.
  • The prescription to avoid all of it: eat a diet of meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, and no sugar. Do that and you're exempt — no coronary heart disease.
  • It's not about genetics in the way people think. The genetic piece is an intolerance to excess carbohydrate — like a predisposition to alcoholism. Having the gene doesn't mean it expresses; you'd have to drink. Don't eat the carbs your relatives ate and you don't get their disease (the Dr. Barry Sears example: his father and uncles all died at 49 from atherosclerotic thrombosis — he won't, because he isn't eating their carbohydrates).
  • Four practical food rules to reach the same endpoint: (1) if you couldn't have harvested it from a garden/farm and eaten it an hour later, it's not food; (2) shop the perimeter of the grocery store, not the aisles; (3) if it has a food label, it's not food (no label on chicken or tomatoes — labels are on chips and cookies); (4) if it's not perishable ('Best if used before 2019'), it's not food.
  • Back in 1995 the same lecture got disbelief ('Fat makes you fat, right?'). It's not true.
  • OPTIMIZING PERFORMANCE is the next layer. The meat-and-vegetables prescription alone won't optimize your OUTPUT — to get a sub-three-minute Fran you have to weigh and measure your meat, vegetables, nuts, seeds, fruit, and starch and eliminate sugar.
  • Top-fuel performance needs top fuel. Glassman has worked with tens of thousands of people and has never seen anything but inferior capacity on a diet where people didn't weigh and measure.
  • You don't have to weigh and measure forever — but at least to start, and it's good to go back to it occasionally. Without it, portions drift: you under-eat the foods you dislike and over-eat the ones you love ('one spear of asparagus... a pound of ice cream'), biasing the wrong way.
  • Take any group, get one person to weigh and measure, and that person pulls away. There's a one-to-one correspondence between elite CrossFit performance and the accuracy/precision of consumption — short of doing more pull-ups, almost nothing gets you more pull-ups like eating this way.
  • At some point you may need to STOP an athlete from leaning out further — it's possible to get too lean to perform. If output plateaus: when the athlete is as lean as desired and before performance drops, DOUBLE the fat. If they don't feel a lot better, try three times the fat. If that just makes them thicker, go back to two times. Let performance dictate the change. (Same logic raises intake for hard gainers.) There's more room to play with someone who has padding; be more careful with someone already ripped.
  • The only inputs that matter for the prescription are lean body mass and activity level. There's no inherent difference for men vs. women or young vs. old — everything else is extraneous.
  • Everyday life (schedules, appetite) creates intake fluctuations without weighing and measuring — that's the coarse path. The fine path required for optimized performance is why you won't get there by luck. An average athlete can become extraordinary this way; commitment and focus overcome genetic limitations, all in this 40-30-30 milieu of macronutrient intake.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

The performance diet operates in a 40-30-30 (carb-protein-fat) ratio — precise eating, not luck, makes the average athlete extraordinary.

Words to know (plain English)

Hyperinsulinism
Chronically too much insulin in the blood — the root cause (per Kaplan) of the deadly quartet, atherosclerosis, and cardiac death; driven by eating too much carbohydrate.
Insulin
A pancreatic hormone that stores energy (including fat) inside cells. Essential to life, but excess drives chronic disease.
Glucagon
Insulin's counter-regulatory hormone — it releases stored energy back out of cells.
Deadly quartet
The four conditions Kaplan tied to hyperinsulinism: upper-body obesity, glucose intolerance, hypertriglyceridemia (high blood triglycerides), and hypertension (high blood pressure).
Receptor downgrade phenomenon
When chronic excess insulin makes the receptors on liver, muscle, and fat cells go 'blind' to it (the key stops working in the lock) — the mechanism of Type 2 diabetes.
Type 2 diabetes
Disease caused by receptor downgrade on liver, muscle, and fat cells from too much insulin; ranked by physicians as the condition they least want to get.
Atherosclerotic disease
Arteries 'paved over' with plaque, leading to thrombosis, occlusion, heart attack, and death; collectively (with cardiac death) called coronary heart disease (CHD).
Post hoc, ergo propter hoc fallacy
The logical error of assuming that because one thing happened before another, the first caused the second; the flaw in the old fat→cholesterol→hypertension→diabetes model.
French paradox
The observation that the French eat far more fat than Americans yet have far less heart disease — not actually a paradox; it reflects that carbohydrate (and ~20x less refined sugar), not fat, drives heart disease.
Lean body mass
Body weight minus fat — one of the only two inputs (with activity level) that determine the nutrition prescription.
Weigh and measure
Precisely portioning food to optimize performance; not required forever, but the practice that separates elite output from coarse, drifting intake.
40-30-30 (Zone ratio)
The macronutrient milieu (≈40% carbohydrate, 30% protein, 30% fat by block balance) within which precise eating produces optimized performance.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Coaching nutrition is education within scope — diagnosing, treating, or managing diabetes, hypertension, or heart disease is a physician's job, not a trainer's. Refer medical conditions out.
  • Blaming dietary fat for heart disease — the section's central correction is that excess carbohydrate (and sugar), not fat, drives the disease.
  • Treating 'genetics' as destiny — the heritable piece is intolerance to excess carb that only expresses if you eat the carbs.
  • Assuming the basic 'meat and vegetables' prescription will optimize performance on its own — optimizing OUTPUT requires weighing and measuring and eliminating sugar.
  • Letting an athlete lean out indefinitely — it's possible to get too lean to perform; adjust fat upward and let performance guide it.
  • Cluttering the prescription with sex, age, or other inputs — only lean body mass and activity level are relevant.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A member says, 'My doctor told me to cut fat to protect my heart, so I switched to fat-free snacks and more whole grains.' Within a coach's scope, what's the best educational response?

  1. 'Just eat fat-free and you'll be fine.'
  2. 'The evidence points to excess carbohydrate, not dietary fat, as the driver — meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar is the prescription. Keep following your doctor for any medical condition.'
  3. 'Great call — dietary fat is the main driver of heart disease.'
  4. 'Stop taking any medication your doctor prescribed.'
  5. 'Genetics decide this, so diet won't matter much.'
Show answer

Answer: B — 'The evidence points to excess carbohydrate, not dietary fat, as the driver — meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar is the prescription. Keep following your doctor for any medical condition.'. The section argues carbohydrate excess (not fat) drives heart disease, and gives the meat-and-vegetables prescription — while staying in scope and deferring medical management to the physician. C and A repeat the disproven fat model; D is dangerously out of scope; E misstates the genetics point (intolerance to excess carb only expresses if you eat the carbs).

Scenario 2. An athlete is eating clean (meat, veg, nuts, some fruit, no sugar) but his Fran time has stalled and he wants to break the plateau. What does the guide recommend trying first?

  1. Increase training volume only and leave diet alone
  2. Add more refined carbohydrate for quick energy
  3. Start weighing and measuring his food precisely and eliminate any remaining sugar
  4. Switch to fasting on workout days
  5. Cut fat to get leaner

Answer: C — Start weighing and measuring his food precisely and eliminate any remaining sugar. The basic prescription prevents disease, but optimizing output requires accuracy and precision — weighing and measuring. There's a one-to-one correspondence between precise consumption and elite performance. B adds sugar/carb the section warns against; E and D aren't the recommended first move; A ignores the diet lever entirely.

  2. Stop weighing and measuring entirely
  3. Cut calories further to push past the plateau
  4. Add a daily sugar serving for energy

When an athlete gets as lean as desired and output plateaus, the protocol is to double the fat, escalate to 3x if needed, and return to 2x if it just adds thickness — guided by performance. C makes too-lean worse; D reintroduces sugar; B abandons the precision that drives optimization; E isn't the prescribed lever.

  2. The prescription is identical for everyone regardless of size or activity

L1 · Section 7 of 27 Tier: ___

Fitness, Luck and Health — CrossFit as Non-Medical Health Care

Open the official guide

Why chronic disease is the world's biggest health problem, how it sits on the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum, and why the CrossFit stimulus is non-medical health care.

Key ideas to understand

  • In 2002 CrossFit observed that almost any health marker sits well-ordered on a continuum from sick to well to fit. Examples: HDL cholesterol — under 35 mg/dL is a problem, 50 is nice, 75 is a lot better; blood pressure — 195/115 is a problem, 120/70 is healthy, 105/50 looks athletic. Triglycerides, bone density, muscle mass, body fat, and HbA1c all plot the same way.
  • These markers are the predictors, the cause, AND the manifestation of chronic disease — they're the same measures across the board.
  • Chronic diseases include obesity, coronary heart disease, Type 2 diabetes, stroke, cancer (breast, colon, lung — and Glassman's theory is eventually all PET-positive cancers, ~95% of all cancers), Alzheimer's, peripheral artery disease, advanced biological aging, and drug addiction, among others.
  • If you have any chronic disease, you very likely have deranged markers — e.g., with Alzheimer's you'd see suppressed HDL, elevated blood pressure, elevated triglycerides, high body fat, low muscle mass, low bone density, high HbA1c. The same pattern holds for diabetes and most cancers.
  • Medicine has NO effective treatment for chronic disease — it's symptomatic only. Drugs lower cholesterol, raise bone density; bariatric surgery, bypass surgery, insulin — none of these are fixes, they mask the problem. (If you have persistent malignant hypertension you should take an antihypertensive if you can't get pressure down otherwise — but 'how would you get it down otherwise?')
  • CrossFit holds a uniquely elegant solution to the greatest problem facing the world — not climate change, not politics: chronic disease. The CrossFit stimulus (constantly varied, high-intensity functional movement + meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar) can give you a pass on chronic disease. It's elegant in the mathematical sense — simple and effective.
  • Seventy percent of U.S. deaths are attributable to chronic disease — of 2.6 million deaths in 2014, 1.8 million were chronic. The pattern holds even in countries ravaged by infectious disease, and adding PET-positive cancers could push it to 80-85%. The CDC estimates the U.S. could have up to 100 million diabetics by 2050 — you'd see heart attacks on every corner. Medicine has no solution; you do.
  • The other 30% die from accidents in four '-ic' variants: kinetic (physical trauma — car crash, bike hit), toxic (environmental toxins like lead), genetic (disorders like cystic fibrosis you're born with), and microbic (virus, bacteria, prions). THIS is where medicine's miracles live and treatment can be symptomatic.
  • Doctors are like lifeguards; CrossFit trainers are like swim coaches. When you're drowning you don't need a swim coach — you need a lifeguard. Trainers teach people to swim; when they don't pay attention and go under, doctors take care of it.
  • Accidents are largely beyond your control — except for one thing: be fit. Fitter people survive kinetic trauma, toxic exposure, and microbic threats better, and some genes only express based on diet and exercise behavior. (Who's most vulnerable to viral pneumonia? The frail and feeble.)
  • But even assuming fitness offers no protection from accidents, what prevents accidents is largely LUCK — and there's no 'good' vs. 'bad' luck, just whether these things happen to you. So get fit and don't waste time worrying about germs, tires through the windshield, toxic air, or your genes — worry won't make you happier, better, safer, or longer-lived.
  • This is the 'kinetic theory of health': a singular focus on kinematics — increasing work capacity, increasing fitness — is how you avoid chronic disease. Get a better Fran, a better deadlift, a better Diane time, and do what supports that: eat meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar; get plenty of sleep; maybe take some fish oil. After that, there's nothing else that matters.
  • The magic formula: Fitness + Luck (bad) = Health. The part you CAN do something about (fitness) plus the part you CAN'T (luck) sums to your outcome — so maximize fitness and you won't be one of the seven in ten who die unnecessarily from lifestyle. Chronic disease is a deficiency syndrome: sedentation with malnutrition.
  • The cost: U.S. medical expenditure is ~$4 trillion/year. A 2008 PricewaterhouseCoopers estimate said roughly half of U.S. medical spending was wasted (unnecessary procedures, administrative inefficiency, treating preventable conditions); with fraud and abuse, well over a trillion wasted. About 86% of overall health-care spending goes to treating the chronically diseased ineffectively; of the remaining 14%, half goes to what medicine can actually fix — so only ~7% of spending isn't wasted.
  • CrossFit trainers provide non-medical health care; doctors treating the accident 30% provide medical health care. The easy way to tell them apart is by methods and tools: cut open, irradiated, prescribed pills, injected with syringes = medicine, a doctor's work. Rings, dumbbells, pull-up bars, our own bodies = CrossFit.
  • The prescription is UNIVERSAL — it doesn't matter where you fall on the continuum, everyone gets the same program. If the prescription is universal, it can't be medicine. Like air, oxygen, or vitamin C (without which you get scurvy), it's something everyone needs. Physicians shouldn't control orange and kale production because those have vitamin C — and we can't let exercise fall under that kind of control either. There's a well-funded movement to pull exercise into the medical arena so it falls under the Affordable Care Act.
  • There are ~13,000 gyms with 2-4 million people safe from chronic disease right now. But the gyms thrive not because of their impact on chronic disease — they thrive because the customers are extremely happy with the transformation, which is part physical, part emotional, part health markers, part relationships. That's the miracle of CrossFit: people get something they didn't even know they wanted or needed.

Words to know (plain English)

Sickness-wellness-fitness continuum
The single ordered scale (sick → well → fit) on which virtually every health marker plots; the further toward 'fit,' the further from chronic disease.
Chronic disease
Lifestyle-driven diseases (obesity, CHD, Type 2 diabetes, stroke, most cancers, Alzheimer's, etc.) that medicine can only treat symptomatically; described as a deficiency syndrome of sedentation plus malnutrition.
Deficiency syndrome (sedentation with malnutrition)
Glassman's framing of chronic disease as a deficiency of activity (sedentation) and good nutrition — which is why the fix is fitness, not medication.
CrossFit stimulus
Constantly varied, high-intensity functional movement combined with the diet (meat and vegetables, nuts and seeds, some fruit, little starch, no sugar) — the dose that gives a pass on chronic disease.
Non-medical health care
What CrossFit trainers provide — improving health via exercise and diet (rings, bars, bodyweight) rather than the doctor's tools (surgery, radiation, pills, syringes).
The four '-ic' accident variants
The non-chronic ~30% of deaths: kinetic (physical trauma), toxic (environmental toxins), genetic (born-with disorders), and microbic (viruses, bacteria, prions) — the domain where medicine's miracles apply.
Kinetic theory of health
The idea that a singular focus on kinematics — increasing work capacity/fitness — is how to avoid chronic disease.
HbA1c (glycated hemoglobin)
A blood marker reflecting average blood sugar over time; one of the markers that plots on the sickness-wellness-fitness continuum.
HDL cholesterol
High-density lipoprotein; a health marker where higher is better (≈35 = problem, 50 = nice, 75 = much better) and which plots on the continuum.
Fitness + Luck (bad) = Health
Glassman's formula: your health outcome is the part you control (fitness) plus the part you can't (luck/accidents) — so maximize fitness and stop worrying about luck.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Don't let 'non-medical health care' blur scope-of-practice — trainers improve fitness and nutrition; they do not diagnose, treat, or manage disease. The accident/chronic-disease '-ic' framing reinforces that doctors handle medical conditions.
  • Don't tell a client to stop a prescribed medication (e.g., an antihypertensive for malignant hypertension) — the section explicitly allows medication when pressure can't be controlled otherwise. Refer, don't override.
  • Don't oversell fitness as protection against accidents — the section's own caveat is that accident prevention is largely luck; fitness improves survival odds but isn't a guarantee.
  • Treating worry about germs, genes, and toxins as productive — the section calls it a waste of time that won't make anyone healthier or longer-lived.

L1 · Section 8 of 27 Tier: ___

Zone Meal Plans and Block Prescriptions: Making the Diet Practical

Open the official guide

This section turns the Zone diet into something a coach can actually hand a client — what a block is, how many blocks they need, ready-made meals, and how to adjust fat as they lean out.

Key ideas to understand

  • CrossFit's position: Dr. Barry Sears' Zone Diet closely models optimal nutrition, and CrossFit's best performers are Zone eaters. When second-tier athletes go strict on the Zone, they tend to jump to top-tier fast — the Zone accelerates and amplifies the effects of training.
  • The catch: most of the Zone's benefit only shows up for people who, at least at first, weigh and measure their food. CrossFit spent a decade trying scale-free portioning and concluded the natural variance in calories and macros without measuring is bigger than the resolution it takes to turn good performance into great.
  • So the recommendation is blunt: weigh and measure your portions for a couple of weeks — not because it's fun, because it's worth it. Guesstimate instead, and you'll only get CrossFit's top-performer results if and when you get lucky.
  • The payoff of those two weeks: you build an uncanny ability to eyeball common portions AND, more importantly, a keen visual sense of your own nutritional needs. That awareness is the real prize.
  • The whole scheme is built on the BLOCK — a unit of measure that simplifies building balanced meals. One block of protein = 7 g protein, one block of carbohydrate = 9 g carbohydrate, one block of fat = 3 g fat.
  • Key fat wrinkle: because most protein sources already carry fat (meat, for example), when you're building a meal you only add 1.5 g of fat per fat block from your fat list. The block chart tells you the amount of each item that hits that 1.5 g.
  • When a meal is equal blocks of protein, carb, and fat, the calories come out to 40% carbohydrate, 30% protein, 30% fat. That 40/30/30 split is the signature of the Zone.
  • Everybody lands on 2-, 3-, 4-, or 5-block meals at breakfast, lunch, and dinner, plus 1- or 2-block snacks twice a day (one between lunch and dinner, one between dinner and bedtime). The section assumes you're doing CrossFit — i.e., active.
  • Being a '4-blocker' means three meals a day, each built from 4 blocks of protein, 4 blocks of carb, and 4 blocks of fat. Whether you're a 'smallish' or 'largish' medium person decides whether your two daily snacks are 1 or 2 blocks.
  • The hard rule: every meal and every snack must contain EQUIVALENT blocks of protein, carbohydrate, and fat. You don't mix a 4-block protein with a 2-block carb.
  • How to build a meal once you know your size: use the block chart and pick (say, for a 4-block meal) four times something from the protein list, four times something from the carb list, and four times something from the fat list. A 1-block snack is one item from each list at face value; a 2-block snack is two of each.
  • Non-fat protein exception: if a protein source is specifically labeled 'non-fat,' double the usual fat blocks for that meal (because that protein isn't bringing its normal fat along). The 'why' is in 'Enter the Zone.'
  • On a Zone diet body fat comes off fast. When CrossFit's men drop below 10% body fat and start approaching 5%, the fat intake gets kicked up — the majority of their best athletes end up at X blocks of protein, X blocks of carb, and 4X or 5X blocks of fat. The lesson: learn to modulate fat to dial in a leanness that optimizes performance.
  • The Zone is food-agnostic. It neither requires nor bans any particular food — it can run paleo, vegan, organic, kosher, fast food, or fine dining, while still delivering high-performance nutrition.
  • Use Table 1 (Block Prescription by Sex and Body Type) as the starting point — it lays out blocks per meal and snack and a daily total for everyone from a small female (10 blocks/day) up to an athletic, well-muscled male (25 blocks/day). Pick the row that fits the client and start there.
  • The block chart (the food lists) is your build tool: proteins, fats (nuts/seeds and 'other'), vegetables, processed carbohydrates, and fruits, each with an 'eyeball' amount and exact gram amounts for 1 block. Two notes that matter: fiber in carb sources is subtracted to determine a block, and a '*' marks foods that are virtually unlimited (over 5 cups for a block) — think leafy greens like arugula and watercress.
  • Table 4 gives ready-made Sample Zone Meals and Snacks at 2-, 3-, 4-, and 5-block sizes for breakfast, lunch, and dinner, plus a long list of 1-block snacks. These are the 'what do I eat for dinner?' answer you hand a client so they're not stuck — same recipes scale cleanly up a block size at a time.
  • There's a more precise way to find a client's number than the chart: the Zone block prescription formula = lean body mass (lb.) x activity level (g/lb. of lean body mass) / 7 (g protein per block).
  • Activity level runs 0 to 1. For someone who works out several days a week without a labor-intensive job — most CrossFit athletes — use 0.7. Dividing 0.7 by 7 simplifies the whole thing to a shortcut: the prescription is just 10% of lean body mass.
  • Bump the activity factor up if the athlete does CrossFit twice a day, trains for another sport on top of CrossFit, or has a strenuous daily job (construction, farming — and potentially coaching if you're on your feet all day). It's VOLUME of activity that raises the factor, not intensity — CrossFit workouts are intense but short, so intensity alone doesn't move it.
  • Worked example: a 185 lb. athlete at 16% body fat doing CrossFit five days a week with an office job. Lean body mass = 185 - (0.16 x 185) = 185 - 29.6 = 155.4 lb. With activity factor 0.7, prescription = 155.4 x 0.10 = 15.54, or about 15 blocks/day. (Calipers are the convenient, reasonably accurate way to get body fat.)
  • What 15 blocks looks like in macros (Table 1 in that article): protein 15 x 7 = 105 g (420 cal), carb 15 x 9 = 135 g (540 cal), fat 15 x 3 = 45 g (405 cal) — total about 1,365 calories. Note that total is an underestimate because of 'hidden calories': foods get filed under one macro even though they carry others (nuts are a fat but also have protein and carb), and those aren't counted.
  • Rounding strategy: when the calculation has a decimal, you can round UP to the next whole block (e.g., 15.54 to 16), especially for someone likely to struggle with compliance — the Zone is calorie-restrictive and hard for new adopters, so rounding up may slow progress slightly but improves long-term adherence. Once they're used to it, round back down (e.g., to 15), especially if they haven't hit the body composition they want.
  • Increasing fat intake — the restriction leans the athlete out while keeping protein and carb adequate for training, but they can get TOO lean. 'Too lean' is defined by performance: when losing mass coincides with dropping performance. It is NOT based on body weight or appearance alone.
  • The fix for too-lean is to add calories by doubling fat (Table 2): keep protein at 105 g and carb at 135 g, take fat to 30 blocks x 3 g = 90 g (810 cal), total about 1,770 cal. The macro ratio shifts from 30/40/30 to roughly 23% protein, 31% carb, 46% fat.
  • You can keep multiplying fat if mass loss and performance decline continue. Some CrossFit athletes run five times the fat (Table 3): fat 75 blocks x 3 g = 225 g (2,025 cal), total about 2,985 cal — a ratio of roughly 14% protein, 18% carb, 68% fat. Protein and carb blocks stay fixed; only fat climbs.

Words to know (plain English)

Block
The Zone's unit of measure for building balanced meals: 1 block = 7 g protein, OR 9 g carbohydrate, OR 3 g fat.
Block prescription
The number of blocks a person eats per day, set either off the body-type chart (Table 1) or by the formula, then split across meals and snacks.
4-blocker (block size)
A shorthand for someone whose meals are 4 blocks each — three meals a day of 4 blocks protein, 4 blocks carb, 4 blocks fat; the same idea applies to 2-, 3-, and 5-blockers.
40/30/30
The Zone macro split by calories when blocks are equal: 40% carbohydrate, 30% protein, 30% fat.
Fat block (1.5 g rule)
When building a meal, each fat block from the fat list is added at only 1.5 g of fat (not the full 3 g) because the protein source already supplies the other half.
Non-fat protein adjustment
If a protein is specifically labeled 'non-fat,' you double that meal's fat blocks, since the protein isn't bringing its usual fat.
Lean body mass (LBM)
Body weight minus fat weight. Calculated as total weight - (body fat % x total weight); it's the input for the block prescription formula.
Activity level / activity factor
A 0-to-1 multiplier in the prescription formula reflecting how much you train. 0.7 fits most CrossFit athletes; it rises with the VOLUME of activity, not intensity.
Block prescription formula
Lean body mass (lb.) x activity level / 7 (g protein per block). With activity 0.7 it simplifies to 10% of lean body mass.
Hidden calories
Calories from a food's secondary macronutrients that aren't counted because the food is filed under one macro (e.g., nuts are 'fat' but also carry protein and carb), which makes calorie totals an underestimate.
Too lean
The point where continued weight loss coincides with a drop in performance — defined by performance, not by body weight or appearance — signaling it's time to add fat calories.
Modulating fat intake
Adjusting only the fat blocks (2X, 5X, etc.) while holding protein and carb fixed, to dial in a leanness that optimizes performance and reverse the 'too lean' state.
Calipers
A convenient, reasonably accurate skinfold tool for measuring body fat percentage so you can compute lean body mass.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Telling a client to 'guesstimate' from day one. CrossFit is explicit: skip weighing and measuring and you'll only get top-performer results when you get lucky — at least the first couple weeks should be measured.
  • Forgetting the 1.5 g fat-block rule when building meals. Because protein already carries fat, the fat list is added at 1.5 g per block, not 3 g — getting this wrong over-fats every meal.
  • Mixing unequal blocks in a meal or snack. Every meal and every snack must have equivalent blocks of protein, carb, and fat — that balance is the whole point.
  • Calling an athlete 'too lean' based on the scale or the mirror. The definition is performance-based: mass loss plus a performance drop. Don't add fat just because someone looks lean if performance is fine.
  • Raising the activity factor for intensity. It's driven by VOLUME (two-a-days, a second sport, a strenuous job), not by how hard a single workout feels — CrossFit workouts are intense but short.
  • Treating the calorie totals as exact. They're underestimated by design because of hidden calories from secondary macros — don't chase the number as if it were precise.
  • Forgetting to double fat blocks for a 'non-fat' protein source — that meal will come up short on fat.
  • Scope of practice: this is general performance-nutrition coaching from the guide, not medical or clinical dietetics. A coach prescribes blocks and meals — not treatment for medical conditions.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new client is a typical CrossFit athlete — trains five days a week, desk job, no second sport. You want to set her block prescription quickly. What's the fastest sound method?

  1. Give her the largest row on the chart to be safe
  2. Skip the math and tell her to eat until full
  3. Use activity level 1.0 because CrossFit is high intensity
  4. Use her total body weight, not lean mass, times 0.7
  5. Estimate her lean body mass and take 10% of it (activity factor 0.7 simplified)
Show answer

Answer: E — Estimate her lean body mass and take 10% of it (activity factor 0.7 simplified). For a typical CrossFit athlete the activity factor is 0.7, and 0.7/7 simplifies the formula to 10% of lean body mass — that's the quick, correct shortcut. C overstates activity (1.0 is for high-volume cases). B abandons the prescription entirely. D uses the wrong input — the formula uses lean mass, not total weight. A ignores her actual size and would likely over-feed.

Scenario 2. An athlete has been strict on the Zone for two months. He's still losing weight, but now his lifts and benchmark times are getting WORSE. What does the guide say to do?

  1. He's likely 'too lean' — add calories by multiplying his fat blocks (e.g., 2X), keeping protein and carb fixed
  2. Increase all three macros equally
  3. Cut his blocks further to keep leaning him out
  4. Tell him this is normal and to push through
  5. Add more carbohydrate blocks to fuel the workouts

Answer: A — He's likely 'too lean' — add calories by multiplying his fat blocks (e.g., 2X), keeping protein and carb fixed. 'Too lean' is defined as weight loss together with a performance drop — exactly this. The fix is to add calories by doubling (then if needed multiplying) ONLY fat, while protein and carb blocks stay fixed. C makes it worse. D ignores a clear signal. E and B change the wrong macros — the guide's method is to modulate fat specifically.

Scenario 3. A client building a 3-block dinner picks 3 blocks of chicken from the protein list and is about to add fat. He grabs enough olive oil for 3 blocks at the full 3 g of fat each. What should you correct?

  1. He should add the fat list at 1.5 g per block, since the chicken already supplies fat

Answer: A — He should add the fat list at 1.5 g per block, since the chicken already supplies fat. Most protein sources carry fat, so the fat list is added at 1.5 g per fat block, not the full 3 g — the block chart gives the amount for that 1.5 g. D double-counts fat. E drops fat that the Zone still wants balanced. B and C confuse block size with the per-block fat rule.

Scenario 4. Your block-prescription calculation comes out to 15.54 blocks for a brand-new client who you expect to struggle with adherence. How should you handle the decimal?

  1. The decimal means the formula was done wrong
  2. Drop to 14 to be aggressive early

  4. Always round down to 15 to maximize fat loss immediately

Answer: C — Round up to 16 for now to support compliance, then round down to 15 once she's accustomed. The guide says round up the decimal (15.54 to 16) for clients prone to compliance issues — it may slow progress slightly but improves long-term adherence — then round back down (to 15) once they're used to the diet, especially if body comp goals aren't met. D and B prioritize early aggression over adherence for a new, struggling client. E treats a half-block as meaningful precision the system doesn't need. A is false — decimals are expected.

  1. Only if she eats five times the fat
  2. Only if she abandons the block system

  5. Yes — the Zone neither requires nor prohibits any food and can accommodate a vegan approach while still hitting the blocks

The section states the Zone neither prohibits nor requires any particular food and can accommodate paleo or vegan, organic or kosher, etc., while delivering high-performance nutrition — she just builds her blocks from vegan-friendly sources. D is the opposite of what's stated. B is unnecessary — the block system still applies. A confuses the fat-adjustment lever with the diet's flexibility. C is false.

L1 · Section 9 of 27 Tier: ___

Supplementation

Open the official guide

Gives a coach CrossFit's whole stance on supplements — whole foods first, and the one blanket recommendation (fish oil) to fix the omega-6 to omega-3 ratio.

Key ideas to understand

  • Whole, unprocessed foods are the best source of both macronutrients and micronutrients — best in composition, variety, and density — so supplementation is generally NOT recommended.
  • Eating known quantities of high-quality whole foods is the single most important part of nutrition for performance and health. Supplements are poorer nutrient sources and a waste of focus for anyone not already eating the basic weighed-and-measured plan.
  • There's exactly one supplement CrossFit recommends across the board: fish oil. It provides omega-3 fatty acids, a type of polyunsaturated fat.
  • Background on fats: dietary (physiological) fats are triglycerides — a glycerol backbone with three fatty acids attached. Those fatty acids are a mix of saturated, monounsaturated, and polyunsaturated; one type is prominent in each food but all three are present to some degree.
  • The two polyunsaturated fats found most in food are omega-3 and omega-6 — the difference is chemical structure.
  • Polyunsaturated fats supply the two essential fatty acids — meaning the body can't make them, you must eat them: alpha-linolenic acid (ALA, an omega-3) and linoleic acid (LA, an omega-6).
  • Omega-3s are the 'anti-inflammatory' fats; omega-6s are the 'pro-inflammatory' fats — based on their physiological functions. Both are needed in roughly equal amounts.
  • The problem: modern diets carry far too much omega-6, pushing the body toward pro-inflammatory processes. The current omega-6:omega-3 ratio is about 20:1 or higher, where primitive populations were likely closer to 2:1.
  • Dietary omega-6 comes from vegetable oils, nuts, conventionally raised (grain-fed/feed-lot) meat and eggs, and farm-raised fish.
  • Even on the approved food list you can still end up pro-inflammatory: cutting processed food removes vegetable-oil omega-6, but most meat and eggs are conventionally raised (more omega-6 than wild or grass-fed), and nuts and seeds carry more omega-6 than omega-3.
  • Fish-oil supplementation improves the omega-6 to omega-3 ratio and reduces inflammatory responses in the body.
  • Fish oil supplies two omega-3s — EPA (eicosapentaenoic acid) and DHA (docosahexaenoic acid) — the forms of omega-3 the brain and body prefer. The body can convert ALA into EPA and DHA, but the conversion is inefficient.
  • Some practitioners recommend about 3 grams of combined EPA and DHA per day for an otherwise healthy person, though the exact amount depends on total omega-6 intake.
  • Read the label: every brand has a different EPA/DHA concentration per serving, so you may need multiple servings to hit 3 grams — and some brands pad the omega-3 count with ALA, which isn't EPA or DHA.
  • Flax seed or flax oil is NOT an appropriate omega-3 supplement: flax is a good source of ALA, but the poor ALA-to-EPA/DHA conversion makes it ineffective. Vegans can get DHA from algae oil.
  • Research links fish oil to real benefits: omega-3s increase cell-membrane fluidity, and supplementation can improve insulin sensitivity, cardiovascular function, nervous-system function, immune health, memory, and mood.
  • Safety note: omega-3s act as an anti-coagulant (blood thinner). Military personnel should consider stopping fish oil a couple of weeks before deployment, and anyone with upcoming surgery may want to stop two weeks out — and they should talk to their doctor.
  • You CAN skip omega-3 supplementation if you're fastidious — avoid all vegetable oils (used in nearly every restaurant) and nuts and seeds, eat only grass-fed meat and pasture-raised eggs, and eat wild-caught fish a few times a week. Because that's impractical for most people, supplementation is effective.
  • Ratio isn't the only thing — total polyunsaturated fat matters too. It's not ideal to take in high doses of either omega-6 OR omega-3, because polyunsaturated fats are less stable than other fats.
  • Fish oil does NOT cancel out a bad diet — it won't undo fast food or excessive nuts and nut butters.
  • The ideal total polyunsaturated intake isn't well established; an equal representation of saturated, monounsaturated, and polyunsaturated fats appears prudent. Individuals should work with a primary care doctor on whether supplementation is appropriate, especially with specific medical conditions.

What you should get from it

The default answer on supplements is no — whole food does the job better. This is the baseline before any exception.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Fish oil is the single across-the-board supplement recommendation in CrossFit — everything else is case-by-case at most.

What you should get from it

Dietary fat is stored and eaten as triglycerides; the three attached fatty acids are a mix of saturated, mono-, and polyunsaturated.

What you should get from it

The core idea: you need both fats, balanced — omega-3 calms inflammation, omega-6 promotes it.

What you should get from it

'Essential' fatty acids are the ones the body can't make, so they have to be eaten.

What you should get from it

Modern diets are roughly ten times too heavy on omega-6, tilting the body toward inflammation — that gap is the problem fish oil addresses.

What you should get from it

Knowing where omega-6 hides explains why even a 'clean' eater can still be pro-inflammatory.

What you should get from it

Eating off the list isn't automatically enough — sourcing (grass-fed vs feed-lot) still tilts the ratio.

What you should get from it

Words to know (plain English)

Supplement
A product taken in addition to food (pills, oils, powders) to add specific nutrients. CrossFit's stance: generally not needed if you eat whole foods — fish oil is the one blanket exception.
Triglyceride
The form dietary fat takes — a glycerol backbone with three fatty acids attached. Those fatty acids are a mix of saturated, monounsaturated, and polyunsaturated.
Polyunsaturated fat
A type of fat that includes omega-3 and omega-6. Less chemically stable than saturated or monounsaturated fat, so total intake shouldn't be excessive.
Omega-3 fatty acid
An 'anti-inflammatory' polyunsaturated fat. Includes ALA (from plants) and, more usefully, EPA and DHA (from fish oil) — the forms the brain and body prefer.
Omega-6 fatty acid
A 'pro-inflammatory' polyunsaturated fat from vegetable oils, nuts, grain-fed meat/eggs, and farm-raised fish. Needed in small amounts, but modern diets have far too much.
Essential fatty acid
A fatty acid the body cannot make and must get from food. The two are alpha-linolenic acid (ALA, omega-3) and linoleic acid (LA, omega-6).
EPA and DHA
Eicosapentaenoic acid and docosahexaenoic acid — the two omega-3s in fish oil that the body actually uses well. The body converts ALA into them only inefficiently, so getting them directly matters.
Omega-6:omega-3 ratio
The balance of pro- to anti-inflammatory fats in the diet. Modern diets run ~20:1 or higher; primitive diets were near 2:1. Fish oil improves this ratio.
Anti-coagulant
Something that thins the blood / slows clotting. Omega-3s act this way, which is why fish oil may need to be paused before surgery or deployment — a physician's call.
Algae oil
A plant-based source of DHA suitable for vegans who can't take fish oil.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Pushing supplements on a client who hasn't fixed their basic diet — food first; supplements are 'an unnecessary focus' until the eating is right.
  • Treating fish oil as a license to eat badly — it 'does not negate the effects of a bad diet.'
  • Recommending flax oil for omega-3 — flax is ALA only and converts poorly to EPA/DHA; it's not an appropriate omega-3 supplement.
  • Ignoring the blood-thinning effect — fish oil is an anti-coagulant; clients near surgery or military deployment should consider stopping ~2 weeks out and consult their doctor.
  • Assuming more omega-3 is always better — total polyunsaturated fat should not be excessive because these fats are less stable.
  • Coaching dosing or supplement decisions for clients with specific medical conditions — that belongs with a primary care doctor, outside the coach's scope of practice.

  5. Tell her fish oil will offset the fast food.

A and C ignore the anti-coagulant risk; B is absurd and unsafe; D swaps in an ineffective omega-3 source and still doesn't address the surgery.

L1 · Section 10 of 27 Tier: ___

A Theoretical Template for CrossFit's Programming

Open the official guide

This section gives you a flexible model for building workouts by rotating three modalities across single-, two-, and three-element days, so you can apply real variance without losing CrossFit's structure.

Key ideas to understand

  • This article bridges the gap between CrossFit's philosophy of fitness and the actual workouts, how you get from theory to practice. It offers a model or template for programming. Important: CrossFit.com has never actually used this template, but it gives new trainers a way to apply variance inside CrossFit's methodology.
  • At first the template looks like a routine, which seems to clash with the demand for constant variance. The resolution: the template still allows wide variance of mode, exercise, metabolic pathway, rest, intensity, sets, and reps. It's mathematically likely that any given three-day cycle is a one-of-a-kind stimulus that's never repeated in a lifetime of training. As Glassman put it, 'What your regimen needs is to not become routine.'
  • The mission is to blend structure and flexibility: enough structure to define your programming goals, but not so rigid that it kills the radically varying stimulus the workouts need. The purpose of the template is as much descriptive (it reflects what good programming already looks like) as prescriptive.
  • Macro view: the default is a three-days-on, one-day-off pattern. This allows a relatively higher volume of high-intensity work than other patterns tried. The athlete can work at or near max intensity for three straight days; by the fourth day the body is hammered enough that continued work gets noticeably less effective without backing off intensity.
  • The drawback of three-on, one-off is that it doesn't line up with the five-on, two-off rhythm most of the world works on, or the seven-day week. For clients in professional settings or with family/work/school scheduling needs, there's a five-days-on, two-days-off version that works very well.
  • History: the WOD started as five-on, two-off and worked perfectly. Three-on, one-off was created later to boost both the intensity of and recovery from the workouts, and feedback confirmed it succeeded. The takeaway: if life is easier on five-on, two-off, use it, the difference in potential isn't big enough to rebuild your whole life around. Convenience, attitude, exercise selection, and pacing will outweigh it.
  • The template rotates three distinct modalities: monostructural metabolic conditioning (M), gymnastics (G), and weightlifting (W).
  • Monostructural metabolic conditioning (M) is what people call 'cardio.' Its main purpose is to improve cardiorespiratory capacity and stamina. These are repetitive, cyclical movements you could sustain for long periods. Examples: run, bike, row, jump rope.
  • Gymnastics (G) is bodyweight work and calisthenics. Its main purpose is to improve body control through neurological components, coordination, balance, agility, accuracy, and to improve functional upper-body capacity and trunk strength. Examples: air squat, pull-up, push-up, dip, handstand push-up, rope climb, muscle-up, press to handstand, back/hip extension, sit-up, jumps, lunge.
  • Weightlifting (W) is the key weight-training basics, Olympic lifts and powerlifting, anything with an added external load. The aim is mainly to increase strength, power, and hip/leg capacity. Examples: deadlift, cleans, press, snatch, clean and jerk, medicine-ball drills/throws, kettlebell swing.
  • The elements in each modality were chosen for their functionality, their neuroendocrine response, and their overall capacity to broadly and dramatically impact the body.
  • Workout structure varies by how many modalities are in a given day: one, two, or three. Days 1, 5, 9 are single-modality; days 2, 6, 10 are couplets (two modalities); days 3, 7, 11 are triplets (three modalities). In every case each M, G, or W is a single exercise from that modality.
  • Single-element days (1, 5, 9), recovery is NOT a limiting factor; the priority is the element itself. A single 'M' (day 1) is typically a long, slow distance effort. A single 'G' (day 5) is practice of one skill, usually one complex enough to need real practice but not yet ready for a timed workout. A single 'W' (day 9) is one lift, typically heavy and low-rep. These days are for long-distance cardio, high-skill gymnastics practice, and heavy single/low-rep lifting, NOT for sprints, pull-ups, or high-rep clean and jerks.
  • Two-element days (2, 6, 10) are 'task priority': a couplet alternated for a set 3-5 rounds, performed for time and usually scored by time. The task is fixed and the time varies. The two elements are moderate to high intensity (made intense by pace, load, reps, or a mix), and work-rest interval management is critical. Ideally round one is hard but possible, and later rounds force pacing, rest, and breaking the work into manageable chunks.
  • Three-element days (3, 7, 11) are 'time priority': a triplet repeated for a set number of minutes (typically 20) and scored by rounds/reps completed. The athlete is kept moving for a fixed time and the goal is as many cycles as possible. The elements are chosen so the challenge shows up only through repeated cycles; individually they're not that significant outside the blistering pace. This is in stark contrast to the higher-intensity two-element days, here work-rest management is only a marginal factor.
  • As the number of elements rises from one to two to three, the workout's effect depends less on the individual element selected and more on the effect of repeated efforts. Each of the three day types has its own distinct character.
  • Application: this template does NOT generate the CrossFit.com WOD, but the qualities of one-, two-, and three-element workouts seen on the WOD motivated its design. The mix has proven crushing in impact and unrivaled in bodily response, and athlete feedback on the WOD gave CrossFit a way to estimate and evaluate workout effects that might have taken decades otherwise.
  • The most effective workouts are like art, remarkable in composition, symmetry, balance, theme, and character. There's a 'choreography' of exertion drawn from physiological knowledge, a sense of the limits of human performance, effective elements, experimentation, and even luck. The hope is the model helps you learn that art.
  • What the template delivers: it encourages new skill development, generates unique stressors, crosses modes, incorporates quality movements, and hits all three metabolic pathways, all inside a framework of sets and reps and a cast of exercises CrossFit has repeatedly tested and proven.

What you should get from it

The template is a teaching model for applying variance, not the actual source of the daily WOD.

What you should get from it

Variance is the point; the template provides structure without becoming a fixed routine.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

The template both describes what good CrossFit programming already looks like and prescribes a way to build it.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Three-on, one-off was created to increase both the intensity of and recovery from workouts, and feedback confirmed it worked.

What you should get from it

The potential difference is small; convenience, attitude, exercise selection, and pacing matter more than squeezing out the last bit of theoretical effectiveness.

What you should get from it

M = monostructural metabolic conditioning (cardio), G = gymnastics (bodyweight), W = weightlifting (external load).

What you should get from it

Each modality targets a different adaptation: M for engine, G for body control and upper-body/trunk, W for strength and power.

What you should get from it

Run/bike/row/jump rope are M; air squat/pull-up/push-up/HSPU/muscle-up etc. are G; deadlift/clean/press/snatch/C&J/KB swing are W.

What you should get from it

Movements aren't random; they're chosen for functionality, hormonal response, and broad bodily impact.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Single M = long slow distance; single G = practice of one complex skill; single W = one heavy, low-rep lift, NOT sprints/pull-ups/high-rep C&J.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Concrete examples: e.g., Run 10 km (single M), a deadlift/HSPU couplet for time, a run/pull-up/thruster triplet for 20 min for rotations.

What you should get from it

The model is designed to deliver variance and completeness inside a proven framework of sets, reps, and exercises.

Words to know (plain English)

Modality
One of CrossFit's three categories of movement used to build workouts: monostructural metabolic conditioning (M), gymnastics (G), and weightlifting (W).
Monostructural metabolic conditioning (M)
'Cardio', repetitive, cyclical movements (run, bike, row, jump rope) aimed at building cardiorespiratory capacity and stamina.
Gymnastics (G)
Bodyweight movements and calisthenics aimed at body control (coordination, balance, agility, accuracy) plus functional upper-body and trunk strength.
Weightlifting (W)
Any movement with an added external load, Olympic lifts and powerlifting basics, aimed at strength, power, and hip/leg capacity.
Couplet
A two-element workout pairing two modalities, usually performed for a fixed number of rounds for time.
Triplet
A three-element workout combining three modalities, usually repeated for a set time and scored by rounds/reps completed.
Task priority
A workout where the task (the rounds and reps) is fixed and the time varies; you race to finish set work, so the score is time.
Time priority
A workout where the time is fixed and the task varies; you keep moving for a set clock and score how many rounds/reps you complete (e.g., a 20-minute AMRAP).
Three-on, one-off
The default training cycle: three consecutive days of high-intensity work followed by one rest day, which allows higher intensity and better recovery.
Five-on, two-off
An alternate cycle (five training days, two rest days) that fits the typical workweek; nearly as effective and easier to schedule for many clients.
Metabolic pathways
The three energy systems (phosphagen, glycolytic, oxidative); the template is built to hit all three across its mix of workouts.
Neuroendocrine response
The hormonal and nervous-system response a movement provokes; one of the reasons specific elements were chosen for the template.
Variance
Deliberately varying mode, exercise, load, reps, rest, and intensity so training stays unpredictable and broadly effective rather than becoming a fixed routine.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Treating the template as the literal source of the CrossFit.com WOD. The text is explicit that CrossFit.com has never used it; it's a teaching model.
  • Reading the template as a rigid routine. The whole point is to apply wide variance inside the structure, not to repeat fixed days.
  • Programming sprints, high-rep pull-ups, or high-rep clean and jerks on a single-element day. Single days (1/5/9) are for long-slow distance, high-skill practice, and heavy low-rep lifting, the wrong place for those.
  • Forcing every workout to fit the template neatly. The author says workouts do not, and should not, fit cleanly inside it.
  • Claiming this (or any) program is legitimate because of an invented scientific theory. Per Glassman's sidebar, programming derives from clinical practice and is justified only by results, not by theory.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new trainer wants to program a single-modality gymnastics day (a single 'G' day) for the class. Which choice best matches the template's intent for that day?

  1. A triplet of pull-ups, push-ups, and air squats for 20 minutes
  2. Dedicated practice of a complex skill like the muscle-up or handstand walk, with long deliberate rest
  3. A long, slow 10 km run
  4. Max-rep pull-ups for time
  5. A heavy, low-rep clean and jerk
Show answer

Answer: B — Dedicated practice of a complex skill like the muscle-up or handstand walk, with long deliberate rest. A single 'G' day is practice of one skill that's complex enough to need real practice but not yet ready for a timed workout, with recovery as a non-limiting factor. Max pull-ups for time (D) and a triplet (A) are intensity/metcon work, not skill practice. A heavy clean and jerk (E) is a single 'W' day, and a 10 km run (C) is a single 'M' day.

Scenario 2. A coach is building a couplet day (two elements) and wants to honor what the template calls 'task priority.' How should it be structured and scored?

  1. A single heavy lift for a 1-rep max, scored by load
  2. A triplet repeated until failure, scored by total reps
  3. A long slow distance effort, scored by distance
  4. As many rounds as possible in 20 minutes, scored by rounds
  5. A fixed 3-5 rounds of the two movements performed for time, scored by time, with work-rest management critical
Show answer

Answer: E — A fixed 3-5 rounds of the two movements performed for time, scored by time, with work-rest management critical. Two-element days are 'task priority': the task (a couplet for a set 3-5 rounds) is fixed and the time varies, so the score is time, and managing work-rest intervals is critical. Option D describes a 'time priority' triplet day. A and C describe single-element days. B mixes up the structure.

Scenario 3. A client works a standard Monday-Friday job and can't reliably train on a rotating three-on, one-off schedule. What does the section recommend?

  1. Have them train seven days a week to make up for it
  2. Insist on three-on, one-off because it's the only effective pattern
  3. Drop weightlifting entirely to simplify the week
  4. Use the five-on, two-off version, since the difference in potential is small and convenience and adherence matter more
  5. Tell them CrossFit won't work for their schedule
Show answer

Answer: D — Use the five-on, two-off version, since the difference in potential is small and convenience and adherence matter more. The text explicitly offers the five-on, two-off regimen for people whose work, family, or school scheduling requires fixed days, and says the difference in potential isn't worth restructuring your whole life. Insisting on three-on, one-off (B) or declaring it won't work (E) ignores that. Seven days a week (A) and dropping a modality (C) aren't supported.

Scenario 4. As you move from a single-element day to a couplet to a triplet, what does the template say happens to where the training effect comes from?

  1. The effect depends more and more on the specific element chosen
  2. The effect disappears because variance cancels it out
  3. The effect depends less on the individual element and more on the effect of repeated efforts
  4. The effect becomes purely about load
  5. The effect only matters on rest days
Show answer

Answer: C — The effect depends less on the individual element and more on the effect of repeated efforts. The section states that as the number of elements increases from one to two to three, the workout's effect is due less to the individual element selected and more to the effect of repeated efforts. The other options contradict that principle.

L1 · Section 11 of 27 Tier: ___

Scaling CrossFit — Modify the Workout, Keep the Stimulus

Open the official guide

How to dial a workout up or down for any athlete without losing what the workout was built to do.

Key ideas to understand

  • Scaling just means modifying the workout so it's safe and productive for the person in front of you — the CrossFit.com workouts are written to challenge the most advanced athlete, so most people need to change something.
  • Finding a CrossFit affiliate is one way to get proper coaching through scaling; this article is the basic playbook when an experienced trainer isn't there.
  • Athletes scale for different lengths of time depending on their athletic background and current health and fitness — plan on at least a month of significant scaling for a beginner. This break-in period builds movement competency AND eases the athlete into intensity and volume gradually.
  • Mechanics and consistency come first, intensity last — that's CrossFit's charter for the best balance of safety, efficacy, and efficiency. Don't add load and speed until the movement is correct and repeatable. Skipping that order raises injury risk and can stall long-term progress, especially when bad mechanics meet load.
  • For a brand-new athlete, just doing the movements unloaded can already be intense — that's fine.
  • The one rule that governs everything: PRESERVE THE STIMULUS. The stimulus is the effect of the specific mix of movements, time domain, and load. Adjust the pieces so the workout hits each athlete roughly the same way regardless of ability.
  • There is no single scaling rule — the range of workouts and beginners is too wide. Use your judgment or a qualified trainer's. Deviating from these guidelines can be the right call, especially for advanced athletes.
  • Don't be afraid to change the workout AFTER it has started — if form goes bad, stop the workout or drop the load to where mechanics are clean.
  • Two things must be scaled for every beginner: intensity and volume. A solid starting method is cutting intensity and/or volume by half for at least two weeks, then building back up over weeks, months, and years as the athlete progresses.
  • Intensity is the power the athlete generates, and you modify it three ways: load, speed, and/or volume.
  • Load is the FIRST variable to scale — it's the easiest way to match the stimulus to an athlete's capacity, and it stays the most common thing you adjust even after the beginner period. On conditioning workouts, pick a load the athlete can complete the first set or round with WITHOUT breaking form or hitting muscular failure.
  • Beginner loads are estimates — scaling won't be perfect, and newer athletes are often under 50% of the prescribed load, especially if they're new to lifting. Err on the side of scaling too much, not too little.
  • Speed is mostly self-regulated by the athlete's fitness, but a coach can manage it — sometimes you slow an athlete down to fix mechanics, occasionally you push a well-moving athlete to go faster (less common with beginners).
  • Volume is the total work done, and you lower it by cutting time, reps/rounds, and/or distance.
  • Beginners will try to grind through a workout that's beyond their current capacity — a workout an advanced athlete finishes in 2 minutes might take a new athlete 15+ minutes as written. Times don't have to match, but they should be relatively similar (Fran should still finish within several minutes).
  • Lowering volume does two jobs: it can raise intensity (more power), AND it lets muscles, ligaments, and tendons adapt gradually — which cuts excessive soreness and the risk of rhabdomyolysis and injury.
  • Movement substitution is the LAST variable to change, because avoiding a movement keeps the athlete from ever getting good at it. Try reducing the load before swapping the movement — e.g., do the snatch with a PVC pipe rather than substituting 95-lb. overhead squats.
  • Swap a movement out entirely when there's a physical limitation or injury, or when load can't be reduced any further. When choosing a substitute, preserve the original movement's function and range of motion as closely as you can.
  • Four things to weigh when picking a substitute: (1) is it primarily lower-body or upper-body driven, (2) its function (push vs. pull), (3) its range of motion — specifically at the hips, knees, and ankles, and (4) its plane of movement.
  • The sample week shows the same logic in action — each WOD gets versions that adjust volume, load, and movements, and these are just a few of many possible options. They don't replace what an experienced trainer would build for a specific athlete.
  • Bottom line: focus on movement proficiency before speed and load, scale significantly for at least a month (especially intensity and volume), and expect to keep scaling load for months or years. Scaled right, the athlete makes real gains by training at their own level of physical and psychological tolerance.

The stimulus is the effect of the specific combination of movements, time domain, and load — that's what you protect when you scale.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Properly scaled training delivers real gains by meeting the athlete where they are — physically and mentally.

Words to know (plain English)

Scaling
Modifying a workout (load, volume, speed, or movement) so it's safe and productive for a specific athlete while still doing what the workout was designed to do.
Preserve the stimulus
The governing rule of scaling — adjust the pieces so the workout produces roughly the same effect on each athlete, regardless of their physical ability.
Stimulus
The training effect of a workout, created by its specific combination of movements, time domain, and load.
Mechanics, consistency, then intensity
CrossFit's charter for safe progress — get the movement correct (mechanics), make it repeatable (consistency), and only then add speed and load (intensity).
Intensity
The amount of power an athlete generates; modified by changing load, speed, and/or volume.
Load
The weight used — the first and most common variable to scale because it's the easiest way to match the stimulus to an athlete's capacity.
Volume
The total amount of work done in a workout; lowered by cutting time, reps/rounds, and/or distance.
Time domain
The duration or time structure of a workout (for time, AMRAP in X minutes, etc.) — one of the three pieces that make up the stimulus.
Movement substitution
Swapping a prescribed movement for a different one — the LAST variable to change, used mainly for injury/limitation or when load can't be reduced; choose a sub that preserves the original's function and range of motion.
Rhabdomyolysis (rhabdo)
A serious condition where overworked muscle breaks down and releases contents into the blood, risking kidney damage; one reason to scale volume for beginners.
Range of motion (ROM)
How far a joint travels through a movement — specifically hips, knees, and ankles — a key thing to match when substituting a movement.
Plane of movement
The direction/orientation in which a movement happens; a consideration when picking a substitute so the swap stays as similar as possible.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Adding load or speed before mechanics are correct and consistent — it raises injury risk and can stall long-term progress.
  • Letting an athlete keep going (or keep loading) once form breaks down — stop the workout or drop the load to where mechanics are clean.
  • Under-scaling a beginner — err on the side of scaling too much; newer athletes are often below 50% of prescribed load.
  • Substituting a movement when you could have just reduced the load — substitution is last because avoiding a movement prevents the athlete from ever developing it.
  • Pushing high volume on a beginner — connective tissue needs gradual adaptation, and excessive volume raises soreness and rhabdomyolysis risk.
  • Forcing a beginner to complete the full prescribed volume just to 'finish like everyone else' — grinding through capacity-exceeding work defeats the purpose.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new member is scheduled for a workout that calls for 95-lb. snatches. She can't snatch that load with sound mechanics. What's the best first move?

  1. Let her attempt the 95 lb. and coach her through the failures
  2. Have her perform the snatches with a PVC pipe or very light load to keep the movement
  3. Substitute 95-lb. overhead squats so she still uses the weight
  4. Replace it with kettlebell swings to save time
  5. Skip the snatch entirely and add extra reps of another movement
Show answer

Answer: B — Have her perform the snatches with a PVC pipe or very light load to keep the movement. Reduce load before substituting — keeping the snatch (even with a PVC pipe) lets her develop the movement. C swaps the movement unnecessarily (substitution is the last variable); E and D substitute when load reduction would have worked; A ignores the mechanics-first rule and the form-breakdown stop point.

Scenario 2. Cindy is '20-minute AMRAP of 5 pull-ups, 10 push-ups, 15 air squats.' You're scaling for a true beginner with limited upper-body strength. Which scaling best preserves the stimulus?

  1. Keep Rx volume but add weight so she gets stronger faster
  2. Cut the time or cap the rounds, scale the pull-up (ring rows/jumping/banded) and push-up (from knees/wall), and keep the air squats
  3. Replace air squats with leg presses and keep pull-ups and push-ups Rx
  4. Remove pull-ups and push-ups and have her only do air squats for 20 minutes
  5. Keep all movements and time as Rx and tell her to just go slower
Show answer

Answer: B — Cut the time or cap the rounds, scale the pull-up (ring rows/jumping/banded) and push-up (from knees/wall), and keep the air squats. This scales the high-skill upper-body movements (the usual bottleneck for beginners) and reduces volume via time/round cap while keeping the air squat, which should be maintained unless injured. E leaves the upper-body load too high; C swaps a foundational movement unnecessarily; D guts the workout's pushing/pulling stimulus; A violates mechanics-before-load.

Scenario 3. Mid-workout, an athlete's deadlift form starts rounding badly under the load. What should the coach do?

  1. Stop the workout or reduce the load right now to where mechanics are clean
  2. Switch her to a 1-rep max attempt instead
  3. Add a belt and keep the same load
  4. Tell her to speed up to get the reps over with
  5. Let her finish the round, then talk about it afterward
Show answer

Answer: A — Stop the workout or reduce the load right now to where mechanics are clean. The guide says don't be afraid to alter the workout after it begins — at the appearance of unsafe form, end the workout or reduce the load to where proper mechanics return. E and D keep loading bad mechanics (injury risk); C and B don't fix the mechanics problem.

Scenario 4. You're setting a starting point for a brand-new athlete's first couple of weeks of CrossFit. What's the most prudent approach?

  1. Run everything as prescribed so they learn the real standard
  2. Only scale load and never touch volume
  3. Scale hard for one session, then go full Rx
  4. Maximize volume early to build a base fast
  5. Reduce intensity and/or volume by about half for at least two weeks, then build gradually
Show answer

Answer: E — Reduce intensity and/or volume by about half for at least two weeks, then build gradually. Both intensity and volume must be scaled for every beginner, and cutting them by about half for at least two weeks (then progressing) is the recommended method. A ignores beginner scaling; B ignores volume; C abandons the at-least-a-month break-in; D risks soreness, rhabdo, and injury from too much volume too soon.
