"The Girls" for Grandmas: Scaling Benchmarks for Anyone

Open the official guide

Shows you exactly how to tune down six benchmark 'Girl' WODs for the elderly, beginner, or deconditioned athlete while keeping the workout's stimulus intact -- proof that CrossFit scales to everyone.

Key ideas to understand

  • The whole point of this article is to PROVE universal scalability -- if Angie, Barbara, Chelsea, Diane, Elizabeth, and Fran can be made to work for grandmas, they can be made to work for anyone.
  • The golden rule of scaling: preserve the stimulus. You adhere to as many of the original workout factors as possible, relative to that individual's physical AND psychological tolerances. You keep what makes the workout what it is; you adjust what they can't yet handle.
  • Two scaling levers show up over and over here: substitute the movement (swap to an easier version that hits the same pattern) and reduce the volume/load (fewer reps, fewer rounds, lighter weight, shorter clock).
  • Angie (original: 100 pull-ups, 100 push-ups, 100 sit-ups, 100 squats for time) scales to 25 ring rows, 25 push-ups off the knees, 25 sit-ups, 25 squats -- same four movements and same couplet logic, just substituted pulling/pushing and quartered volume.
  • Barbara (original: 5 rounds of 20 pull-ups / 30 push-ups / 40 sit-ups / 50 squats, 3 min rest between rounds) scales to 3 rounds of 20 ring rows / 30 push-ups off the knees / 40 sit-ups / 50 squats, keeping the 3-minute rest. Note: the rep scheme per round stays the same -- they cut the number of rounds, not the reps, and kept the rest interval that defines Barbara.
  • Chelsea (original: 5 pull-ups / 10 push-ups / 15 squats every minute on the minute for 30 minutes) scales to 5 ring rows / 10 push-ups off the knees / 15 squats EMOM for 20 minutes -- same EMOM structure and same rep triplet, just substituted movements and a shorter total duration.
  • Diane (original: 21-15-9 deadlift 225 lb. and handstand push-ups) scales to 21-15-9 deadlift 50 lb. and dumbbell shoulder press 10 lb. -- the 21-15-9 couplet stays, the deadlift load drops, and the inverted handstand push-up becomes an upright dumbbell shoulder press (same vertical press pattern, far less skill and risk).
  • Fran (original: 21-15-9 thruster 95 lb. and pull-ups) scales to 21-15-9 thruster 25 lb. and ring rows -- keep the 21-15-9 thruster/pull couplet, lighten the thruster, and swap the pull-up for the ring row.
  • Elizabeth (original: 21-15-9 clean 135 lb. and ring dips) scales to 21-15-9 clean 25 lb. and bench dips -- keep the 21-15-9 clean/dip couplet, lighten the clean, and swap ring dips for the much more accessible bench dips.
  • Across all six, the pattern is identical: keep the rep scheme and the movement pattern (the stimulus), and adjust load, total volume, and movement difficulty to fit the person in front of you.
  • The common substitutions to memorize: pull-up -> ring row, push-up -> push-up off the knees, handstand push-up -> dumbbell shoulder press, ring dip -> bench dip, and heavy barbell -> light barbell.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

A reusable substitution toolkit: every harder movement has an easier same-pattern version you can sub in.

Words to know (plain English)

Scaling
Adjusting a workout (load, volume, movement) so a given athlete can do it safely and effectively while keeping its intended effect.
Preserve the stimulus
The guiding rule of scaling -- keep as many of the original workout's defining factors (rep scheme, movement pattern, time domain) as possible relative to the person's physical and psychological tolerances.
Benchmark / 'The Girls'
Named, repeatable standard workouts (Angie, Barbara, Chelsea, Diane, Elizabeth, Fran) used to measure progress over time.
Ring row
A scaled pulling movement -- rowing the body up to gymnastics rings from an angled body position; the standard sub for the pull-up.
Push-up off the knees
A scaled push-up performed from the knees to reduce the load while keeping the pushing pattern.
Dumbbell shoulder press
An upright vertical press with dumbbells -- the accessible scale for the handstand push-up's inverted pressing pattern.
Bench dip
A dip performed with hands on a bench and feet on the floor -- the easy scale for ring dips.
21-15-9
A descending rep scheme: 21 reps, then 15, then 9 of each movement -- the structure behind Diane, Fran, and Elizabeth.
EMOM (every minute on the minute)
A format where you perform a set amount of work at the start of each minute and rest the remainder -- the structure of Chelsea.
Psychological tolerance
How much intensity/volume an athlete can handle mentally and emotionally, not just physically -- a factor you respect when scaling.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Don't scale by gutting the stimulus. If you cut so much that the workout no longer looks or feels like the original (wrong rep scheme, wrong movement pattern, wrong time domain), you've stopped scaling and started writing a different workout.
  • Respect psychological tolerance, not just physical. A deconditioned or elderly athlete can be overwhelmed mentally as well as physically -- scale to both.
  • Match the movement pattern when you substitute. A pull-up scales to a ring row (a pull), not to something that changes the pattern. Same with handstand push-up -> dumbbell press (both vertical pushes).

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A fit but brand-new athlete can't do strict handstand push-ups yet. You're programming a scaled Diane. Which substitution best preserves the stimulus?

  1. Replace handstand push-ups with dumbbell shoulder press and lighten the deadlift
  2. Replace handstand push-ups with pull-ups
  3. Replace handstand push-ups with extra deadlifts
  4. Replace the whole workout with a light jog
  5. Keep handstand push-ups but let them kip off the wall sloppily
Show answer

Answer: A — Replace handstand push-ups with dumbbell shoulder press and lighten the deadlift. The guide scales Diane's handstand push-up to a dumbbell shoulder press -- both are vertical pressing patterns, so the stimulus is preserved while skill and risk drop. The deadlift also lightens. C and B change the movement pattern entirely, D abandons the workout, and E keeps an unsafe movement the athlete hasn't earned.

Scenario 2. You're scaling Barbara for a deconditioned group. The original is 5 rounds of 20 pull-ups / 30 push-ups / 40 sit-ups / 50 squats with 3 minutes rest between rounds. Which scale best follows the guide's approach?

  1. 5 rounds of the original movements but remove all rest
  2. 1 round of the original reps as fast as possible
  3. 5 rounds of 4/6/8/10 reps with no rest
  4. 3 rounds of 20 ring rows / 30 push-ups off the knees / 40 sit-ups / 50 squats, keeping the 3-minute rest
  5. 3 rounds of pull-ups and push-ups only, drop sit-ups and squats
Show answer

Answer: D — 3 rounds of 20 ring rows / 30 push-ups off the knees / 40 sit-ups / 50 squats, keeping the 3-minute rest. The guide cuts Barbara from 5 to 3 rounds, substitutes ring rows and knee push-ups, KEEPS the per-round rep scheme, and KEEPS the 3-minute rest that defines the workout. The rest interval is part of Barbara's stimulus. C changes the reps and kills the rest, B and A remove the defining rest, and E drops movements that are part of the workout.

Scenario 3. An elderly client is physically capable of more reps but gets anxious and discouraged by long workouts. How should that affect your scaling of Chelsea (30-minute EMOM)?

  1. Add weight so each minute is more challenging
  2. Ignore it -- only physical capacity matters in scaling
  3. Drop the EMOM format entirely and let them go untimed
  4. Shorten the duration (e.g., the 20-minute scaled version) to respect psychological tolerance while keeping the EMOM format and rep triplet
  5. Make it harder to build mental toughness
Show answer

Answer: D — Shorten the duration (e.g., the 20-minute scaled version) to respect psychological tolerance while keeping the EMOM format and rep triplet. Scaling preserves the stimulus relative to the individual's physical AND psychological tolerances. The scaled Chelsea keeps the EMOM and 5/10/15 triplet but shortens to 20 minutes -- exactly the right lever for an athlete limited by mental tolerance. B ignores half the rule, E and A push the wrong direction, and C destroys the format that makes it Chelsea.

Scenario 4. What is the single most important principle that should guide every scaling decision in this article?

  1. Always make the workout shorter
  2. Replace barbell movements with bodyweight movements every time
  3. Keep the exact same total reps no matter what
  4. Always reduce the load by exactly 50%
  5. Preserve the stimulus -- keep as many defining workout factors as possible relative to the person's physical and psychological tolerances
Show answer

Answer: E — Preserve the stimulus -- keep as many defining workout factors as possible relative to the person's physical and psychological tolerances. The article states the intent of scaling is to preserve the stimulus, adhering to as many original workout factors as possible relative to the individual's physical and psychological tolerances. Everything else (shortening, lightening, substituting) is just a tool in service of that one principle. A, D, B, and C are rigid rules the guide never sets -- scaling is judgment in service of the stimulus.

L1 · Section 13 of 27 Tier: ___

Running a CrossFit Class: Lesson Plans and WOD Scales

Open the official guide

A short primer on planning and running a group class, plus three full minute-by-minute lesson plans (Fran, the back squat, a 20-minute AMRAP) you can copy.

Key ideas to understand

  • At most affiliates, group classes outnumber private sessions. The same coaching shows up in both, but a group adds logistical pressure, so planning matters more, not less.
  • Programming a good workout and offering scaling options is only PART of running a class. At a minimum, draft a warm-up, workout, and cool-down plan before class that lays out how long each part takes and exactly what is in it.
  • Use the warm-up checklist as your filter: does it raise core temperature, prepare athletes for the workout's intensity, let you correct the mechanics the workout needs, let you assess who needs scaling, and (time permitting) build skill — even on movements not in the workout?
  • Use the workout checklist: does it describe range-of-motion standards, include scaling for EVERY athlete in the room, let each athlete hit their own relative high intensity, challenge their current fitness, and let you keep correcting mechanics while intensity is high?
  • Use the cool-down checklist: does it let heart rate and breathing slow and mental sharpness return, let athletes log their score to track progress, reset the gym for the next class, and use leftover time for recovery, extra skill work, or education?
  • The class is COACH LED start to finish. Demonstrate every new piece before athletes do it, and keep cueing better positions through every section — whiteboard, warm-up, workout, cool-down.
  • Every lesson plan follows the same skeleton: whiteboard brief, general warm-up, movement-specific warm-up(s), a short break and logistics/safety check, the workout, then the cool-down — all inside a 60-minute window.
  • Each lesson plan starts with an INTENDED STIMULUS (what the workout is supposed to feel like and accomplish) and a BREAKDOWN (rep targets, time estimates, the Rx'd female weight, scaling options, and the specific things to demonstrate, ask, and explain).
  • Fran (21-15-9 thrusters at 95 lb. and pull-ups, scored by total time) is a fast couplet — lower-body push paired with upper-body pull — that lets movement stay continuous, so the real challenge is managing a very high heart rate, not strength.
  • On Fran, the rule of thumb for unbroken work: don't break the movements more than three times in the set of 21, twice in the 15, once in the 9; aim to finish under 10 minutes; if a scaled Fran came in under 5 minutes last time, make it harder.
  • The Fran lesson plan uses progressive specific warm-ups: a pull-up warm-up where 8-10 consecutive pull-ups predicts the athlete can handle the prescribed reps, and a thruster warm-up where you assess the movement to set the right workout load.
  • The back squat lesson plan (5-5-5-5-5, score = max load for a set of 5) is a single-modality weightlifting HEAVY day with ascending sets; at 5 reps it biases toward strength-stamina, not a true 1-rep max, and demands 3-5 minutes rest between working sets.
  • On a heavy day, EVERY athlete should find a heavy set of 5 relative to their own capacity; beginners or intermediates without an established 5-rep max may do more than five working sets, as long as total volume stays appropriate, and you reduce load when reps fail or form breaks down.
  • The 20-minute AMRAP (400-m run, 15 L pull-ups, 5 reps of 205-lb. clean and jerk, scored by rounds and reps) is a triplet — monostructural, gymnastics, weightlifting — built so the run elevates heart rate and makes the other two harder; load and reps should be well within capacity when each is considered alone.
  • WOD Scale pages give you ready-made BEGINNER and INTERMEDIATE versions of each workout that preserve the stimulus by adjusting load, volume, or movement (e.g., ring rows for pull-ups, reduced run distance, lighter clean and jerk) — but you're encouraged to use judgment for challenging-but-manageable substitutions per athlete.
  • Every workout block includes the same coach housekeeping: demonstrate each movement with its standards, explain how it's scored, and ASK whether any athletes are injured before they start.
  • The break-and-logistics block before the workout is your safety gate: bathroom, review each athlete's scaling, check for adequate space around barbells (including bounce room) and pull-up stations, teach/confirm spotting on the heavy day, then re-brief the workout, flow, and safety.

What you should get from it

Group coaching is the default at most affiliates, and the extra logistics are exactly why a written plan matters more than in a private session.

The minimum bar for 'planned' is three drafted blocks with times and contents — not a workout scribbled on the board at the last minute.

A warm-up earns its place only if it raises core temp, preps for intensity, lets you correct needed mechanics, lets you assess for scaling, and (time permitting) develops skill.

Fran flows continuously because the two movements don't compete for the same muscles; the real test is surviving an extremely high heart rate.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

In a triplet built around a run, you load the other two movements lighter than they'd be alone, because the run is doing the metabolic damage.

What you should get from it

The pre-workout break is the dedicated safety gate: space, spotting, scaling review, and a final re-brief before anyone starts.

What you should get from it

Three things happen at the board every single class: demo with standards, state the scoring, and screen for injuries.

What you should get from it

Coaching doesn't stop when the clock starts — you keep cueing and you keep scaling live, mid-workout.

What you should get from it

Closing the class means capturing data (the score) and the human moment (celebration) — both are part of the plan.

Words to know (plain English)

Intended stimulus
What a workout is designed to feel like and accomplish — the time domain, loading, and challenge it should produce. You scale TO preserve this, not just to make a workout easier.
Breakdown
The coaching notes for a workout: rep and time targets, the Rx'd weights, scaling options, and the things the coach must demonstrate, explain, and ask before starting.
Rx'd (as prescribed)
The standard, unscaled version of a workout — the prescribed loads, reps, and movements (e.g., 95-lb. thrusters for men, 65-lb. for women on Fran).
Couplet / triplet
A workout built from two movements (couplet, like Fran) or three movements (triplet, like the 20-minute AMRAP). Pairing complementary patterns lets athletes keep moving.
AMRAP
As Many Rounds (and reps) As Possible in a set time. The 20-minute AMRAP is scored by completed rounds and reps.
Monostructural / gymnastics / weightlifting
The three modalities CrossFit draws from: monostructural is cardio-type work like running or rowing; gymnastics is bodyweight movement like pull-ups; weightlifting is loaded movement like the clean and jerk.
Heavy day
A single-modality strength session (e.g., back squat 5-5-5-5-5) where the goal is to find a heavy load for the rep scheme, with long rest between sets.
Ascending sets
Adding load after every set, so each working set is heavier than the last.
L pull-up
A pull-up performed with the legs held straight out in an 'L' the whole time; it demands more midline and pulling strength than a strict pull-up, and the legs start elevated rather than swinging up with momentum.
Threshold training
Pushing an athlete's intensity right up to the edge of where mechanics would break down — high intensity on sound technique, the line a coach manages during the workout.
General vs. specific warm-up
The general warm-up raises temperature and primes the whole body; the specific warm-up builds and assesses the exact movements (and load) the workout will use.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Don't run a class off a last-minute whiteboard scribble. The minimum is a drafted warm-up, workout, and cool-down with times and contents — group logistics demand it.
  • Always ask if any athletes are injured before the workout starts — it's listed in every breakdown for a reason.
  • Safety gate before every workout: confirm adequate space around barbells (including bounce room when bars are dropped) and around pull-up stations, and that athletes know how to spot on the heavy day.
  • On the heavy day, reduce the load the moment an athlete misses 5 reps or form degrades significantly — don't let ego keep weight on the bar.
  • Keep correcting mechanics DURING the workout, not just in the warm-up. If mechanics don't improve over multiple reps, be willing to slow the athlete down, lower the load, or stop them.
  • Finishers and extra skill work are optional — a finisher must not turn into a second workout, and not every class needs one.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. You're briefing Fran (21-15-9, 95-lb. thrusters and pull-ups). A newer athlete got kipping pull-ups two weeks ago but can only string 4-5 together. What's the best scaling call?

  1. Reduce the pull-up volume (e.g., 15-12-9) and lower the thruster load so she still hits a fast, continuous stimulus
  2. Tell her to rest as long as she needs between every rep so she can finish Rx'd
  3. Swap all her pull-ups for ring rows and keep 95-lb. thrusters
  4. Have her do strict pull-ups for the full 21-15-9 to build strength
  5. Keep her fully Rx'd — she has kipping pull-ups now, so reps don't need to change
Show answer

Answer: A — Reduce the pull-up volume (e.g., 15-12-9) and lower the thruster load so she still hits a fast, continuous stimulus. The guide says when kipping pull-ups are newly acquired and 8-10 consecutive aren't yet feasible, lower the volume — and either element can be modified in load. Reducing pull-up reps and the thruster load preserves Fran's fast, continuous stimulus. E ignores that she can't sustain the reps; C over-scales away the pull-up entirely when a volume cut would do; D turns a heart-rate workout into a strength grind; B lets the workout drag well past the intended fast stimulus.

Scenario 2. It's the back squat heavy day (5-5-5-5-5). A beginner has never established a 5-rep max and is still moving well after five working sets without finding a true heavy five. What should you do?

  1. Stop at five sets regardless — the prescription is 5-5-5-5-5
  2. Drop the rest to 60 seconds so she can fit more sets in the time cap
  3. Let her continue adding load for a couple more working sets to find a heavy 5, while you keep total volume appropriate
  4. Switch her to a 1-rep max attempt to find her max faster
  5. Move her to front squats so the load is lighter
Show answer

Answer: C — Let her continue adding load for a couple more working sets to find a heavy 5, while you keep total volume appropriate. The WOD Scale page explicitly says beginners/intermediates without an established 5-rep max may do more than five working sets, as long as the coach keeps overall volume appropriate. A wastes the chance to find her heavy 5; D abandons the day's 5-rep stimulus and adds risk; B violates the 3-5 minute rest needed to maximize loading; E changes the movement when she's squatting fine — the issue is finding load, not the variation.

Scenario 3. You're setting up the 20-minute AMRAP (400-m run, 15 L pull-ups, 5 reps of 205-lb. clean and jerk). One athlete can do the run, but in the warm-up he can only hold a brief L and gets 2-3 L pull-ups before his form falls apart. Best move?

  1. Reduce his L pull-up volume and/or modify to banded L pull-ups so he keeps full range in good positions, and scale as needed
  2. Replace the L pull-ups with kipping pull-ups where he swings the legs up with momentum
  3. Keep him Rx'd on the L pull-ups — capacity will show up once the clock starts
  4. Drop him to ring rows for the whole workout
  5. Cut the run instead and leave the L pull-ups Rx'd
Show answer

Answer: A — Reduce his L pull-up volume and/or modify to banded L pull-ups so he keeps full range in good positions, and scale as needed. The L pull-up specific warm-up is the assessment: the capacity he shows there tells you whether L pull-ups belong in his workout. The beginner scale modifies L pull-ups in reps and loading (banded) so the band still allows full range in proper positions. C ignores the warm-up signal; B defeats the L pull-up by using momentum (the guide says it starts with legs already elevated, not swung up); E scales the wrong element and leaves a movement he can't sustain; D over-scales when a banded L pull-up with reduced volume would keep the intended midline/pulling stimulus.

Scenario 4. Mid-Fran, an athlete is shifting his weight onto his toes and pressing the thruster too early, fatiguing his arms. What's the best coaching response in the moment?

  1. Pull him out of the workout for the day
  2. Say nothing — corrections belong in the warm-up, not the workout
  3. Tell him to push harder and faster to get through it
  4. Stop the clock for the whole class and re-teach the thruster
  5. Cue him toward a better position (weight on heels, finish the squat drive before pressing) while keeping him moving, and lower the load if mechanics don't improve
Show answer

Answer: E — Cue him toward a better position (weight on heels, finish the squat drive before pressing) while keeping him moving, and lower the load if mechanics don't improve. The workout block specifically says to watch for thruster athletes who shift to the toes and press too soon, and to cue better positions while maintaining technique and further scale as needed. Movement change continues through the workout, not just the warm-up. B is wrong because coaching doesn't stop at the clock; D and A over-react to a fixable fault; C adds intensity to a breaking pattern, which is the opposite of threshold training.

L1 · Section 14 of 27 Tier: ___

Anatomy and Physiology for Jocks: A Shared Movement Language

Open the official guide

Gives you a dead-simple shared vocabulary -- four body parts, three joints, two motions, three rules -- so you and your athletes can talk about movement fast and get them coachable.

Key ideas to understand

  • Coaching is communication, and communication gets way easier when you and the athlete share the same words for body parts and for how those parts move. This whole lesson exists to make athletes more coachable, not to turn them into anatomy students.
  • Learn just FOUR body parts: tibia, femur, pelvis, and spine. That's it -- you don't need a textbook's worth of names.
  • Group those four parts into two things athletes can feel: the LEG is the tibia plus the femur, and the TRUNK is the pelvis plus the spine.
  • Learn THREE joints (the spine itself not counted as one): the knee connects tibia to femur, the hip connects femur to pelvis, and the sacroiliac (SI) joint connects pelvis to spine. Also make the point that the spine is really a whole bunch of joints stacked up.
  • Learn TWO directions of motion: flexion is reducing the angle of a joint (closing it), extension is increasing the angle of a joint (opening it). Same two words work at every joint.
  • Before moving on, test the athletes -- can they flex and extend the leg (knee), hip, spine, and trunk (SI) on cue? Once that's solid, cue combinations like 'flex one leg and your trunk but NOT your hip' to prove they really own it.
  • Then the payoff: three simple rules of biomechanics. Rule one -- functional movement weds the spine to the pelvis; the SI joint and spine are built for small movement in many directions, so keep the trunk tight and solid for running, jumping, squatting, throwing, cycling, everything.
  • Rule two -- the power of those movements comes from the HIP, primarily hip extension. Powerful hip extension is necessary and nearly sufficient for elite athletic capacity.
  • Rule three -- do not let the pelvis chase the femur instead of the spine. That mistake is 'muted hip function': the hip angle stays open, so it has nothing left to extend and the movement loses its power.
  • The whole package -- four parts, three joints, two motions, three rules -- gives a small but powerful shared lexicon whose immediate effect is a more coachable athlete.

Find & highlight0/10 found

Open the guide to this section and track these down — highlight each one, then check it off. Tap a clue to reveal what it teaches.

What you should get from it

The entire reason for this lesson: a common language makes coaching fast and accurate -- it's a coaching tool, not an anatomy course.

What you should get from it

Tibia, femur, pelvis, spine -- the only four names anyone needs to memorize.

What you should get from it

Leg = tibia + femur; trunk = pelvis + spine. Two groupings the athlete can actually feel and move.

What you should get from it

Knee connects tibia-femur, hip connects femur-pelvis, SI connects pelvis-spine; the spine itself is many joints.

What you should get from it

Flexion closes a joint angle, extension opens it -- the same two words apply at every joint.

What you should get from it

You don't move on until the athlete can flex/extend each joint independently on cue -- that's how you confirm they own the language.

What you should get from it

Midline stabilization: lock the trunk as one solid unit for every functional movement.

What you should get from it

Power comes from the hip. Hip extension is the engine of athletic performance.

What you should get from it

Muted hip = open hip angle with no extension left = powerless movement. Keep the pelvis tied to the spine, not the femur.

What you should get from it

The whole point in one sentence -- a tiny shared vocabulary instantly makes athletes easier to coach.

Words to know (plain English)

Tibia
The shin bone -- the lower leg bone between the knee and ankle.
Femur
The thigh bone -- the upper leg bone between the hip and knee.
Pelvis
The hip bone structure that the femur attaches to below and the spine attaches to above.
Spine
The back/vertebral column. Has a natural 'S' shape and is really many small joints stacked together.
Knee joint
The joint connecting tibia and femur -- the bend in the leg.
Hip joint
The joint connecting femur to pelvis -- where most athletic power is generated.
Sacroiliac (SI) joint
The joint connecting pelvis to spine; built for small movement in many directions, so we keep it tight during lifts.
Leg
Coaching shorthand for the tibia plus the femur together.
Trunk
Coaching shorthand for the pelvis plus the spine together.
Flexion
Reducing (closing) the angle of a joint.
Extension
Increasing (opening) the angle of a joint.
Hip extension
Opening the hip angle by driving the pelvis and femur apart -- the primary source of athletic power; 'power comes from the hip.'
Midline stabilization
Keeping the trunk tight and solid -- the spine wedded to the pelvis as one unit -- through a movement.
Muted hip function
The fault where the pelvis chases the femur instead of staying with the spine; the hip angle stays open and can't extend, so the movement loses power.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Muted hip function -- if the pelvis chases the femur instead of the spine, the hip can't extend and you lose all the power. Watch for it in every functional movement.
  • Don't turn this into an anatomy class. The goal is a shared coaching vocabulary that makes athletes coachable, not memorizing every bone in the body.
  • Don't move past the lesson until the athlete can flex and extend each joint on cue. If they can't, your cues won't land later.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. You cue an athlete to 'open the hip' at the top of a deadlift, but you notice the bar drifts forward and their butt shoots up first while the hip angle stays open the whole time -- they never actually finish with the hips. What is this fault called and what's the priority fix?

  1. Trunk extension -- have them arch the back harder
  2. SI joint instability -- send them to a physio
  3. Muted hip function -- get the pelvis to stay with the spine and drive to full hip extension
  4. Flexion fault -- tell them to bend the knees more
  5. Knee fault -- cue the knees out
Show answer

Answer: C — Muted hip function -- get the pelvis to stay with the spine and drive to full hip extension. When the pelvis chases the femur and the hip angle stays open with no extension, that's muted hip function -- the textbook power leak. The fix is tying the pelvis to the spine and finishing with powerful hip extension. D and E address the wrong joint, B is outside your scope and unnecessary here, and A (arching harder) doesn't restore hip extension.

Scenario 2. A brand-new athlete has no movement vocabulary. Following this lesson, what should you teach FIRST before you ever ask them to combine movements?

  1. The three rules of biomechanics
  2. The Olympic lifts so they learn by doing
  3. Every bone and muscle group in the lower body
  4. The four parts, three joints, and the two motions -- flexion and extension at each joint
  5. How to read a whiteboard workout
Show answer

Answer: D — The four parts, three joints, and the two motions -- flexion and extension at each joint. The lesson builds in order: first the four parts and three joints, then the two motions (flexion/extension), confirmed at each joint, and only then the three rules. You can't cue combinations or teach the rules until the athlete owns the basic parts and motions. A comes after, C is overkill the lesson explicitly avoids, and B/E skip the foundation.

Scenario 3. You want to confirm an athlete truly understands the shared movement language before progressing. Which check best proves it?

  1. Have them complete a benchmark WOD
  2. Ask them to name all four bones out loud
  3. Ask them to point to their SI joint
  4. Have them recite the definition of flexion
  5. Cue a combination like 'flex one leg and your trunk but not your hip' and watch them do it correctly
Show answer

Answer: E — Cue a combination like 'flex one leg and your trunk but not your hip' and watch them do it correctly. The guide says once flexion vs. extension is understood at each joint, you cue combinations to test real understanding. Doing 'flex one leg and trunk but not your hip' on cue proves they can isolate and control each joint -- that's mastery of the language. Naming, reciting, or pointing (B, D, C) shows memorization, not movement control, and A doesn't isolate the concept.

Scenario 4. An athlete asks why you keep harping on keeping the trunk tight and solid during running, squatting, and throwing. Based on the three rules, what's the most accurate coach's answer?

  1. Because the SI joint and spine are built for big ranges of motion, so we move them a lot
  2. Because flexion is always safer than extension
  3. Because the knees track the toes
  4. Because the leg generates the power, not the hip
  5. Because a tight trunk lets the hip be the engine -- the spine and pelvis stay wedded so power transfers, while the hip provides the dynamics through extension
Show answer

Answer: E — Because a tight trunk lets the hip be the engine -- the spine and pelvis stay wedded so power transfers, while the hip provides the dynamics through extension. Rule one says wed the spine to the pelvis and keep the trunk tight and solid; rule two says the dynamics come from the hip via extension. A locked trunk is what lets the hip be the power source. A is backwards (the SI/spine are for SMALL multi-directional movement, so we stabilize them), C is unrelated, B is false, and D contradicts 'power comes from the hip.'

L1 · Section 15 of 27 Tier: ___

Squat Clinic — Nature's Movement, the Faults, and the Fixes

Open the official guide

Why the squat is a natural, rehabilitative human movement and exactly how to coach it, spot a bad one, and fix it.

Key ideas to understand

  • The squat is essential to your well-being — it improves athleticism and keeps the hips, back, and knees sound into old age.
  • The squat is not bad for the knees — it's remarkably rehabilitative of cranky, damaged, or delicate knees, hips, and backs. The flip side: if you DON'T squat, your knees aren't healthy, no matter how pain-free you feel.
  • The squat is no more invented by a coach than the hiccup or the sneeze — it's a natural, functional component of being human. The bottom of the squat is nature's intended sitting posture (chairs aren't part of our biology), and rising from the bottom is the biomechanically sound way we stand up.
  • Most of the world sits in a squat, not on chairs — meals, ceremonies, conversation, and defecation all happen without seats. Only the industrialized world needs chairs, and that costs us functionality and contributes to decrepitude.
  • When a doctor or chiropractor tells someone not to squat, it's almost always ignorance — ask by what method the patient is supposed to get off the toilet and they're stuck for an answer.
  • The 'don't bend the knee past 90 degrees' advice is nonsense — you can't even stand up off the floor without bending a knee well past 90, and getting up off the floor loads at least one knee far more than a squat does.
  • People who counsel against squatting are usually repeating media/gym myths, or they've seen people hurt themselves squatting INCORRECTLY. Yes, you can injure yourself with a bad squat — but it's also exceedingly easy to make the squat as safe as walking.
  • Athletically, the squat is the quintessential hip-extension exercise, and hip extension is the foundation of all good human movement. Powerful, controlled hip extension is necessary AND nearly sufficient for elite athleticism — without it you're nowhere near your potential, and everyone who can explosively open the hip can also run, jump, throw, and punch hard.
  • The squat also elicits a potent neuroendocrine response — by itself a strong reason to include it.
  • There's a long list of cues to a sound squat (22 of them) — many drive the same behavior. Key ones: feet about shoulder width and slightly toed out; head up looking slightly above parallel, never down; accentuate the lumbar arch then pull the excess out with the abs; midsection very tight; butt back and down; knees track over the line of the foot and never roll inside it; weight on the heels, off the balls of the feet; delay the knees' forward travel; in profile the ear travels straight down, not forward; pull yourself down with the hip flexors rather than just sinking; break parallel (hip fold below the knee); rise by squeezing glutes and hams with no forward lean; return on the exact path you descended; use every bit of musculature; and stand as tall as possible at the top.
  • All athletes start with the AIR SQUAT — body weight only. 'Squat' on its own means an unladen, body-weight squat; weighted versions get named: back squat, front squat, overhead squat. Loading those before the air squat is mastered retards athletic potential and compromises safety and efficacy.
  • The squat is mastered when both technique AND performance are superior — no points of performance are deficient, and fast multiple reps are possible. The favorite standard: a Tabata squat (20s on / 10s off x 8) where the weakest of the eight intervals is 18-20 perfect reps. Don't even think about weighted squats until you can do that.
  • The most common faults to look for: surrendering the lumbar curve at the bottom (rounding the back — maybe the worst fault), not breaking parallel, slouching the chest and shoulders, lifting the heels, and not fully extending the hip at the top.
  • A small angle of hip extension (an upright-but-shallow beginner's/weak squat from weak hip extensors) is NOT strictly a fault as long as the lumbar spine stays neutral.
  • There are five root causes of a bad squat: weak glute/hamstring; poor engagement/weak control/no awareness of the glute and ham (a three-to-five-year odyssey to build); trying to squat with the quads (leg-extension dominance); inflexibility (tight hamstrings pull you into lumbar flexion — the worst fault); and sloppy work / poor focus.
  • There are specific therapies for fixing faults: Bar Holds (settle into a perfect bottom holding a high, close bar; let go, repeat higher/closer — lifts the squat and loads the heels and glute/hams); Box Squatting (squat to a 10-inch box, rest without losing posture, squeeze and rise without rocking — perfected at Westside Barbell); Bottom-to-Bottoms (live at the bottom, come to full extension only briefly and return, spending far more time down than up — learn to like the bottom); and Overhead Squats (broomstick at snatch-grip overhead, arms locked, the arm-stick triangle stays perpendicular to the ground — a shoulder stretch that instantly diagnoses tightness and 'lifts' the squat).
  • The troubleshooting table maps faults to causes to therapies — e.g., not hitting parallel comes from weak hip extensors/laziness/quad dominance and is fixed with bottom-to-bottoms, bar holds, and box squatting; rolling knees inside the feet comes from weak adductors/abductors and quad cheating and is fixed by pushing the feet to the outside of the shoe and deliberately abducting (trying to stretch the floor apart).
  • The broomstick overhead-squat foundation is critical to learning the snatch — the world's fastest lift.
  • Two named weighted squats close the section: the FRONT SQUAT (bar racked on the chest and shoulders with a loose grip; mechanics otherwise like the air squat; the rack position is the hard part and forces shoulder and wrist flexibility — handstands help), and the air-squat points of performance recap: maintain the back arch, look straight ahead, weight on heels, full range of motion below parallel, chest high, midsection tight.

The squat heals knees/hips/backs; NOT squatting is itself a sign of unhealthy joints.

What you should get from it

The squat is a natural, built-in human movement — not a coach's invention; chairs aren't part of our biology.

Powerful, controlled hip extension is the foundation of all good human movement — the athletic core of the squat.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

A shallow/upright beginner squat from weak hip extensors is acceptable if the lower back stays neutral — weakness isn't the same as a fault.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

The air-squat checklist — the standard every weighted squat is built on.

Words to know (plain English)

Air squat
The unladen, body-weight-only squat; when CrossFit says 'squat' with no qualifier, this is it. All athletes start here and master it before loading.
Back squat / Front squat / Overhead squat
The named weighted squats — bar on the back, bar racked on the chest/shoulders, and bar locked out overhead, respectively. Loading any of these before the air squat is mastered hurts potential and safety.
Hip extension
Opening the hip joint (driving the hips from a bent to a straight position) — the foundation of all good human movement and the athletic essence of the squat. Powerful, controlled hip extension is necessary and nearly sufficient for elite athleticism.
Breaking parallel
Descending until the fold/crease of the hip drops below the top of the knee — the depth standard for a full squat.
Lumbar curve / lumbar extension
The natural arch of the lower back. Keeping it (not letting it round into lumbar flexion) is critical; surrendering it at the bottom is the worst squat fault.
Points of performance
The specific technique standards that define a correct rep; a squat is 'mastered' only when none of them are deficient.
Neuroendocrine response
The hormonal/nervous-system response a hard, full-body movement like the squat triggers — a benefit that on its own justifies including the movement.
Tabata squat (mastery standard)
Eight intervals of 20 seconds of squats / 10 seconds rest; the squat is considered mastered when the weakest of the eight intervals is 18-20 perfect reps.
Quad dominance / leg-extension dominance
Driving the squat mostly with the quadriceps (knee extension) instead of the hips — a leading obstacle to elite performance and a cause of a bad squat.
Bar Holds
A fault-fixing drill: settle into a perfect bottom while holding a bar racked higher and closer than normal, then let go and repeat higher/closer — 'lifts' the squat and loads the heels and glute/hams.
Box Squatting
Squatting to a ~10-inch box, resting at the bottom without losing posture, then squeezing up without rocking forward; a technique perfected at the Westside Barbell Club.
Bottom-to-Bottoms
Spending most of the time in the bottom of the squat and rising to full extension only briefly before returning — teaches the athlete to be comfortable and strong at the bottom.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Accepting a doctor's, chiropractor's, or trainer's blanket 'don't squat' or 'never bend past 90 degrees' advice — it's almost always misinformed and contradicts how humans stand up off the floor.
  • Adding weight (back, front, or overhead squat) before the air squat is mastered — it retards athletic potential and compromises safety and efficacy.
  • Rounding the lower back (losing lumbar extension) at the bottom — the worst fault of all and the one to hunt for first.
  • Letting the knees roll inside the line of the foot or the heels lift off the ground — both are common, correctable faults.
  • Not breaking parallel — shallow depth from laziness or quad dominance (distinct from an acceptable weak-but-neutral beginner squat).
  • Treating a weak/upright beginner squat as a fault when the lumbar spine is actually neutral — that's weakness to develop, not a technique error to scold.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new athlete is squatting with a rounded lower back at the bottom but otherwise hits depth. What should the coach prioritize fixing?

  1. Add weight so the back is forced to stay tight
  2. Restrict the depth so the back can't round
  3. Tell them to squat faster to power through it
  4. Nothing — depth is the only thing that matters
  5. The rounded lower back (loss of lumbar extension) — it's the worst fault of all
Show answer

Answer: E — The rounded lower back (loss of lumbar extension) — it's the worst fault of all. Losing lumbar extension (rounding) is identified as the worst fault — fix it first, often with bar holds, overhead squats, and addressing tight hamstrings. D ignores a dangerous fault; A loads a broken position; C speeds up a faulty pattern; B sacrifices full range of motion when the real fix is teaching a neutral spine.

Scenario 2. An athlete's knees keep caving inward (rolling inside the feet) during air squats. Per the troubleshooting table, what's the best corrective cue/drill?

  1. Add a heavy back squat to build leg strength
  2. Push the feet to the outside of the shoe and deliberately abduct — 'stretch the floor apart' under the feet
  3. Squat to a higher box to avoid the bottom
  4. Look down at the knees to watch them
  5. Narrow the stance so the knees can't travel
Show answer

Answer: B — Push the feet to the outside of the shoe and deliberately abduct — 'stretch the floor apart' under the feet. Rolling knees come from weak adductors/abductors and quad cheating; the table's therapy is pushing the feet to the outside of the shoe and actively abducting (trying to spread the floor). A loads a faulty position; E masks rather than fixes; D violates the head-up cue; C avoids the problem instead of correcting it.

Scenario 3. A member who's been doing solid air squats asks when she can start adding a barbell to her squats. What standard does the guide give?

  1. When technique AND performance are superior — e.g., a Tabata squat with the weakest of eight intervals at 18-20 perfect reps and no deficient points of performance
  2. Only after she can deadlift bodyweight
  3. As soon as she can hit parallel once
  4. After her first month of CrossFit, regardless of technique
  5. When she asks for it — readiness is psychological
Show answer

Answer: A — When technique AND performance are superior — e.g., a Tabata squat with the weakest of eight intervals at 18-20 perfect reps and no deficient points of performance. Mastery means superior technique and performance — no deficient points of performance and fast multiple reps, with the Tabata squat (weakest set 18-20 perfect reps) as the favored benchmark. D, C, E, and B aren't the stated standard, and loading before mastery hurts potential and safety.

Scenario 4. An athlete can't keep the dowel from drifting forward in the overhead squat and his shoulders feel tight. What does the squat clinic suggest this tool/drill is good for?

  1. He should stop overhead work entirely until he can bench more
  2. He should immediately load a barbell to force the position
  3. It's only a finisher and has no diagnostic value
  4. Forward drift is fine as long as he hits depth
  5. The overhead squat is a quick punisher of sloppy technique and gives an instant diagnosis of shoulder tightness; it also 'lifts' the squat
Show answer

Answer: E — The overhead squat is a quick punisher of sloppy technique and gives an instant diagnosis of shoulder tightness; it also 'lifts' the squat. The overhead squat is described as a quick punisher of sloppy technique that instantly diagnoses tight shoulders and lifts the squat — making it a therapy as well as a movement. C and D dismiss real faults; B violates learning the move with a dowel before load; A is unrelated to the actual fix.

L1 · Section 16 of 27 Tier: ___

The Overhead Squat — The Ultimate Core Movement and Heart of the Snatch

Open the official guide

How to teach the overhead squat step by step with a dowel and the frontal plane, and why it's the truest test of midline stability you have.

Key ideas to understand

  • The overhead squat is the ultimate core exercise, the heart of the snatch, and peerless for developing effective athletic movement.
  • It trains efficient transfer of energy from large to small body parts — the essence of sport movement — which makes it indispensable for developing speed and power.
  • It demands and develops functional flexibility, and it develops the squat itself by amplifying and cruelly punishing any fault in squat posture, movement, and stability.
  • What the clean and snatch are to power, the overhead squat is to midline control, stability, and balance — unsurpassed.
  • It's deceptively simple yet universally troublesome for beginners. Three common obstacles: (1) the scarcity of skilled instruction — outside the weightlifting community most overhead-squat instruction is flat wrong; (2) a weak squat — you need a rock-solid squat first; and (3) starting with too much weight — you cannot learn it with a bar. Use a dowel or PVC pipe; anything over 5 lb. will leave your overhead squat 'stillborn.' Even a 15-lb. training bar is way too heavy to learn it.
  • There's a seven-step learning progression. Step 1: only start when you have a strong squat and use a dowel/PVC — you should be able to hold a rock-bottom squat (back arched, head and eyes forward, weight predominantly on the heels) for several minutes as a prerequisite.
  • Step 2: learn locked-arm 'dislocates' / 'pass-throughs' with the dowel — move it nearly 360 degrees from in front of the body to behind it without bending the arms. Start with a wide grip and bring the hands in until the pass-through is a moderate shoulder stretch — that's your training grip.
  • Step 3: perform the pass-through at the top, the bottom, and everywhere in between while descending — stop at several points on the way down, hold, and slowly swing the dowel front to back with locked arms.
  • Step 4: learn to find the frontal plane with the dowel from every position — practice with eyes closed to build a keen sense of where the frontal plane is, bringing the dowel to a stop in the plane each time. Have a partner verify.
  • Step 5: start the rep standing tall with the dowel held as high as possible in the frontal plane — directly overhead, NOT behind you and definitely not in front.
  • Step 6: lower very slowly to the bottom keeping the dowel in the frontal plane the whole way. A partner watches from the side; moving slightly BEHIND the frontal plane is acceptable, FORWARD is dead wrong. If you can't keep the dowel back, the grip may be too narrow — you must pull it back deliberately as you descend, especially if the chest comes forward.
  • Step 7: practice regularly and add load in tiny increments — 2.5-lb. plate, then 5, then 5+2.5, then 10, then a 15-lb. bar — but only with perfect form. There's no benefit to adding weight if the dowel/bar can't stay in the frontal plane.
  • With practice you can bring the hands closer together and still keep the bar in the frontal plane — eventually descending to a rock-bottom squat with feet together and hands together. That's a superb warm-up/cool-down drill and stretch.
  • The overhead squat builds core control by punishing any forward wobble with an enormous, instant increase in the moment about the hip and back. Held perfectly overhead and still (nearly impossible), it doesn't add load on the hip or back — but moving too fast, on the wrong line of action, or wiggling brings even light loads down 'like a house of cards.'
  • You have exactly two safe bailout options: dump the load forward and step/fall backward, or dump it backward and step/fall forward. Both are safe and easy. Lateral escapes are NOT an option.
  • The gap between your overhead squat and your back/front squat is a solid measure of your midline stability, control, and squatting precision — and developing the overhead squat fixes faults that aren't even visible in the back and front squat.
  • Use the numbers: an average of your max back and front squat measures core, hip, and leg strength; your max overhead squat measures core stability/control and your ability to generate effective, efficient athletic power. The overhead squat will always be a fraction of the back/front average, but over time they should CONVERGE.
  • If they diverge, you're building hip and core strength but losing the ability to apply power distally (to the extremities) — and you may be injury-prone in athletic pursuits. If they converge, you're building useful strength and power that transfers to athletic movement.
  • The real-world utility: many objects are high enough to get under but too heavy or not free enough to be jerked or pressed overhead — you can still elevate them by lowering your hips until the arms lock out, then squatting up.
  • The angle diagram (Figure 8) explains the mechanics: 'A' is the torso's angle of inclination above horizontal — as a squat matures this angle increases and the squat becomes more upright as posterior-chain strength and neural 'connectedness' grow; low angles cantilever onto the quads off a weak posterior chain (mechanically disadvantaged under load). '90-A' is the arm rotation past overhead needed to keep the bar in the frontal plane — the lower A, the more rotation and the wider the grip required. Posterior-chain strength ultimately determines grip width, squat elevation, and shoulder rotation. Maturity and quality of the squat determine ALL the mechanics of the overhead squat.
  • Bar positions in the diagram: position 'a' is the overhead squat (with perfect stability it adds no moment about the hip/back); 'b' is roughly the back/front squat; 'c' has the load behind the frontal plane and can actually DECREASE the moment as long as balance holds; 'd' is in front of the plane — a fatal flaw, where even slight movement greatly increases the moment and can collapse the squat like a house of cards. The frontal plane ('f') divides front half from back half, and the goal is to keep the load in it.

Words to know (plain English)

Overhead squat
A full squat performed with the load locked out overhead in the frontal plane; the ultimate core/midline movement and the foundation of the snatch.
Frontal plane
The vertical plane dividing the body's front half from its back half; the line the athlete keeps the load in throughout the overhead squat. Behind it can be acceptable; in front of it is a fatal flaw.
Dislocates / pass-throughs
A mobility drill moving a dowel in a wide, locked-arm arc from in front of the body to behind it; used to find shoulder range and set the overhead-squat training grip width.
Training grip
The hand spacing at which a pass-through produces a moderate shoulder stretch — the grip width used to learn and perform the overhead squat.
Midline stabilization / midline control
Keeping the spine and torso rigid and aligned under load; the overhead squat is the premier test and developer of it.
Moment (about the hip and back)
The rotational/torque load on the hip and back; forward drift of the bar instantly and enormously increases it, collapsing the squat 'like a house of cards.'
Line of action
The path the load travels; moving on the wrong line (or too fast, or wiggling) brings even light loads down in the overhead squat.
Distal application of power
Transferring force out to the small/far body parts (the extremities); convergence of the overhead squat with the back/front squat shows this ability is developing.
Posterior chain
The muscles on the back of the body (glutes, hamstrings, back); its strength and neural 'connectedness' determine the squat's uprightness, grip width, and shoulder rotation in the overhead squat.
Angle of inclination (A)
The torso's angle above horizontal in the squat; it increases (becomes more upright) as posterior-chain strength grows. Low angles cantilever onto the quads and are mechanically disadvantaged under load.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Trying to learn the overhead squat with a barbell — even a 15-lb. training bar is too heavy; learn it with a dowel/PVC (nothing over 5 lb.).
  • Attempting the overhead squat without a rock-solid air squat first — a weak squat is one of the three main obstacles.
  • Letting the bar drift FORWARD of the frontal plane — that's the fatal flaw ('d') that collapses the squat; behind the plane is acceptable.
  • Bailing out of a failed overhead squat sideways — only forward-step-back or backward-step-forward are safe; lateral escapes are not an option.
  • Adding load while the dowel/bar can't be kept in the frontal plane — there's no benefit, and it ingrains a dangerous line of action.
  • Ignoring a diverging overhead-squat-to-back/front-squat ratio — it signals strength that can't be applied distally and an athlete who may be injury-prone.

  1. Have him bail sideways when he loses it
  2. Drop to a dowel/PVC, fix the descent so the bar stays in (or slightly behind) the frontal plane, and possibly widen the grip — forward drift is the fatal flaw
  3. Let him keep drifting forward as long as he hits depth
  4. Move straight to a heavy snatch to build the position
  5. Add weight so he's forced to stabilize harder

Learn the overhead squat with a dowel (a 15-lb. bar is too heavy), keep the bar in or slightly behind the frontal plane, and widen the grip if he can't pull it back — forward of the plane is dead wrong. E loads a broken line; C accepts the fatal flaw; A teaches an unsafe (lateral) bailout; D skips the foundation.

L1 · Section 17 of 27 Tier: ___

Shoulder Press, Push Press, Push Jerk: The Overhead Progression

Open the official guide

How the three-lift pressing progression builds the power zone and core-to-extremity recruitment, what each lift adds, and the muted-hip fault to hunt down and destroy.

Key ideas to understand

  • The progression runs shoulder press to push press to push jerk, a longtime CrossFit staple. As you move up the chain, each lift becomes more athletic, more functional, and suited to heavier loads, while leaning more and more on the hip.
  • Why these lifts matter: they teach essential motor recruitment patterns found in sport and life, and they massively build strength in the upper body and the 'power zone.' For power-zone and functional recruitment, the push press and push jerk have no peer, even the bench press (the 'king' of upper-body lifts) can't touch them.
  • The big concept they teach is core-to-extremity recruitment: good athletic movement starts at the core and radiates out to the extremities. As you climb the progression, this concept gets learned and reinforced. This alone would justify training these lifts.
  • Why core-to-extremity is everything: the most common errors in punching, jumping, throwing, and countless athletic movements are violations of this concept. Because movement begins at the core, core strength is absolutely essential to athletic success.
  • The 'power zone' is the region of the body these movements emanate from, the core. Its muscle groups are the hip flexors, hip extensors (glutes and hams), spinal erectors, and quadriceps.
  • The push press and jerk also train power and speed, which are 'king' in sport. Power and speed come from coupling force with velocity, and these lifts are performed explosively, that's the hallmark of speed and power training. Many great lifts (like a slow grind) lack this explosive quality.
  • These lifts are the perfect tool to expose and destroy the muted hip, a postural/mechanical fault where the pelvis 'chases' the leg during hip flexion. It plagues more athletes than not, and a push press under heavy stress conjures it up so you can eliminate it.
  • SHOULDER PRESS setup: take the bar from supports or clean it to the rack; bar on the shoulders, grip slightly wider than shoulder width, elbows below and in front of the bar, stance about hip width. Press: drive the bar to directly overhead, and the head must move out of the way to let the bar pass (the head 'accommodates' the bar).
  • PUSH PRESS setup is the same as the shoulder press. Then DIP: bend the hips and knees while keeping the torso upright, only a couple of inches. DRIVE: with NO pause at the bottom of the dip, forcefully extend the hips and legs. PRESS: as the hips and legs finish extending, the shoulders and arms forcefully press the bar overhead to full extension.
  • PUSH JERK setup, dip, and drive are identical to the push press. The difference is the PRESS UNDER: instead of just pressing, you press AND dip a second time at the same moment, catching the bar in a partial squat with arms fully locked out overhead. FINISH: stand fully erect with the bar overhead, same finish as the push press and shoulder press.
  • The role of the abs in overhead lifts: the abdominals' primary athletic job is midline stabilization, NOT trunk flexion. That stabilizing role is never more critical than when driving loads overhead, and the heavier the load, the more critical it gets. Train every exercise as an ab exercise, but in overhead lifts it's essential.
  • How to spot weak ab engagement overhead: the body arches so the hips, pelvis, and stomach push ahead of the bar. Constant vigilance is needed from every lifter to prevent and correct this postural deformation.
  • Summary of the hip's growing role: in the shoulder press the power zone is used for stabilization only. In the push press it provides both stability AND the primary impetus in the dip and drive. In the push jerk it's called on for the dip, drive, second dip, and squat. The hip's role increases at every step.
  • The payoff in load: the push press lets you drive as much as 30% more overhead than the shoulder press, and the push jerk lets you drive as much as 30% more than the push press. The hip is increasingly recruited to help the arms and shoulders raise the load.
  • As you master the push jerk it will unconsciously displace the push press as your go-to for going overhead. And as you master technique and add load, the second dip gets lower and lower, until at very heavy loads the upper body contributes only a fraction and an increasing share of the lift is done by the overhead squat (the catch becomes very low).
  • On both the push press and jerk the 'dip' is critical to the whole movement: the stomach is held very tightly and the turnaround from dip to drive is sudden, explosive, and violent.
  • TRY THESE practice protocols: (1) start at 95 lb and push press or push jerk 15 straight reps, rest 30 sec, repeat for 5 sets of 15; go up in weight only when all 5 sets are done with just 30 sec rest and no pause in any set. (2) Rep 1 shoulder press, Rep 2 push press, Rep 3 push jerk; repeat until the shoulder press is impossible, then continue until the push press is impossible, then five more push jerks; start at 95 lb and add load only when total reps exceed 30.

What you should get from it

The progression is a deliberate teaching ladder; each step is more athletic and leans harder on the hip.

What you should get from it

These lifts beat the 'king' bench press where it counts for athletic recruitment, that's why they're trained.

What you should get from it

Athletic movement starts at the core and radiates out; violating this is the root of most athletic faults.

What you should get from it

The power zone is the core engine these lifts build, and it's defined by those specific muscle groups.

What you should get from it

The muted hip is a near-universal fault, and heavy push presses are how you find and fix it.

What you should get from it

The strict press: hip-width stance, elbows below and in front of the bar, and the head moves back so the bar travels straight overhead.

What you should get from it

The push press adds the hip drive: short vertical dip, no pause, then explosive leg-and-hip drive into the press.

What you should get from it

The push jerk adds the second dip: you drop under the bar and receive it locked out, then stand to finish.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

The dip-to-drive turnaround is the engine of both the push press and jerk; it must be tight and explosive, not soft.

What you should get from it

Concrete rep schemes for grooving the progression and earning load increases only when standards are met.

Words to know (plain English)

Shoulder press (strict press)
An overhead press with no help from the legs; the bar goes from the rack to overhead using only the arms and shoulders, with the hip stabilizing.
Push press
A press that adds a short, vertical dip and explosive hip-and-leg drive, then presses the bar to lockout; lets you move ~30% more than a strict press.
Push jerk
A push press plus a 'press under', a second dip that drops you under the bar to receive it locked out in a partial squat; lets you move ~30% more than a push press.
Power zone
The core region athletic movement emanates from, made of the hip flexors, hip extensors (glutes and hams), spinal erectors, and quadriceps.
Core-to-extremity recruitment
The principle that sound athletic movement starts at the core and radiates outward to the limbs; violating it is the root of most movement faults.
Muted hip
A fault where the pelvis 'chases' the leg during hip flexion, killing the hip drive; the push press under load is used to expose and eliminate it.
Dip / drive
The short downward bend of hips and knees (dip) and the explosive upward extension that powers the bar (drive); the turnaround between them must be sudden and tight.
Press under
The second dip in the push jerk where you drop under the bar to receive it overhead with locked arms, rather than pressing it all the way up.
Midline stabilization
The abs' primary athletic job: bracing and keeping the trunk rigid (not flexing it), which is critical to driving loads overhead.
Coupling force with velocity
Producing strength fast, the essence of power and speed; the push press and jerk train it because they're performed explosively.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • The muted hip (pelvis chasing the leg in the dip) kills the drive and is a near-universal fault, hunt it down with heavy push presses and destroy it.
  • Pausing at the bottom of the dip on a push press or jerk; the turnaround must be sudden and explosive, with no pause.
  • Arching the body so the hips, pelvis, and stomach push ahead of the bar overhead is the sign of weak midline bracing; correct it constantly, especially as load increases.
  • Treating the overhead lift as an arm-only effort instead of bracing the abs to stabilize the midline, the heavier the load, the more dangerous a soft midline becomes.
  • Adding load before meeting the standard (e.g., before all 5 sets of 15 are unbroken with only 30 sec rest) skips the strength base the protocol builds.

  3. A flexibility issue; have them stretch the shoulders
  4. A grip problem; widen the grip

  2. It allows pressing with bent arms
  3. It uses a wider grip for more leverage
  4. It is performed slower for more control

  2. The abs flex the trunk to help push the bar up

  5. The abs should relax to let the bar travel

  2. The dip becomes a pause to control the bar
  3. The second dip gets higher and the overhead squat disappears
  4. The second dip gets lower and the overhead squat does an increasing share of the lift, because the upper body can contribute only a fraction
  5. The athlete should switch back to the shoulder press

At heavy loads the catch becomes very low, the second dip deepens, and more of the lift is accomplished by the overhead squat since the upper body can only contribute a fraction. L1 · Section 18 of 27 Tier: ___

The Deadlift and Sumo Deadlift High Pull

Open the official guide

Gives a coach the 'healthlift' — the safe, sound way to pick anything off the ground — plus its full cue list, benchmarks, and the sumo deadlift high pull.

Key ideas to understand

  • The deadlift is dead simple but unrivaled for building head-to-toe strength — it's just picking a thing off the ground done right.
  • Its old name, 'the healthlift,' was the better name. Fear of the deadlift is groundless, same as fear of the squat.
  • Whatever a client wants — rev up metabolism, more strength or lean mass, less body fat, a healthier back, better sport performance, or staying independent into old age — the deadlift is a shortcut to it.
  • It belongs in the same family as standing, running, jumping, and throwing for functionality, but it pays off in athletic ability faster than anything else.
  • Because of its whole-body nature and big mechanical advantage with heavy loads, it hits hard on the hormonal (neuroendocrine) side — athletes feel the strength and the sense of power quick.
  • Want to get stronger? Drive your deadlift up. It tends to pull your other lifts up with it, especially the Olympic lifts.
  • No other exercise protects the back from the injuries of sport and life and the wear of time like the deadlift does.
  • Program it near-max about once a week, and maybe one other day at light loads for low reps. Be patient — learn to celebrate small, infrequent PRs.
  • Benchmarks: a bodyweight deadlift is 'beginning,' twice bodyweight is 'good,' three times bodyweight is 'great.'
  • Proper technique rests on three pillars: orthopedic safety, functionality, and mechanical advantage.
  • We reject stances wider than hip-to-shoulder width — a super-wide (powerlifting) stance is less functional (you can't safely walk, clean, or snatch from out there) and piles more force on the hip. Use wide stances only rarely and light.
  • Work all three grips over time — alternate, parallel, and hook — and carefully vary stance, grip width, even plate diameter, because each variant stresses the margins of the movement and builds hip capacity.
  • The setup and pull cues all drive the same thing: a flat back, weight on the heels, bar close to the legs traveling straight up and down, and arms acting only as straps.
  • Two things stay constant while the bar is below the knees: the torso's angle of inclination, and shoulders and hips rising at the same rate.
  • The five-cue short version of the deadlift: look straight ahead, keep the back arched, arms don't pull (they're just straps), bar travels along the legs, push through the heels.
  • The doctor-and-Coach conversation makes the point: even a frail, elderly client already sets a bag down and picks it back up in real life — our job is to teach her to do that essential activity safely, not to avoid it.
  • The sumo deadlift high pull (SDHP) is a separate movement: a wide sumo stance, narrow grip, pull from mid-shin, drive with hips and legs to full extension, then shrug and pull the bar under the chin.
  • On the SDHP the sequence matters — open the hip fully first, shrug, THEN pull with the arms, keeping the elbows high and above the hands.
  • The SDHP is a great conjugate to the thruster (similar range of motion, line of action, speed), and at low loads it's the favorite substitute for Concept2 rowing.
