Open the guide to this section and track these down — highlight each one, then check it off. Tap a clue to reveal what it teaches.

What you should get from it

The deadlift's whole selling point: about as simple as it gets, yet it builds strength from head to toe like nothing else.

What you should get from it

The name 'deadlift' scared people off; 'healthlift' described it better — it's a health-building movement, not a dangerous one.

What you should get from it

Almost whatever the client's goal is, the deadlift moves them toward it.

What you should get from it

Picking something off the ground is a core human movement, right alongside the most basic ones — it isn't a niche gym lift.

What you should get from it

Because it's whole-body and loads heavy, the deadlift triggers a big hormonal response — that's why people feel stronger and more powerful fast.

What you should get from it

The deadlift is the lever for general strength; raising it carries your clean, snatch, and the rest up with it.

What you should get from it

The deadlift isn't the back-wrecker people think — done right, it's the best back insurance there is.

What you should get from it

Heavy deadlifts are a once-a-week thing, and progress is slow and lumpy — small PRs are the win.

What you should get from it

A quick yardstick for where a client's deadlift stands relative to their own bodyweight.

What you should get from it

Every deadlift cue serves one of these three: protect the joints, keep it useful in life, and keep the leverage on your side.

What you should get from it

CrossFit keeps the deadlift stance roughly hip-width because that's what transfers to life and the other lifts, and it's easier on the hips.

What you should get from it

Deliberately varying grip and setup over time stresses the movement's edges and grows the hip.

What you should get from it

The complete setup-and-pull checklist — review each cue and picture the position it produces.

What you should get from it

Below the knee, the back angle doesn't change and the hips don't shoot up early — chest and hips rise together.

What you should get from it

The five things you actually shout on the floor — the teaching shorthand for a sound deadlift.

What you should get from it

Everyone already deadlifts in daily life (setting a bag down, picking it up) — the only question is whether they're taught to do it safely. That's the scope-of-practice answer for 'frail' clients.

What you should get from it

The SDHP is its own movement with a strict order: legs/hips first to full extension, shrug, then arms.

What you should get from it

On the SDHP, elbows stay high and lead the arm pull, and the bar finishes under the chin — not curled up the front.

What you should get from it

Use the SDHP when you want a thruster-like stimulus or a rowing alternative at light loads.

Words to know (plain English)

Deadlift ('the healthlift')
The movement of lifting a loaded bar (or any object) off the ground to a standing position with a flat back, hips and legs doing the work. CrossFit treats it as the safe, sound way to pick anything up — its older name 'healthlift' fits better.
Neuroendocrine response
The hormonal reaction the body has to hard training. Big, whole-body lifts with heavy loads (like the deadlift) trigger a strong one, which drives strength and adaptation.
Orthopedic safety
Protecting the joints, spine, and connective tissue. One of the three pillars guiding deadlift technique — keep the load off the structures that get hurt.
Mechanical advantage
Setting up the body so leverage works for you instead of against you (bar close, back flat, weight on heels), letting you safely move bigger loads. The third pillar of proper deadlift technique.
Angle of inclination (torso angle)
How far the torso is leaned forward relative to the ground. In the deadlift it stays constant while the bar is below the knees — the back doesn't change angle as you start the pull.
Alternate / parallel / hook grip
Three ways to hold the bar. Parallel (both palms facing you / pronated), alternate (one palm forward, one back, the 'mixed' grip), and hook (thumb trapped under the fingers). Coaches rotate all three to build grip and stress the movement's margins.
Sumo deadlift high pull (SDHP)
A pulling movement from a wide ('sumo') stance with a narrow grip: drive the bar up with hips and legs to full extension, shrug, then pull with the arms (elbows high) to bring the bar under the chin.
Conjugate
A movement that closely matches another in range of motion, line of action, and speed, so it trains a similar quality. The SDHP is a conjugate to the thruster.
Line of action
The path the load travels through space during a movement. The SDHP shares a similar line of action with the thruster, which is why they pair well.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Arms pulling the bar instead of acting as straps — the arms stay locked; hips and legs do the lifting.
  • Letting the back round or lose its arch — the #1 way the deadlift earns its scary reputation; keep the chest up and back flat.
  • Hips shooting up faster than the shoulders off the floor — they must rise at the same rate while the bar is below the knees.
  • Weight drifting onto the toes or the bar drifting away from the legs — keep weight on the heels and the bar tracking straight up and down close to the body.
  • Defaulting to a super-wide (powerlifting) stance for general training — it's less functional and loads the hips more; keep stance hip-to-shoulder width.
  • Telling 'frail' or elderly clients to simply avoid the deadlift — they already pick things up in daily life; scope of practice is to teach the safe pattern, not forbid it (and refer out for genuine medical concerns).
  • On the SDHP, pulling with the arms early or curling the bar before the hips and legs reach full extension — open the hip and shrug first, then pull.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A new member says her doctor told her she shouldn't deadlift because she's older and worried about her back. What's the best coaching response?

  1. Put her in a super-wide powerlifting stance because it's safer for the back.
  2. Tell her the doctor is wrong and load her up to a bodyweight deadlift today.
  3. Explain that she already picks things off the ground daily, teach her the safe deadlift pattern with light load, and have her clear any specific medical concerns with her doctor.
  4. Replace every deadlift with a machine leg press so she never bends over.
  5. Drop the deadlift entirely from her program and never revisit it.
Show answer

Answer: C — Explain that she already picks things off the ground daily, teach her the safe deadlift pattern with light load, and have her clear any specific medical concerns with her doctor.. C matches the doctor-and-Coach conversation: deadlifting is essential daily activity, so we teach it safely rather than avoiding it — while respecting genuine medical clearance. B ignores load progression and medical caution; E abandons a movement that protects the back; D removes the functional pattern she needs; A is the less-functional, higher-hip-force stance the guide explicitly de-emphasizes.

Scenario 2. Watching a deadlift, you see the lifter's hips shoot up first and the bar swing out away from the shins. Which correction targets the root fault best?

  1. Tell him to look up at the ceiling and pull faster.
  2. Widen his stance to a sumo position.
  3. Cue 'shoulders and hips rise together, weight on your heels, keep the bar against your legs.'
  4. Add more weight so he's forced to stay tight.
  5. Tell him to bend his arms to keep the bar close.
Show answer

Answer: C — Cue 'shoulders and hips rise together, weight on your heels, keep the bar against your legs.'. C restores the two constants below the knee — hips and shoulders rising at the same rate and the bar tracking close over the heels. A risks neck position and doesn't fix the hips; B changes the movement instead of correcting it; D loads a broken pattern; E violates the 'arms are just straps' cue and rounds mechanics further.

Scenario 3. You want a thruster-like stimulus in a workout but the only thing free is a barbell, and you'd like a low-load alternative to the rower. Which movement fits best?

  1. Bench press.
  2. Back squat.
  3. Strict shoulder press.
  4. Sumo deadlift high pull at a light load.
  5. Conventional max-effort deadlift singles.
Show answer

Answer: D — Sumo deadlift high pull at a light load.. D is correct — the guide calls the SDHP a great conjugate to the thruster and the favorite substitute for Concept2 rowing at low loads. E is a slow, heavy strength movement, not a metabolic thruster stand-in; C, B, and A don't share the thruster's range of motion, line of action, or speed.

L1 · Section 19 of 27 Tier: ___

Medicine-Ball Cleans

Open the official guide

Gives a coach the trick for teaching everyone the clean fast and safely — using a friendly Dynamax ball to kill the fear of the Olympic lifts and weed out faults in warm-ups.

Key ideas to understand

  • The Olympic lifts — clean and jerk and snatch — are the toughest learning challenge in all of weight training; outside them there are no truly complex movements in the weight room.
  • By comparison, the average collegiate gymnast has learned hundreds of movements at least as complex as the clean or snatch — so 'too complex to learn' doesn't hold up; weight training is just usually too simple to prepare people for them.
  • Many coaches avoid the quick lifts because of their technical complexity, but that complexity is exactly the source of their value: the lifts simultaneously demand AND develop strength, power, speed, flexibility, coordination, agility, balance, and accuracy.
  • The people knocking the Olympic lifts usually have no real first-hand experience with them — nobody who actually knows the lifts can articulate why they'd be dangerous or off-limits for any population.
  • At CrossFit, everyone learns the Olympic lifts — everyone.
  • The Dynamax medicine ball is the teaching tool: a soft, large, pillowy ball, 4 to 30 lb in increments, nonthreatening and even friendly — which is the whole point.
  • The teaching sequence with the ball: introduce the deadlift starting position and posture, then the deadlift itself, then shift to front squatting with the ball, then to the clean.
  • Reduced to its essence, the clean becomes 'pop the hip and drop — catch it in a squat,' and the group is cleaning in about five minutes. It's a legitimate, functional clean.
  • The med-ball clean may actually have clearer real-life application than the bar clean — it's the same pattern as heaving a bag of cement into a truck or hoisting a toddler into a car seat.
  • Every fault common to lifting beginners shows up just as plainly with the ball as with the bar, so nothing important is lost by starting here.
  • The standard faults to hunt: heels up in the first pull, back rounded, head down, arms bent, pulling too high, no hip extension, no shrug, curling the ball, and a low/slow catch with elbows down.
  • The corrected starting position is heels down, head up, back arched; the catch correction is elbows high.
  • The general clean corrections: arms locked, full extension, shrug, pull the body down under the ball, ball kept close to the body, and catch with elbows high.
  • Load scales the stimulus inside one class: in a mixed group, newbies get light balls and veterans get heavy ones; whoever holds the 30-lb ball gets a real workout no matter their ability.
  • A heavy med-ball clean hits harder than the same weight in a bar or dumbbell, because of the extra effort to adduct the arms and 'pinch' the ball so it doesn't slip.
  • Use the med-ball clean in warm-ups and cool-downs to reinforce the movement — the payoff shows up as more and faster PRs in the bar clean, even for the better lifters. The benefit transfers to the bar.
  • A warm-up is full of chances to weed out bad mechanics — pulling with the arms, not finishing hip extension, no shrug, pulling too high, lifting the heels, curling, losing back extension, looking down, catching high then squatting, slow drop, slow elbows — they're all on display.
  • The 'follow the leader' drill: put athletes in a small circle, put the best clean in the center as the model, and have everyone mirror it in real time; out-of-sync bodies make screw-ups obvious.
  • Mirroring kills 'paralysis through analysis' — thinking becomes doing. Athletes who ignore verbal cues become self-correcting by watching and comparing to others, and they even shout corrections to each other across the circle.
  • Turning it into a child's game of 'follow the leader' cuts coaching cues down to the bare minimum — the model does the teaching.

What you should get from it

The Olympic lifts are genuinely hard to learn — that's the honest starting point, not a reason to skip them.

What you should get from it

'Too complex to learn' is a weak excuse — people learn far more complex movements all the time; weight training just usually doesn't ask them to.

What you should get from it

The very thing that makes the quick lifts hard is what makes them valuable — they train nearly every physical skill at once.

What you should get from it

The Olympic lifts are not reserved for elite athletes — they're for every client, scaled appropriately.

What you should get from it

The whole teaching trick is the friendly tool: a soft ball removes the fear a loaded barbell creates.

What you should get from it

The clean is built up from pieces the group already learned — deadlift, then front squat, then put them together.

What you should get from it

Stripped down, the clean is just an explosive hip and a fast drop into a squat to catch — simple enough to teach in minutes.

What you should get from it

The med-ball clean is arguably MORE functional than the bar clean — it mirrors how we actually lift awkward objects to the chest in life.

What you should get from it

This is your fault-spotting checklist for the clean — the same faults appear with the ball and with the bar.

What you should get from it

Two anchor fixes: a solid setup (heels down, head up, arched) and a high-elbow catch.

What you should get from it

The standard correction bundle for a sloppy clean: lock the arms, finish extension, shrug, pull yourself under, keep it close.

What you should get from it

One drill scales to everyone in the room just by handing out different ball weights.

What you should get from it

A heavy med-ball clean is harder than the same weight on a bar because you're also squeezing the arms in to hold the ball.

What you should get from it

Reinforcing the pattern with the ball in warm-ups/cool-downs raises everyone's bar clean — including advanced lifters.

What you should get from it

A mirroring circle teaches the clean faster than talking — bad reps stick out visually and athletes self-correct.

What you should get from it

Over-explaining freezes people; mirroring a good model turns thought straight into action.

What you should get from it

Some athletes won't respond to your words but will fix themselves the second they see they're out of sync with the group.

Words to know (plain English)

Olympic lifts (quick lifts)
The clean and jerk and the snatch — the two competitive weightlifting movements that explosively move a load from the ground to overhead. CrossFit teaches them to everyone because they build power, speed, and coordination.
Clean
Explosively moving a load from the ground to the front of the shoulders, finishing by dropping under it into a front squat to catch. Reduced to 'pop the hip and drop — catch it in a squat.'
Dynamax medicine ball
A soft, large, pillowy weighted ball ranging from 4 to 30 lb in increments. Its nonthreatening feel makes it the ideal tool for teaching the clean and removing fear of the Olympic lifts.
Hip extension ('popping the hip')
Fully and forcefully opening the hips from bent to straight — the power source of the clean. 'No hip extension' or not finishing it is a top fault.
Shrug
Elevating the shoulders to continue the upward drive of the load after the hips open. A missing shrug is a common clean fault.
Catch (receiving position)
Where you receive the load at the shoulders after pulling under it. The fault is a low, slow catch; the correction is to catch with the elbows high.
Adduction (pinching the ball)
Squeezing the arms inward toward the body. With a heavy med-ball you must adduct to 'pinch' the ball so it doesn't slip, which makes it harder than the same weight on a bar.
Paralysis through analysis
When too much thinking or over-coaching freezes an athlete so they can't move well. The mirroring drill avoids it by turning thinking into doing.
Follow-the-leader (mirroring) drill
A teaching method: athletes form a circle around the best mover and copy them in real time. Out-of-sync bodies make faults obvious and athletes self-correct without much verbal coaching.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Avoiding the Olympic lifts because they're 'too technical,' 'dangerous,' or 'bad for the joints' — the guide rejects this; people learn far more complex movements, and critics typically have no real experience with the lifts.
  • Curling the ball (or bar) up with the arms instead of driving with the hips — arms stay locked until the pull under; the power comes from hip extension and the shrug.
  • Pulling too high or catching high then squatting down to it — pull the body DOWN under the load and catch in the squat with elbows high.
  • Lifting the heels in the first pull, rounding the back, or looking down at the start — corrected starting position is heels down, head up, back arched.
  • Over-coaching with a flood of verbal cues — it causes 'paralysis through analysis'; cut cues to the minimum and let a good model do the teaching.
  • Handing everyone the same ball weight in a mixed group — scale by ball weight so beginners and veterans both get the right stimulus.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. You have a brand-new group that's nervous about barbell cleans. What's the fastest, safest way to get them doing a legitimate clean?

  1. Put empty barbells in their hands and drill the full bar clean for 30 minutes.
  2. Use Dynamax balls: teach the deadlift posture, then the deadlift, then front squat, then 'pop the hip and drop — catch it in a squat.'
  3. Skip the clean entirely until they've trained for six months.
  4. Show a video of an elite lifter and have them copy it from memory.
  5. Start them with max-weight med-ball cleans to build confidence.
Show answer

Answer: B — Use Dynamax balls: teach the deadlift posture, then the deadlift, then front squat, then 'pop the hip and drop — catch it in a squat.'. B is the guide's exact progression with the friendly ball — it gets a group cleaning legitimately in about five minutes while removing fear. A intimidates beginners and slows learning; C needlessly withholds a foundational lift; D gives no real-time feedback; E loads a movement they haven't learned yet.

Scenario 2. During a med-ball clean warm-up, an athlete keeps curling the ball up with bent arms and never fully opens his hips. Which correction set fits the fault?

  1. 'Catch lower and slower so you feel the bottom.'
  2. 'Use a heavier ball so you can't curl it.'
  3. 'Arms locked, full extension, shrug, then pull the body down and keep the ball close.'
  4. 'Look down at the ball to track it better.'
  5. 'Widen your stance into a sumo position.'

Answer: C — 'Arms locked, full extension, shrug, then pull the body down and keep the ball close.'. C is the guide's correction bundle for exactly these faults — lock the arms, finish hip extension, shrug, and pull under while keeping the ball close. B loads a broken pattern; A and D are themselves listed faults (slow catch, looking down); E changes the movement instead of fixing the pull.

Scenario 3. You're coaching a mixed group — some seasoned, some brand-new — and want one med-ball clean drill that challenges everyone appropriately. Best approach?

  1. Split them into separate rooms and coach them one at a time.
  2. Drop the med ball and have them all do barbell cleans instead.
  3. Give everyone a 30-lb ball so it's fair.
  4. Give everyone a 4-lb ball so no one gets hurt.
  5. Hand newbies light balls and veterans heavy balls, and run a follow-the-leader circle around the best mover.

Answer: E — Hand newbies light balls and veterans heavy balls, and run a follow-the-leader circle around the best mover.. E is straight from the guide — scale by ball weight so each person gets the right stimulus, and use the mirroring circle so the group self-corrects off a good model. C overloads beginners; D under-challenges veterans; A wastes the group dynamic that makes the drill work; B reintroduces the intimidating bar this method is designed to delay.

L1 · Section 20 of 27 Tier: ___

The Glute-Ham Developer (GHD): Building a Bulletproof Midline

Open the official guide

This section teaches you why static midline control beats crunches and how to build the posterior chain and midline safely with four GHD exercises plus the AbMat sit-up.

Key ideas to understand

  • Core strength in CrossFit means one thing: midline stabilization. Picture a line that splits the spine down the middle and cuts the pelvis in half. Holding that line rigid, with no bending or deflection, is the whole game. Hold it solid and you move more efficiently and put out more power.
  • That midline matters everywhere: the deadlift, the loaded squat, the shoulder press, and every sport. Any time the torso wobbles off that line, energy leaks out the side. A swimmer who twists, a fighter throwing a punch, a cyclist, a squatter all lose power to deflection.
  • Most people are 'frontally fixated.' They train pecs, abs, and quads but ignore the rhomboids, the erectors, and the glute-hamstrings on the back side. The catch: the real horsepower for punching, jumping, throwing, and running comes from the posterior, not the front.
  • Trying to keep the hip flexors out of training is a mistake. By their attachment points and leverage, the hip flexors have several times the contraction capacity of the abs. Midline stabilization needs all of it: hip extensors, hip flexors, trunk flexors, and trunk extensors. The abs are only one piece.
  • Midline work is static control. You're trying NOT to let the spine-to-pelvis relationship move. Yet most common 'core' moves are dynamic, like the crunch, which is a deliberate bending of the trunk. When you deadlift, you deliberately hold that relationship locked.
  • Lots of training communities do almost no hip extension, have almost no awareness of the spine-to-pelvis relationship, and limit hip flexion on purpose. The only 'core' work they do is dynamic trunk flexion (crunches). Many of those same communities have chronic back-injury problems, which is no coincidence. Run, pull-up, sit-up, push-up on repeat has no real core movement in it. The crunch does not count.
  • The GHD is a bulky, space-hungry machine, but it's essential. CrossFit Santa Cruz ran four of them in 2,500 square feet. It's used for four exercises that build awareness and capacity at midline stabilization.
  • The punchline: static contractions that stabilize the midsection are the most important and most powerful muscular contractions in that region. The L-sit, overhead squat, and deadlift get you somewhere no pile of crunches ever will.
  • Why static can be so much stronger than dynamic: the guess is that if you could fire the abs dynamically with the same force you can hold them statically, you'd be able to break your own back at will. We're hardwired so we can't, which is protective.
  • The four GHD movements are taught in a strict order, and the client earns each one by showing capacity in the one before it (about 25-30 clean consecutive reps).
  • Movement 1, GHD hip extension: hinge at the hip ONLY, keeping the distance from xiphoid process (bottom of the breastbone) to pubic bone fixed. No trunk flexion, no shortening of the trunk. The erectors work statically to hold the line; the glutes and hamstrings do the moving (concentric and eccentric). Set the pad so the femur is on the pad and the pelvis is free. Trap the pelvis and the athlete can't hold the lumbar curve.
  • GHD hip extension is exceptionally safe and genuinely rehabilitative for the lower back. Even people with near-acute low-back issues can do it as long as there's no trunk flexion. Hit 25-30 reps without momentum and they'll usually feel real relief. It's a milder dose to the low back than even a moderate deadlift. Air squat plus a light deadlift plus this movement makes a great starting point for any client, any age.
  • Movement 2, GHD back extension: adjust the pad UNDER the pelvis. Now the athlete deliberately gives up the lumbar curve, flexing and extending the trunk. The erectors work dynamically; the glutes and hamstrings now hold static (isometric). Controlled reps, no bouncing or flopping, done unloaded at first. Earn it with 25-30 clean reps.
  • Movement 3, hip-and-back extension: pad goes back to the hip-extension setting. From the bottom (spine extended, hip fully flexed), the pelvis lifts first, then a wave of contraction rolls 'south to north' from lumbar all the way to the cervical spine, finishing with a rhomboid pull-back at the top. It starts with a powerful, dynamic glute-hamstring drive that extends the hip.
  • The hip-and-back extension does a lot for a coach. It builds neurological awareness and lets you teach the client the vocabulary: hip flexion, trunk flexion, hip extension, trunk extension. If you can't name those and get the right response, you're nearly worthless with a client, so teach the terms early. The movement itself is a four-way 'snake' combo: hip flexion, hip extension, trunk flexion, trunk extension, all under control.
  • Movement 4, the GHD sit-up: set the pad so the pelvis is free. The athlete descends back to touch the ground, then comes back to seated. It involves NO trunk flexion. Critics have called it 'pure hip flexion, no abs in that.' What the abs are actually doing here is midline stabilization.
  • Earn the GHD sit-up only after demonstrating the hip extension, back extension, and hip-and-back extension. Even then, start with a SHORTENED range of motion and the trainer spotting behind. That might be the whole first dose. See how they respond, then add range and volume as capacity allows.
  • How to do a GHD sit-up right: there's some leg flexion on the way down, then the leg extends dramatically to pull the athlete to seated. If the athlete does NOT extend the leg, they're relying mostly on the psoas alone, which is a weak, dysfunctional pull. Straightening the leg recruits the full hip-flexor complement, especially the rectus femoris.
  • The anatomy of why leg extension matters: the psoas runs off the femur, through the pelvis without attaching, up to the lumbar spine. The rectus femoris (the big middle piece of the quad) is a powerful partner. It doesn't reach the lumbar spine but attaches to the pelvis, a spot of huge leverage. To fully fire it, the leg has to extend hard, because the rectus femoris is both a leg extensor and a hip flexor.
  • Using only a fraction of the muscles meant to move a joint (psoas alone) is unnatural and a little irritating to the low back because of the shearing force on the lumbar spine. Done right, with the leg extending, the movement is not irritating. Teaching a client to extend the leg and use the full hip-flexor complement is how you push the point where irritation starts from 3 reps to 4 to 10 and beyond. That's rehabilitation, that's neuromuscular re-education.
  • The AbMat sit-up is the complement to the GHD sit-up: dynamic in the trunk, static in the hip. You deliberately take the hip flexors OUT and work the torso. Fat part of the AbMat toward the glutes, soles of the feet together with knees butterflied. That position makes the hip flexors tangential to the line of action so they can't do productive work. Then the athlete comes up slowly and under control by contracting the abs.
  • As the athlete fails on the AbMat, have them adduct and extend the legs a bit. That gives the hip flexors purchase and brings them back into the line of action, so the athlete can modulate the assistance and keep each rep on the midsection.
  • Why a sit-up is 'defective' without the AbMat: done slowly and deliberately, most people fail a ground sit-up. That's not weakness or neuromuscular failure, the movement is just broken without the pad. To move, you have to push against something immovable. When the rectus fully contracts, the low back goes flat, but that isn't enough to bring you to seated.
  • The lumbar spine only has a very short range of safe flexion, by design, each vertebra moves a little, and together they add up. That available range only takes you from spinally extended to neutral, and that's not enough to sit up. Fill the gap between the low back and the floor with the AbMat and now you have something to curl against, so you reach seated.
  • Without the AbMat the sit-up becomes a two-phase (biphasic) movement: the upper rectus works where there's a fulcrum and flattens the back, then it throws the load to the hip flexors, which actually pull you to seated. The result is that the full rectus gets almost no load, only motion. There's no number of ground sit-ups that will work the rectus from pubic bone to a few inches above the belly button under load. The AbMat is what loads that motion.
  • The bottom line on loading: how big would your bench get if you only pushed air? Without the pad the ab fibers shorten but there's no load and no real work done. With the pad you get the same range of motion but under load, so you actually develop strength.
  • Together the two sit-ups complement each other beautifully: GHD sit-up is dynamic in the hips and static in the trunk; AbMat sit-up is dynamic in the trunk and static in the hip. Add the L-sit (static in both the trunk and hip) and you build a formidable, complete midline.

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

What you should get from it

Bringing the legs in/out gives the hip flexors purchase to modulate assistance and keep each rep focused on the midsection.

What you should get from it

Upper rectus flattens the back where it has a fulcrum, then the hip flexors finish the sit-up; the full rectus gets motion but no load.

What you should get from it

Same range of motion with vs. without the pad, but only the loaded version (AbMat) produces real, fruitful work.

What you should get from it

GHD sit-up = dynamic hip/static trunk; AbMat = dynamic trunk/static hip; L-sit = static both; together they build a complete midline.

Words to know (plain English)

Midline stabilization
CrossFit's definition of core strength: keeping the line that splits the spine and pelvis rigid with no deflection while you move or hold a load.
Posterior chain
The muscles on the back side of the body, the glutes, hamstrings, erectors, and rhomboids, that drive jumping, throwing, running, and lifting.
Static contraction
Muscle firing to hold a position without moving the joint (like holding the spine locked in a deadlift), as opposed to a dynamic contraction that bends a joint.
Erectors (erector spinae)
The long muscles running along the spine that extend the back and, in midline work, hold the spine rigid.
Xiphoid process
The small tip at the bottom of the breastbone; keeping the distance from here to the pubic bone fixed means the trunk isn't shortening or flexing.
Lumbar curve
The natural inward arch of the lower back; holding it is the goal in hip extensions, surrendering it is deliberate in back extensions.
Concentric / eccentric
Concentric = a muscle shortening as it works (lifting up); eccentric = a muscle lengthening under control (lowering down).
GHD hip extension
The first GHD movement: hinge at the hip only, trunk stays rigid, glutes and hamstrings move you. Safe and rehabilitative for the low back.
GHD back extension
Pad set under the pelvis; the athlete deliberately flexes and extends the trunk so the erectors work dynamically and the glutes/hams hold static.
Hip-and-back extension
A combined 'snake' move: hip extends first, then a wave of trunk extension rolls up the spine from lumbar to cervical, finishing with a rhomboid pull-back.
GHD sit-up
A sit-up with no trunk flexion: descend to touch the ground, then extend the leg dramatically to pull to seated. The abs work as stabilizers.
AbMat sit-up
A trunk-flexion sit-up done over an AbMat with feet together and knees butterflied so the hip flexors are removed and the abs work under load through a real range of motion.
Psoas
A deep hip flexor that runs from the femur through the pelvis to the lumbar spine; pulling from it alone is a weak, dysfunctional, low-back-irritating way to sit up.
Rectus femoris
The big central quad muscle and a powerful hip flexor that attaches to the pelvis; fully recruited only when the leg extends hard, which makes the GHD sit-up strong and safe.
L-sit
A static hold (legs out front, body supported on the hands) that trains the midline static in both the trunk and the hip; rounds out the two sit-ups.
Tangential to the line of action
Positioned so a muscle's pull no longer lines up with the movement, so it can't contribute force, which is how the AbMat setup sidelines the hip flexors.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Trying to 'protect the back' by leaving out hip extension and full-range hip flexion. The text says these communities often have chronic back-injury problems; the missing posterior and midline work is the cause, not the cure.
  • Treating crunches as core training. Dynamic trunk flexion alone does not build the midline; the crunch does not count.
  • Letting an athlete do a GHD sit-up by pulling from the psoas alone (not extending the leg). That's a weak, unnatural pull and the shearing force irritates the lumbar spine.
  • Skipping the progression. Don't put a client on the GHD sit-up before they've shown capacity in hip extension, back extension, and hip-and-back extension; start shortened range with a spotter behind.
  • Trapping the pelvis in the hip extension. If the pelvis isn't free, the athlete can't hold the lumbar curve and the movement breaks down.
  • Bouncing or flopping through back extensions. Reps must be controlled and start unloaded.
  • Over-dosing GHD volume early. The first GHD sit-up dose may be just a few shortened reps; assess the response before adding range and volume (GHD work has a known link to overuse/low-back issues when ramped too fast).

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A brand-new client with a history of mild low-back pain wants to start training their 'core.' Which approach best fits this section?

  1. Have them do max-effort back extensions to strengthen the erectors fast
  2. Put them straight on full-range GHD sit-ups to test their capacity
  3. Begin with GHD hip extensions (no trunk flexion), an air squat, and a light deadlift as a launching point
  4. Avoid all hip extension to protect the back and stick to planks only
  5. Start with three sets of weighted crunches to build the abs first
Show answer

Answer: C — Begin with GHD hip extensions (no trunk flexion), an air squat, and a light deadlift as a launching point. The text calls hip extension exceptionally safe and rehabilitative for the low back and names air squat + light deadlift + hip extension as a great starting point for any client regardless of age. Crunches (E) don't count as real core work. Full-range GHD sit-ups (B) must be earned after the earlier progressions. Avoiding hip extension (D) is exactly the mistake the section warns against. Max-effort anything (A) is wrong for a deconditioned, painful back.

Scenario 2. An athlete on the GHD sit-up keeps complaining their low back gets 'tweaked' on every rep. What's the most likely fix from this section?

  1. Have them keep the knees bent and pull only from the hip
  2. Tell them to crunch harder at the top
  3. Add load to make the abs work harder
  4. Switch them permanently to ground sit-ups with no pad
  5. Coach them to extend the legs dramatically to recruit the full hip-flexor complement
Show answer

Answer: E — Coach them to extend the legs dramatically to recruit the full hip-flexor complement. Pulling from the psoas alone (which happens when the leg stays bent, A) creates a shearing force that irritates the lumbar spine. Extending the leg recruits the rectus femoris off its high-leverage pelvic attachment, which makes the movement strong and not irritating, and progressively pushes back the point where irritation starts. Adding load (C) or crunching (B) doesn't address the cause; a no-pad ground sit-up (D) is the 'defective' version.

Scenario 3. A coach wants an athlete to actually develop the rectus abdominis through a sit-up under load. Which setup does the section say accomplishes that?

  1. A hanging knee raise
  2. A GHD sit-up with the leg kept bent throughout
  3. A weighted Russian twist
  4. An AbMat sit-up with the fat part toward the glutes, soles together, knees butterflied
  5. A sit-up on the flat floor with hands behind the head
Show answer

Answer: D — An AbMat sit-up with the fat part toward the glutes, soles together, knees butterflied. The AbMat fills the gap under the low back so the athlete can curl against something, loading the rectus through its real range from spinally extended to neutral, with the feet-together/knees-butterflied position taking the hip flexors out. A floor sit-up (E) is the 'defective,' essentially unloaded version. The GHD sit-up (B) trains the midline statically, not the rectus dynamically under load. C and A aren't discussed as the loaded-trunk-flexion answer here.

Scenario 4. Why does this section argue that static contractions are the most important contractions for the midsection?

  1. Because crunches are more effective than the L-sit
  2. Because the body is hardwired so it can't fire the abs dynamically as hard as it can hold them statically (which would otherwise let you break your own back), so the functional, powerful job of the midline is stabilizing
  3. Because static contractions are easier for beginners
  4. Because static holds burn more calories than dynamic movements
  5. Because dynamic movements are always dangerous and should be avoided
Show answer

Answer: B — Because the body is hardwired so it can't fire the abs dynamically as hard as it can hold them statically (which would otherwise let you break your own back), so the functional, powerful job of the midline is stabilizing. The text's reasoning is that if you could crunch with the same force you can stabilize, you'd be able to break your back at will; we're protectively hardwired so we can't. That's why the midline's most powerful, functional role, seen in the L-sit, overhead squat, and deadlift, is static stabilization. The other options misstate or invert the section's claims.

L1 · Section 21 of 27 Tier: ___

Where Do I Go From Here? — The Six Coaching Abilities and How to Grow

Open the official guide

Lays out the six abilities every effective trainer needs, plus a concrete roadmap for developing as a coach and training others while you gain experience.

Key ideas to understand

  • Earning your CF-L1 is the FIRST step in training others, not the finish line. The Level 1 Course is a broad survey of the methodology and movements; this article is the roadmap for what comes after.
  • The credential basics: passing the Level 1 test earns you the CrossFit Level 1 Trainer (CF-L1) designation, usable on a resume or bio, valid five years. Keep it current by retaking the course every five years (or sooner) or by pursuing higher credentials.
  • Coaching mastery mirrors the athlete's pursuit of virtuosity — 'doing the common uncommonly well.' Master coaches have an unmatched ability to improve others' fitness, and true mastery is a lifetime commitment; great coaches never consider themselves finished.
  • An effective trainer needs capacity across SIX abilities: teaching, seeing, correcting, group/gym management, presence and attitude, and demonstration.
  • Think of the six abilities the way you think of the 10 general physical skills: balanced capacity across all of them beats lopsided excellence in one or two. The more effective the trainer, the higher the capacity in EACH. These six are the focus of the Level 2 Course.
  • Teaching = clearly articulating and teaching the mechanics of each movement. That includes hitting major points of performance before subtle ones and adjusting your instruction to the athlete's needs and capacity.
  • Good teaching reflects both your knowledge AND your ability to communicate it. You have to understand what proper mechanics are and what causes bad movement — which takes continual study across every field that intersects with fitness.
  • A great teacher relates to every student regardless of background, distills a large body of knowledge down to the one or few points that matter right now, and takes OWNERSHIP when communication breaks down. The more channels you use — verbal, visual, tactile, varied examples and analogies — the more likely the athlete succeeds.
  • Seeing = discerning good from poor mechanics and spotting faults, gross and subtle, whether the athlete is moving or static. It requires knowing WHEN and WHERE to look (e.g., trunk-to-femur for hip extension, center of pressure for posterior-chain engagement) and knowing the difference between good and bad positions. Newer coaches struggle most with spotting faults while the athlete is MOVING.
  • Correcting = facilitating better mechanics with visual, verbal, and/or tactile cues, including the ability to triage (prioritize) faults and understand how multiple faults relate. Correcting rests on four things: using successful cues, knowing multiple corrections per fault, triaging, and balancing critique with praise.
  • A 'good' cue is defined ONLY by its result — any cue that improves mechanics is good. There's no required formula or format. That said, short, specific, actionable cues ('push the hips back') tend to land best, and you need multiple strategies per fault because different clients respond differently to the same cue.
  • When several faults appear at once, attack them ONE at a time in order of importance. The order depends on the severity of the deviation and the athlete's capacity relative to the task — no single fault order works across all athletes and movements.
  • Celebrate small wins, even just hard work. Acknowledging effort — especially when it isn't immediately successful — builds rapport throughout the cueing process.
  • Seeing and correcting are exactly where newer trainers tend to be weakest. Good coaches relentlessly watch movement with a critical eye, constantly asking: How could this person be more efficient and safe? What cue would produce a better position? How do I deliver it for the best response? Develop that eye by working with great trainers and by filming yourself, other athletes, or classes.
  • Group management = organizing and managing at the micro level (within a class) and the macro level (the whole gym): managing time, organizing space, equipment, and people for good flow, and planning ahead.
  • The point of group management is to cut logistical setup and prep time so you maximize teaching and movement time — plan instruction ahead, pre-arrange equipment and weights, and don't burn the clock talking when athletes could be moving.
  • Build practice time into every class; it serves both of you. It gives you time to observe and cue, and gives the client time to work on form. Every student should feel personally coached within the group — honestly assess after each session how much time and attention each client got. The goal is to maximize your effectiveness AND your reach.
  • Presence and attitude = creating a positive, engaging learning environment with empathy and rapport. It's more intangible than the others, but clients feel its absence immediately. 'Positive' doesn't mean fake or forced — be authentic.
  • Care, empathy, and a passion for service mark trainers with strong presence. Clients perceive that you care faster than they perceive your ability to explain mechanics, anatomy, or nutrition — and it's your job to figure out how to relate to and motivate each individual toward their goals.
  • Demonstration = giving athletes an accurate visual example of the movement, and leading by example. It takes strong awareness of your own mechanics; if you're physically limited, it's fine to use someone else to demo — a coach with a good eye can quickly find a suitable model.
  • Demonstration goes beyond a single class. Leading by example means holding yourself to the same range-of-motion standards as your clients, following your own programming and nutrition advice, and modeling the supportive attitude you want to see.
  • Knowing the six qualities is simple; demonstrating them all at once in a live group is the hard part. A commitment to improving each area is the hallmark of a successful trainer at ANY level of current proficiency — coaching skill is refined across a whole career, which is how you develop virtuosity in coaching.
  • Coach Glassman's 'novice's curse': the compelling tendency among novices in any skill or art to rush past the fundamentals toward more elaborate, sophisticated techniques — the rush to originality and risk. The roadmap that follows is the antidote.
  • To DEVELOP as a trainer, keep refining your knowledge to stay ahead of your athletes. If your clients aren't testing the limits of your knowledge, you aren't doing a good enough job with them. A great coach is proud when a student exceeds them but stays ahead by growing, not by holding the athlete back.
  • Eight concrete ways to develop: (1) Teach to learn — experience in a dynamic environment is the only way to gain competency, even if your first clients are friends and family; academics (biochem, anatomy, teaching methods) support but don't replace it. (2) Watch more experienced coaches in any discipline — watch what they watch and when, listen to their cues, study their rapport. (3) Film yourself coaching to slow down the footage and self-assess against the six criteria. (4) Attend a Level 2 Course to work on seeing and correcting in front of peers and get practical, six-criteria feedback. (5) Attend additional/specialty courses — focus on WHY different methods fit different applications. (6) Read and study everything related to training, movement, and health. (7) Study and follow CrossFit.com — the archives since 2001 are a programming resource; the challenge is to follow CrossFit.com for at least six months to learn variance, volume, and scaling. (8) Pursue higher credentials — Level 2, Certified CrossFit Trainer (Level 3), Certified CrossFit Coach (Level 4, the preeminent designation).
  • CrossFit also offers online courses (e.g., Scaling, Spot the Flaw) and, under its Certification branch, courses like anatomy and physiology and best business practices — usable for continuing-education credits and open to anyone.
  • Three principles guide trainers at ALL levels while gaining expertise: master the fundamentals, limit the scope, and pursue excellence.
  • Master the fundamentals: new athletes succeed by adhering to mechanics, then consistency, then intensity — and the coach manages the timeline to intensity. Don't think new clients need complex movements and high volume to be 'sold' on you. Insist on consistently safe, correct mechanics, then very gradually add load and volume while watching for faults — this both cuts injury risk and sets up long-term success.
  • Intensity is a Goldilocks problem: too much too soon risks injury and long-term inefficiencies; too little or none blunts the adaptation that only intensity drives. When in doubt, err on the side of caution and progress slowly — even low-intensity varied functional movement helps, and it becomes clear over time when to add intensity.
  • Limit the scope: the group-class model is hard for a novice because teaching and class management steal attention from seeing and correcting. New trainers should coach friends/family in one-on-one or small groups (two or three) to perfect their correction skills before big classes, or assist a head coach. Seek internships or assistant roles, and grow class size GRADUALLY — Glassman's 'Scaling Professional Training' makes the point that the skill of making every group member feel individually coached was developed only by migrating gradually from one-on-one to groups.
  • There's a ceiling on quality output, too: even in Santa Cruz, about five appointments a day was the max before an unacceptable drop in energy, focus, and professional standards.
  • Limiting the scope also means admitting when you don't know something. Don't make up answers beyond your knowledge or scope of practice — about anatomy, back pain, or why sugar harms health. Work within your knowledge to protect clients and build credibility, build a referral network of other professionals, keep seeking answers, and ALWAYS refer any medical condition to a physician.
  • Pursue excellence: CrossFit's recommended 'business model' is the relentless pursuit of excellence — build a model focused on making the training (and the clients) better, not on chasing money. Achieve excellence and let the market bring the money to you. Cut through analysis paralysis with one question: 'Will it improve the quality of the programming or the training experience?' If yes, you're pursuing excellence.
  • Community and representation: the L1 formalizes your place in the CrossFit community and transmits its ethos — camaraderie, humility, hard work, and service. Along with affiliates, L1 Trainers are the community's most important ambassadors, and CF-L1 is the first step toward affiliation (a 13,000+ affiliate community). It's the daily work of constantly varied functional movement at high intensity plus the meat-and-vegetables/nuts-and-seeds/some-fruit/little-starch/no-sugar diet that reverses chronic disease and improves lives.
  • Use this material the right way: take what you learned, apply it slowly to others, and incrementally widen your scope over time. That continued development is what eventually leads to coaching virtuosity. Feedback is welcomed at coursefeedback@crossfit.com.

Words to know (plain English)

CrossFit Level 1 Trainer (CF-L1)
The designation earned by participating in the Level 1 Certificate Course and passing the test. Usable on a resume, bio, or business card; valid for five years.
Virtuosity (in coaching)
'Doing the common uncommonly well.' Applied to coaching, it's the mastery of improving others' fitness — pursued across a whole career and never considered finished.
The six abilities of an effective trainer
Teaching, seeing, correcting, group/gym management, presence and attitude, and demonstration. An effective trainer needs balanced capacity in all six; they are the focus of the Level 2 Course.
Teaching
Clearly articulating and teaching the mechanics of each movement — hitting major points of performance before subtle ones and adjusting instruction to the athlete's needs and capacity.
Seeing
Discerning good from poor mechanics and identifying gross and subtle faults, whether the athlete is moving or static — knowing when and where to look.
Correcting
Facilitating better mechanics with visual, verbal, and/or tactile cues, including triaging faults by importance and understanding how multiple faults relate.
Cue
A prompt (verbal, visual, or tactile) used to improve movement. A 'good' cue is defined only by its result; short, specific, actionable cues tend to work best.
Triage (of faults)
Prioritizing which fault to correct first, one at a time, based on the severity of the deviation and the athlete's capacity relative to the task — no single order works for everyone.
Group/gym management
Organizing and managing at the micro (class) and macro (gym) levels — time, space, equipment, and people — to maximize teaching and movement time.
Presence and attitude
Creating an authentic, positive, engaging learning environment built on empathy, care, and rapport — felt by clients before expertise is.
Demonstration
Providing an accurate visual example of a movement and leading by example (same ROM standards, own programming and nutrition, supportive attitude); a model may be used if the trainer is physically limited.
Novice's curse
Coach Glassman's term for the tendency to rush past the fundamentals toward more elaborate techniques — 'the rush to originality and risk.'
Progressive scaling principles for new coaches
Master the fundamentals (mechanics → consistency → intensity), limit the scope (small groups first, grow gradually, admit what you don't know), and pursue excellence (build around better training, not money).
Affiliation
Licensing the CrossFit name to market services. CF-L1 is the first step toward it; the credential alone does not allow marketing services under the CrossFit name.
CrossFit credential ladder
CF-L1, then CrossFit Level 2 Trainer, then Certified CrossFit Trainer (Level 3), then Certified CrossFit Coach (Level 4) — the preeminent trainer designation.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Treating the CF-L1 as the finish line — it's the first step; mastery is a lifetime commitment.
  • Lopsided coaching: leaning on one or two of the six abilities while neglecting the rest — effective trainers build balanced capacity across all six.
  • The novice's curse — rushing clients (or yourself) past the fundamentals toward complex movements and high volume to look sophisticated or to 'sell' your services.
  • Pushing intensity too much too soon (injury risk, long-term inefficiencies) OR too little/none (blunts adaptation) — when in doubt, err on caution and progress slowly.
  • A new coach taking on big group classes before building seeing-and-correcting skill in one-on-one or small-group settings.
  • Running too many sessions a day — quality drops past roughly five appointments, taking energy, focus, and professional standards with it.
  • Making up answers beyond your knowledge or scope of practice — admit what you don't know and ALWAYS refer any medical condition to a physician.
  • Designing the business around chasing money instead of around making the training and the clients better.

  2. Take over programming for the whole gym before coaching anyone

Scenario 5. A coach is strong at explaining mechanics but admits he rarely catches faults while athletes are actually moving. Which ability is weak, and what's the recommended way to build it?

L1 · Section 22 of 27 Tier: ___

Responsible Training — Keeping Clients Safe Beyond the Movements

Open the official guide

The trainer's playbook for the safety stuff that lives outside the points of performance — rhabdo, equipment and spotting, medical monitoring, hydration, special populations, and using the credential legally.

Key ideas to understand

  • Being an expert coach isn't just knowing points of performance and correcting faults — client safety also covers programming, special populations, equipment layout, and accurate representation of your credentials. This article primes new L1 Trainers to train responsibly while gaining expertise.
  • Rhabdomyolysis ('rhabdo') is rare but real: high-intensity or high-volume exercise (CrossFit or anything that damages muscle cells) can break muscle tissue down and dump the cells' contents into the bloodstream. That can damage the kidneys and, in rare cases, cause renal failure or death.
  • How rhabdo is diagnosed and treated: it's diagnosed when a patient with the right history shows elevated creatine kinase (CK/CPK) — CPK is easier to measure than myoglobin and is used as the marker, even though it's the myoglobin that actually does the damage. Treatment is generous IV fluids to flush myoglobin through the kidneys; severe cases may need dialysis; death (rare) comes from electrolyte imbalances triggering cardiac arrhythmias. Most people fully recover after IV rehydration over hours to a week.
  • Five ways to protect athletes from rhabdo: follow the mechanics-consistency-intensity charter; know the movements with higher rhabdo incidence (those that prolong the eccentric contraction) and watch their programmed volume; scale workouts appropriately; avoid progressive scaling; and educate clients on the symptoms and when to seek care.
  • Why the charter protects against rhabdo: slow, gradual increases in intensity and volume let the body acclimate. Even athletes who quickly show sound mechanics still need that gradual ramp. With new athletes, use modest loads, reduce volume, and coach technique.
  • Scale beyond the on-ramp. At gyms with 'elements'/'on-ramp' classes lasting a couple weeks, keep athletes heavily scaled even AFTER that intro period. If there are no beginner classes, treat workouts as technique sessions for newer athletes — mechanics over speed or load.
  • There's no fixed protocol for ramping intensity — err on caution and work toward long-term fitness. Multiple MONTHS at scaled loads and volumes is normal even for the best athletes, with gradual increases after that. Check in frequently to see how the last dose of exercise affected each athlete; everyone has their whole life to keep improving.
  • Know the higher-incidence movements. Beginners should keep 'negatives' (movements that prolong the eccentric phase) to a minimum — negatives build strength but shouldn't be high-volume for beginners; volume can climb gradually over time.
  • The two CrossFit movements most prone to a prolonged eccentric: jumping pull-ups and full-range-of-motion GHD sit-ups. In the jumping pull-up, don't ride the descent — drop immediately to extended arms once the chin clears the bar and absorb impact with the legs. In the GHD sit-up, newer athletes should use fewer reps and possibly a shortened range until capacity is built.
  • Scale the GHD even for experienced athletes who don't use it routinely. There are no exact volume rules, but beginners, new athletes, and even advanced athletes who haven't been on the GHD regularly should start with relatively low reps of the PARTIAL-range (to parallel) GHD sit-up and build from there with consistent exposure.
  • Avoid progressive scaling with beginners and intermediates. Progressive scaling = continually adjusting a workout's difficulty so an exhausted athlete keeps moving (e.g., dropping a 135-lb. thruster to 115 to 95 to 65 to 45 across the workout). Instead, let these athletes STOP and rest as needed to finish. Progressive scaling can be used, but only very cautiously and even then only with the most advanced athletes.
  • Educate athletes about rhabdo — the risk, the reduction strategies, and the symptoms — so they understand WHY you scale, especially when they're eager to go 'Rx'd.'
  • Outside factors raise rhabdo risk: alcohol and drug use (avoid heavy drinking, especially near training) and certain medications, including statins (cholesterol-lowering drugs).
  • Rhabdo symptoms: severe generalized muscle pain, nausea and vomiting, abdominal cramping, and in severe cases dark-red or cola-colored urine (the color is myoglobin — the same molecule that makes red meat red). If these appear after a workout — or dark-red urine at ANY time — seek medical attention immediately.
  • Counterintuitive risk profile: the highest-risk athletes are those with a reasonable baseline fitness from non-CrossFit training, those returning after a layoff, or experienced athletes who spike volume/intensity well outside their norm — they have the muscle mass and conditioning to generate enough intensity to hurt themselves. The most deconditioned are at LESS risk (not zero) because they likely can't generate that intensity yet. Either way, scale and focus on mechanics with every client.
  • Equipment safety has three parts: condition (installation + day-to-day maintenance), arrangement (layout), and the human factors of spotting and bailing. Installation (pull-up rigs, hanging rings, assembling a GHD) should use professional help when the owner is inexperienced.
  • Load-bearing gear must be over-built and tested. Pull-up rigs, rings, and straps should be designed to bear FAR more than the expected max load and tested at maximum loading before regular client use.
  • Maintenance is paramount and ongoing. Gear that lifts feet off the ground or inverts athletes needs extra attention. Straps, racks, bars, and locking mechanisms must be kept working and checked regularly for wear; dumbbells, kettlebells, and barbells need inspection where handles or collars could come apart. Repair, replace, or pull faulty equipment immediately.
  • Arrangement = the layout of equipment and athletes. Each athlete needs enough space for the movements PLUS a buffer for errant equipment, missed attempts, and safe passage of coaches and other athletes. Never let spare bars, plates, or boxes lie around the workout area — they trip athletes or cause ricochets.
  • Plan for falls in dynamic movements. An athlete can lose grip during a kip (pull-up or muscle-up). Wrapping thumbs around the bar can add feedback but isn't foolproof and can be less secure for small hands — and it doesn't replace the athlete developing body awareness of when to bail. (Wrapping thumbs is always recommended for barbell and ring movements for balance and control, especially higher-risk ones like a bench press or muscle-up.) Keep boxes and racks from under, behind, or directly in front of these athletes; lower adjustable rings to the right height; place assistance boxes to the SIDE (out of others' way) to leave a clear exit.
  • The 'dry run' is the safety tool for arrangement: walk through the workout before it starts — check each athlete's space for each proposed movement, rotate stations on your call, verify spacing, and have participants return to the same spot during the workout.
  • Teach bailing and spotting before loading up. In most weightlifting movements, athletes only need to learn to bail safely — teach it and let them practice before any significant load, and keep enough empty space around them to prevent ricochets.
  • Spotting rules for weightlifting: spotting is NOT recommended except for a bench press (where it's mandatory) and possibly a back squat (especially low-bar). Don't assume athletes know how to spot — instruction and practice at lighter loads are required.
  • Spotting rules for gymnastics: experienced trainers/athletes may spot gymnastics movements in a way that minimizes risk to both parties. Generally spot at the torso or hips for support, but the hips or legs can work (e.g., handstands). The spotter can be to the REAR when the risk of getting hit is low (ring support, GHD sit-up), but a position BESIDE the athlete is often best (e.g., handstand).
  • Have a blood plan. Clean equipment regularly to reduce infection; stage proper disinfectants/sterilizers and clean cloths near the floor to wipe blood off bars immediately. A blood-spill cleanup procedure is in the CrossFit Journal archives.
  • Monitor athletes for conditions that need medical attention. Even though your main job is instruction, watch exertion levels — CrossFit's high intensity pushes athletes to their physical and psychological tolerances, and environmental factors can make things worse.
  • Heat is the big environmental risk. In unseasonably hot, humid weather have plenty of water ready and watch for overexertion signs (e.g., dizziness). Heat raises rhabdo risk (though some cases occur in cold climates). For a potential heat stroke after a workout (e.g., altered mental state), remove excess clothing and douse the athlete with cool water BEFORE medical help arrives.
  • Refer other red-flag symptoms: numbness or chronic joint/muscle pain goes to medical professionals; any non-responsive athlete needs medical attention immediately.
  • Be emergency-ready. Get trained in CPR and AED use and keep an AED at the gym — most states require this by law, and trainers/affiliates should comply with all state laws. CPR/AED credentials typically last one to two years (Red Cross, American Heart Association) — keep them current.
  • Hydration, in one line: drink when thirsty, don't when you're not. CrossFit advises AGAINST rehydration strategies that push fluids to prevent body-weight loss during activity — dehydration during exercise is a normal physiological process, and the thirst mechanism is enough to regulate hydration and serum sodium during exercise.
  • Overdrinking is the real danger, not under-drinking. Drinking beyond thirst to prevent weight loss offers no health or performance benefit and risks exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH) — a potentially deadly dilution of the body's serum sodium, caused by overconsuming fluid. It's essentially iatrogenic (caused by the very advice to 'drink as much as tolerable').
  • Sports drinks don't protect against EAH. Electrolyte-enhanced sports drinks do NOT reduce hyponatremia risk; because of flavoring and sugar they may actually drive MORE overconsumption than water alone, raising EAH risk.
  • Special populations need physician clearance. Anyone with a medical condition must be cleared by a physician before you recommend a regimen. A medical-history form helps you spot issues, and you should ask about medical status and know common conditions that need clearance (e.g., diabetes, prescription medications).
  • Pregnant athletes: still get medical clearance and the physician's guidelines once the condition is known. Be especially mindful of reducing fall risk (box jumps, rope climbs) and watch for complaints of calf pain or swelling, which can signal more serious issues. (CrossFit Journal has scaling resources, e.g., 'Pregnancy: A Practical Guide for Scaling.')
  • Post-surgery athletes: workouts are scalable and many recover well staying active, but seek clearance from the SURGEON before restarting a regimen.
  • Know your scope of practice: you can promote anyone's desire to exercise and give direction, but that does NOT extend to diagnosing or treating any medical condition.
  • Use the credential legally. Passing the L1 exam earns the 'CrossFit Level 1 Trainer' designation (abbreviated 'CF-L1 Trainer'), a title approved by ANSI (the third party through which the course is accredited).
  • Two musts for the credential: use the correct terminology, and act in accordance with the Level 1 Trainer Certificate License Agreement (signed when you get your test results).
  • 'Certificate' vs. 'certification' — and never say 'certified.' A CF-L1 holds the Level 1 CERTIFICATE (valid five years; see the Participant Handbook for maintaining active status; the public Trainer Directory verifies credentials). Don't use 'Level 1 Certified' — it misrepresents the credential and isn't endorsed by CrossFit. A 'Certificate Course' has learning objectives, a training component, and a test tied to those objectives; a 'certification' (Certified CrossFit Trainer/Coach) is a test only, no educational component, prepared on the applicant's own time, designed to assess competency across a whole profession. A certification generally demonstrates greater professional competency than a certificate.
  • What the credential lets you do — and not do. CF-L1 may sit next to your name like other educational credentials (M.S., R.N., D.C.) and go on a website bio or business card. It does NOT let you use the 'CrossFit' name to market services (personal CrossFit training, CrossFit classes). You can use the CrossFit METHOD to train yourself, friends, and family for free, but to use the CrossFit name or logo (the brand) to advertise, market, promote, or solicit, you must affiliate (license the CrossFit name).
  • Bottom line: the risk-to-benefit ratio for CrossFit participants is very low — but keeping it low is the trainer's responsibility, and this guidance is the resource for doing it.
