Words to know (plain English)

Rhabdomyolysis ('rhabdo')
A rare but serious condition where muscle tissue breaks down and releases the cells' contents into the bloodstream, which can damage the kidneys and, in rare cases, cause renal failure or death.
Creatine kinase (CK / CPK)
An enzyme used as the diagnostic marker for rhabdo because it's easy to measure in blood; an elevated level in a patient with the right history indicates rhabdo — though it's the myoglobin that actually damages the kidneys.
Myoglobin
The muscle protein released during rhabdo that damages the kidneys and colors dark-red/cola-colored urine; it's the same molecule that gives red meat its color.
Eccentric phase / 'negatives'
The lengthening (lowering) phase of a contraction. 'Negatives' are movements that prolong this phase; they build strength but raise rhabdo risk and should be low-volume for beginners. Jumping pull-ups and full-range GHD sit-ups are the common culprits.
Progressive scaling
Continually lowering a workout's difficulty mid-workout (e.g., dropping the thruster load step by step) so an exhausted athlete keeps moving. To be avoided with beginners and intermediates; used only very cautiously with the most advanced athletes.
On-ramp / 'elements' classes
Introductory classes (often a couple of weeks) for beginners. Athletes should still be heavily scaled even AFTER this intro period.
Exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH)
A potentially deadly dilution of the body's serum sodium caused by overconsumption of fluid (drinking beyond thirst). Essentially iatrogenic — driven by the 'drink as much as tolerable' belief; sports drinks don't reduce the risk and may worsen it.
Iatrogenic
Caused by medical or health advice/treatment itself. EAH is described as iatrogenic because it stems from the prevailing advice to over-hydrate during exercise.
Bailing
Safely ditching a lift when it can't be completed. Athletes must be taught and allowed to practice bailing before any significant load is lifted, with enough empty space to avoid ricochets.
Spotting
Physically assisting an athlete during a movement. Not recommended for most weightlifting except the bench press (mandatory) and possibly the back squat; gymnastics spots are placed to minimize risk to both people (often beside the athlete).
AED (automatic external defibrillator)
A device for cardiac emergencies. Trainers should be CPR/AED trained (credentials renewed every one to two years) and keep an AED at the gym, as most states require by law.
Scope of practice
The boundary of what a trainer may do: promote a person's desire to exercise and provide direction — but NOT diagnose or treat any medical condition.
CrossFit Level 1 Trainer (CF-L1 Trainer)
The ANSI-approved designation earned by passing the Level 1 exam. The holder has the Level 1 CERTIFICATE (valid five years) — never 'Level 1 Certified.'
Certificate vs. certification
A Certificate Course (like Level 1) has learning objectives, a training component, and a test tied to those objectives. A certification (Certified CrossFit Trainer/Coach) is a test only with no educational component, designed to assess competency across a whole profession.
Affiliation
Licensing the CrossFit name/logo to market services. The CF-L1 lets you use the CrossFit METHOD with yourself, friends, and family for free, but you must affiliate to advertise or market under the CrossFit brand.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Ramping intensity or volume too fast — even athletes with quickly sound mechanics need a gradual increase; months at scaled loads is normal, and rhabdo can follow a spike outside an athlete's norm.
  • Progressive scaling on beginners or intermediates (continually dropping load so an exhausted athlete never stops) — let them rest and stop instead; reserve it, very cautiously, for only the most advanced.
  • Programming high-volume 'negatives,' jumping pull-ups, or full-range GHD sit-ups for beginners or anyone not routinely using the GHD — scale reps and range (GHD to parallel) and build gradually.
  • Dark-red or cola-colored urine at ANY time, or severe muscle pain/nausea/cramping after a workout — seek medical attention immediately; that's myoglobin.
  • Assuming the most fit athletes are safest from rhabdo — the fit-but-new, the returning-from-layoff, and the over-reaching experienced athlete are HIGHEST risk.
  • Leaving spare bars, plates, or boxes around the floor, skipping equipment load-testing/maintenance, or skipping a dry run — all set up trips, ricochets, and falls.
  • Loading a bench press without a spotter (mandatory), or letting athletes spot/bail without being taught and practicing at light loads first.
  • Pushing fluids to prevent body-weight loss during exercise — overdrinking risks deadly EAH; sports drinks don't help and may worsen it. Drink to thirst.
  • Skipping physician clearance for any athlete with a medical condition, or for pregnant (physician) and post-surgery (surgeon) athletes.
  • Diagnosing or treating any medical condition — that's outside a trainer's scope of practice; refer to a physician.
  • Saying or writing 'Level 1 Certified' or using the 'CrossFit' name to market services without affiliating — both misrepresent the credential/brand.
  • Letting CPR/AED credentials lapse or not having an AED on site where state law requires it.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. Twenty minutes into a heavy workout, a beginner is clearly gassed and slowing down. The coach starts dropping the thruster load every few reps (135 to 115 to 95 to 65) so she never has to stop. What does Responsible Training say about this?

  1. It's progressive scaling, which must be avoided with beginners and intermediates; let her stop and rest as needed to finish
  2. It's good coaching — keeping her moving maximizes the training effect
  3. It's fine as long as the coach explains it afterward
  4. It's required to prevent rhabdo in beginners
  5. It's acceptable because the loads are getting lighter, not heavier
Show answer

Answer: A — It's progressive scaling, which must be avoided with beginners and intermediates; let her stop and rest as needed to finish. Continually lowering difficulty so an exhausted athlete keeps moving is the textbook definition of progressive scaling, which the guide says must be avoided with beginners and intermediates — allow them to stop and rest to complete the workout. It may be used only very cautiously with the most advanced athletes. So B, C, D, and E all endorse exactly the practice the section warns against; D even inverts the rhabdo guidance.

Scenario 2. After a hot, humid metcon, an athlete becomes confused and disoriented (altered mental state) while you wait for EMS. What's the recommended immediate action?

  1. Walk him around to keep his heart rate up
  2. Wait and do nothing until EMS arrives
  3. Remove excess clothing and douse him with cool water before medical attention arrives
  4. Have him drink as much fluid as tolerable to rehydrate
  5. Give him an electrolyte sports drink to restore sodium
Show answer

Answer: C — Remove excess clothing and douse him with cool water before medical attention arrives. For a potential heat stroke (altered mental state) after a workout, the guide says to remove excess clothing and douse the athlete with cool water before medical help arrives. D and E are dangerous — forcing fluids risks EAH, and sports drinks don't help (and may worsen overconsumption); A and B delay the cooling the athlete needs. Heat also raises rhabdo risk, making aggressive cooling the priority.

Scenario 3. A new member mentions she's pregnant and wants to keep training. What's the correct first step under this section's special-populations guidance?

  1. Keep programming as normal — CrossFit is scalable, so no changes are needed
  2. Tell her to stop exercising entirely until after delivery
  3. Refer her to another gym
  4. Prescribe a pregnancy-specific program yourself based on the CrossFit Journal
  5. Request medical clearance and guidelines from her physician, then be especially mindful of fall risk and watch for calf pain/swelling
Show answer

Answer: E — Request medical clearance and guidelines from her physician, then be especially mindful of fall risk and watch for calf pain/swelling. The guide says to request medical clearance and guidelines from the physician once pregnancy is known, be especially aware of reducing fall risk (box jumps, rope climbs), and watch for complaints of calf pain or swelling. A skips clearance and modifications; B is unnecessarily restrictive; D oversteps scope (a trainer doesn't write a medical-driven plan without physician guidelines); C abandons the client. The Journal has scaling resources, but clearance comes first.

Scenario 4. A trainer wants to put 'CrossFit Level 1 Certified Trainer' on his business card and start advertising 'CrossFit personal training' out of his garage. What's correct?

  1. 'Certified' is fine, but he can't say 'CrossFit' at all
  2. Use 'CrossFit Level 1 Trainer' (not 'Certified'), and don't use the CrossFit name to market services unless he affiliates
  3. Neither the credential nor the name can appear anywhere without a Level 2
  4. Both are fine once you hold the CF-L1
  5. He can advertise 'CrossFit personal training' but must drop the credential from the card
Show answer

Answer: B — Use 'CrossFit Level 1 Trainer' (not 'Certified'), and don't use the CrossFit name to market services unless he affiliates. The guide says those who pass the exam should NOT use 'certified' — the correct term is the Level 1 Certificate/CF-L1 Trainer, which can go on a business card next to the name. But the credential does NOT permit using the 'CrossFit' name to market services; that requires affiliating (licensing the name). D keeps the wrong 'certified' wording and allows unlicensed marketing; A, E, and C mangle the actual rules about terminology, marketing, and what the credential allows.

Scenario 5. Three athletes ask to do a high-volume GHD sit-up workout. One is a beginner, one is a returning member back from a six-month layoff, and one is an experienced CrossFitter who hasn't touched a GHD in a year. How should you program it to manage rhabdo risk?

  1. Let the experienced and returning athletes go Rx; only scale the beginner
  2. Scale all three — start with relatively low reps of partial-range (to parallel) GHD sit-ups and build gradually with consistent exposure
  3. Cancel GHD work entirely; it's never safe
  4. Only the beginner is at risk, so the other two can do full volume
  5. Have them do jumping pull-ups instead, which carry no rhabdo risk
Show answer

Answer: B — Scale all three — start with relatively low reps of partial-range (to parallel) GHD sit-ups and build gradually with consistent exposure. The guide says beginners, new athletes, AND even advanced athletes who haven't been routinely using the GHD should start with low reps of the partial-range (to parallel) GHD sit-up and build with consistent exposure. It also flags returning-from-layoff and out-of-norm athletes as among the HIGHEST rhabdo risk — so A and D are exactly backwards. C overcorrects (the GHD is fine when scaled), and E is wrong because jumping pull-ups are the OTHER named prolonged-eccentric, higher-incidence movement.

L1 · Section 23 of 27 Tier: ___

Fundamentals, Virtuosity and Mastery

Open the official guide

Coach Glassman's open letter on why relentless commitment to the basics — not fancy movements — is the real mark of a master trainer.

Key ideas to understand

  • The gymnastics scoring analogy sets it up: a flawless routine only earns a 9.7, not a perfect 10.0. The last three tenths come from 'risk, originality, and virtuosity' on top of error-free execution.
  • Risk is performing a movement likely to be missed or botched. Originality is a movement or combination unique to the athlete — something not seen before.
  • Novices love risk and originality because they're dramatic, fun, and impressive — especially to the athletes themselves (audiences often don't even notice).
  • Virtuosity is a different beast: defined in gymnastics as 'performing the common uncommonly well.' It's supremely elusive, but instantly recognized by audience, coaches, and athletes alike — and it's always the mark of true mastery (and of genius and beauty).
  • The novice's curse: in any skill or art — violin, poetry, gymnastics — there's a strong pull to rush past the fundamentals toward elaborate, sophisticated movements. That rush to originality and risk is the curse.
  • The novice's curse shows up as excessive adornment, silly creativity, weak fundamentals, and ultimately a marked lack of virtuosity and delayed mastery.
  • Being taught by the very best in any field is usually surprising for how simple, fundamental, and basic the instruction is.
  • The curse afflicts the teacher as much as the learner — and physical training is no different.
  • What dooms a training program and dilutes a coach's efficacy is a lack of commitment to fundamentals — Glassman says this is increasingly common in both programming and supervising execution.
  • The symptoms he sees: the short, intense couplets and triplets that epitomize CrossFit are rarely prescribed anymore, and trainers rarely really nitpick the mechanics of fundamental movements.
  • He understands the temptation: it's natural to want to teach advanced, fancy movements to entertain and impress the client. But make no mistake — it's a sucker's move.
  • Teaching a snatch before there's an overhead squat, or an overhead squat before there's an air squat, is a colossal mistake. The rush to advancement increases injury risk, delays progress, blunts the client's return on effort — in short, it retards his fitness.
  • The counterintuitive payoff: if you really insist on basics, clients will immediately recognize you as a master trainer. They won't be bored — they'll be awed, and they'll come to recognize the potency of fundamentals.
  • Clients grounded in basics also advance faster in every measurable way than those without a teacher committed to fundamentals — and the coach will appear more experienced, more professional, and earn more respect just by recommitting to basics.
  • There's plenty of time in a one-hour session to warm up, practice a basic movement or skill or chase a PR/max lift, discuss and critique the athletes' efforts, and then pound out a tight couplet or triplet — or just play.
  • Play matters — tire flipping, basketball, relay races, tag, Hoover-ball — it's essential to good programming, but it's seasoning (salt, pepper, oregano), not the main course.
  • CrossFit trainers have the tools to be the best trainers on Earth — but 'good enough never is.' The goal is that last tenth of a point, the whole 10.0: virtuosity.

What you should get from it

Perfect execution alone isn't a perfect score — mastery demands something beyond merely not making mistakes.

What you should get from it

Risk and originality are the flashy, dramatic stuff — the things novices reach for first.

What you should get from it

Virtuosity isn't doing rare things — it's doing the ordinary, basic things at an extraordinary level. This is the central idea of the whole letter.

What you should get from it

If you want to spot a true master, look for virtuosity in the basics — not flashy tricks.

What you should get from it

The novice's curse is the urge to skip the fundamentals and jump to advanced, impressive movements — name it so you can resist it.

What you should get from it

Skipping the basics produces showy but weak movement and actually delays mastery — the opposite of what the novice wants.

What you should get from it

The best teachers obsess over basics — and coaches, not just students, fall for the curse.

What you should get from it

The single biggest threat to a coach's effectiveness is abandoning the basics.

What you should get from it

Two warning signs in your own coaching: no tight couplets/triplets, and loose mechanics that go uncorrected.

What you should get from it

Skipping the progression to look impressive is a trap — you must build the basic position before the advanced one.

What you should get from it

Rushing past fundamentals hurts the client three ways: more risk, slower progress, and worse results.

What you should get from it

Counterintuitively, hammering the basics earns respect and awe, not boredom — and clients advance faster for it.

What you should get from it

There's plenty of time to do the basics well AND get a hard workout in one hour — fundamentals don't crowd out intensity.

What you should get from it

Games and play belong in programming, but as flavor — not as the substance of the training.

What you should get from it

The standard is the whole 10.0, not 'good enough.' Chase virtuosity in the basics relentlessly.

Words to know (plain English)

Virtuosity
Performing the common uncommonly well — doing the basic, ordinary movements at an extraordinary level. It's the true mark of mastery, and the whole point of this letter.
Risk (gymnastics)
Performing a movement that's likely to be missed or botched. Dramatic and impressive, but one of the things novices over-reach for instead of mastering basics.
Originality (gymnastics)
A movement or combination unique to the athlete — something not seen before. Like risk, it's flashy and tempting but not a substitute for virtuosity.
The novice's curse
The strong pull — in any skill — to rush past the fundamentals toward elaborate, advanced, impressive movements. It produces weak fundamentals and delays mastery, and it affects coaches as well as students.
Fundamentals
The basic movements and mechanics that everything advanced is built on (e.g., air squat before overhead squat before snatch). Relentless commitment to them is the mark of a master coach.
Couplet / triplet
A short, intense workout built from two (couplet) or three (triplet) movements. Glassman names these as epitomizing CrossFit programming — and warns they're prescribed too rarely now.
Efficacy (of a coach)
How effective a coach actually is at producing results in clients. It gets diluted by a lack of commitment to fundamentals.
Play (as seasoning)
Games like tire flipping, basketball, relays, tag, and Hoover-ball. Essential to good programming for fun and variety, but a flavoring on top of the basics — not the main course.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Teaching advanced movements to entertain or impress a client before the prerequisite basics are in place — Glassman calls this 'a sucker's move.'
  • Teaching a snatch with no overhead squat, or an overhead squat with no air squat — a 'colossal mistake' that raises injury risk, delays progress, and blunts results.
  • Rarely prescribing the short, intense couplets and triplets that epitomize CrossFit — a symptom of drifting away from fundamentals.
  • Failing to really nitpick the mechanics of fundamental movements — letting sloppy basics slide dilutes a coach's efficacy.
  • Treating play (games, sports) as the main course of a session instead of seasoning on top of solid fundamentals and intensity.
  • Settling for 'good enough' — the standard is virtuosity, the whole 10.0.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. An eager new member wants you to teach him the snatch on day one to impress his friends, but he can't yet hold a sound air squat or overhead squat. What's the best coaching move?

  1. Let him max out on snatch to find his ceiling first.
  2. Build the progression: nail the air squat, then the overhead squat, then move toward the snatch — and explain that mastering basics is what makes him advance fastest and safest.
  3. Teach the snatch anyway — keeping him entertained is what retains members.
  4. Give him a flashy, original complex you invented to keep it fun.
  5. Tell him snatching isn't for regular members and refuse to ever teach it.
Show answer

Answer: B — Build the progression: nail the air squat, then the overhead squat, then move toward the snatch — and explain that mastering basics is what makes him advance fastest and safest.. B follows the letter exactly — you must build air squat → overhead squat → snatch, because skipping the progression increases injury risk, delays progress, and blunts results. C is the 'sucker's move' Glassman warns against; E wrongly withholds a movement everyone can eventually learn; D indulges the novice's curse; A loads a movement he hasn't earned.

Scenario 2. A coach worries that drilling air squats and basic mechanics will bore his clients and make him look unimpressive. According to Glassman, what actually happens when a coach relentlessly insists on basics?

  1. Clients recognize him as a master trainer, are awed rather than bored, and advance faster in every measurable way.
  2. Clients learn slower because basics waste time.
  3. Clients get bored and quit.
  4. Nothing changes; basics are neutral.
  5. The coach loses respect for not teaching advanced movements.
Show answer

Answer: A — Clients recognize him as a master trainer, are awed rather than bored, and advance faster in every measurable way.. A is the letter's promise — insisting on basics earns recognition as a master trainer, awes clients rather than boring them, and produces faster measurable progress. C and E are the coach's fear, which Glassman explicitly says is wrong; B contradicts the point that fundamentals accelerate progress; D ignores the clear effect he describes.

Scenario 3. You have a one-hour class and want to honor 'commitment to fundamentals' without sacrificing intensity. Which session structure best fits Glassman's letter?

  1. Warm up, drill a basic movement or chase a PR/max, critique mechanics, then finish with a tight couplet or triplet (with play used sparingly as seasoning).
  2. Spend the entire hour playing Hoover-ball and relay races.
  3. Only do long, single-modality endurance work with no skill practice.
  4. Teach advanced movements the whole time to keep it exciting.
  5. Spend the whole hour on one novel, original complex you designed.
Show answer

Answer: A — Warm up, drill a basic movement or chase a PR/max, critique mechanics, then finish with a tight couplet or triplet (with play used sparingly as seasoning).. A mirrors the session Glassman describes — there's plenty of time to do basics well, critique, and still pound out an intense couplet or triplet, with play as seasoning, not the main course. B makes play the main course; E and D indulge the novice's curse; C abandons skill work and the short, intense couplets/triplets that epitomize CrossFit.

L1 · Section 24 of 27 Tier: ___

Professional Training and Building a Practice

Open the official guide

This section lays out the professional's creed, that your competency is your efficacy and your clients are your top priority, and shows how that standard scaled a one-on-one practice into group classes and the pursuit of excellence as a business plan.

Key ideas to understand

  • The professional's identity: being a fitness trainer is a passion, not just a job, with clients as the top priority and their successes as the trainer's life's work. On the surface the job is to move clients toward physical prowess, but it carries a purpose that transcends the physical. Training is a physical metaphor for the habits and attitudes that drive success everywhere.
  • The lessons of physical training are unavoidable and universally applicable: perseverance, industry, sacrifice, self-control, integrity, honesty, and commitment are best and most easily learned in the gym. Even clients already successful in business, sport, war, or love find their most important values reinforced and refined through rigorous training. You know you've succeeded only when clients bring back concrete examples of this carrying over.
  • Competency is determined solely by efficacy. Your methods must be second to none, which makes fitness trends and fashions distractions, not attractions. To the extent your methods are unconventional, unaccepted, or unique, that's the margin by which you dominate your industry, and a trainer who lusts for popular approval is chasing mediocrity or worse.
  • Commitment to unrivaled efficacy forces you to develop new tools and methods, which can't happen without study and experimentation. A lot of the real work happens not in the gym but in books, scientific literature, and conversation with other trainers and coaches.
  • Efficacy is ultimately determined by your athletes' performance, and performance must be measured. Competition, testing, and recordkeeping are what tell you the difference between merely looking or feeling good and actually being good at your job.
  • Your commitment shows in the first meeting: you are all theirs, the object of your focus and the focus of your conversation. Clients come back not for your physical capacity but because they believe in your capacity to develop theirs. Notably, world-class athletes rarely make world-class trainers.
  • Reject skill-less, low-skill programming, it delivers inferior results and turns trainers into cheerleaders. The professional understands the mechanics, cues, and techniques of complex movements and can teach them, bringing a skill set that scares off most trainers.
  • Keeping up with your athletes' progress demands you keep advancing your own understanding: 'If a trainer's clients are not testing the limits of his knowledge, he is not doing a good enough job with them.' The master trainer is proud to have a student exceed his abilities but seeks to delay it by staying ahead of the athlete's needs, never by holding the athlete back.
  • Because you want the training to transcend the physical, you're obligated to understand clients' jobs, hobbies, families, and goals. Motivating clients to transcend fitness requires being involved in their lives, being both interested in them and interesting to them, which means showing up at parties, weddings, and family gatherings as a genuine friend.
  • The payoff of that relationship: clients do all your marketing. The professional doesn't advertise, promote, or market, he trains very, very well, and the more clients he gets, the more they bring. (The Scaling Professional Training portion that follows shows how this creed played out as the practice grew.)
  • The standard, unyielding commitment to client and efficacy, guided everything and was the primary reason for CrossFit's success. A one-on-one practice running roughly 5-10 a.m. Monday-Saturday produced a low-six-figure income, while keeping afternoons free for family, recreation, and study.
  • There's a real ceiling on one-on-one work. Training at that level of attention and commitment is draining, and about five appointments a day was the limit before an unacceptable drop in energy, focus, and professional standards. When demand exceeded that, the answer was group classes.
  • Group classes have a powerful upside: the social dynamic motivates an athletic output rarely matched one-on-one (the source of the lines 'men will die for points' and CrossFit as 'the sport of fitness'), and they dramatically increase training revenue.
  • Group classes have two drawbacks. First, space, but it's not linear: training 10 people doesn't take 10 times the space, and space for one athlete can serve three or four well. Second, the lower trainer-to-trainee ratio can dilute professional training standards, which can be compensated for only by a rarely found skill set.
  • Running group classes without compromising laser focus requires giving each member the impression he's getting all the attention he'd get one-on-one, which takes tremendous skill. The only proven path to that skill is gradually migrating from one-on-one to group sessions. Trainers who jump straight into group classes without growing into them usually aren't meeting professional standards, and tend to have trouble filling their classes.
  • The concrete migration model: start by doubling up clients into one-on-two appointments. Tell existing clients the good news, their rate goes down and they'll meet a new friend, and ask for a trial period where there's resistance. Structurally, a client paying $75 might now pay $50; adding a third brought it to $40. With each added athlete the per-athlete rate falls and the trainer's hourly revenue rises, which works perfectly unless attention is perceived to drop.
  • All demands on the trainer skyrocket in a group: attention, enthusiasm, voice projection, and engagement all have to escalate. It's an acquired skill, an art. The goal is to give so much attention and 'in your face' presence that each participant is grateful he didn't get more. The level of scrutiny and criticism ratchets up alongside the rate of praise and input. A new trainer can't walk into this and do well, and a trainer who participates in the workout while trying to lead it produces substandard training.
  • Within two years the practice morphed from all one-on-one to all group classes without working more hours (keeping a couple of choice one-on-one clients), charging $15 per class and averaging 10-15 athletes per session. This substantially raised income and boosted the stability of the practice, seasonal dips from summer and Christmas vacations largely disappeared, unlike a one-on-one practice where a few simultaneous client vacations hit income hard.
  • CrossFit.com launched at the same time, motivated by the same commitment to client and efficacy, not to increase revenue but to favorably impact more people. The group classes, website, CrossFit Journal, seminars, and affiliate program were all introduced to bring more quality training to more people, and each addition increased CrossFit's value for everyone. The original one-on-one clients were the first to benefit from all of it, every expansion served the whole community.
  • The business philosophy: pursuit of excellence, not money. Accept three premises, markets are largely unknowable, excellence is obvious to everyone, and free markets reward excellence, and it follows that the most effective business plan is to achieve excellence and let the market bring the money to you. The Santa Cruz expansion was decided not on uncertain financial projections but on a simpler question: 'Will it improve the quality of the programming and the training experience?' The answer was yes; the numbers tripled within six months and the extra space enabled programming refinements.
  • As seminars, journal, website, and affiliate program grew, the group classes were handed off to a new generation of CrossFit trainers who now cover most of the overhead, freeing time and resources for new projects that support and develop the CrossFit community.

Words to know (plain English)

Efficacy
The trainer's competency measured by results, ultimately by the athlete's measured performance; the professional's single standard of value.
The professional's creed
The stated standard that clients are the top priority and competency equals efficacy, an unyielding commitment to client and results.
Recordkeeping / testing
Measuring athletes' performance over time so the trainer can tell real improvement from merely looking or feeling good.

The number of athletes per trainer; a lower ratio in group classes can dilute professional standards unless the trainer has the skill to compensate.

The first step in migrating to groups, pairing two clients in a session, which lowers each client's cost and raises the trainer's hourly revenue.
Group class
Coaching multiple athletes at once; powerful socially and for revenue, but it demands escalated attention, voice projection, and engagement.
Pursuit of excellence (business plan)
The principle that achieving obvious excellence in a free market draws money to you, so excellence, not revenue, drives every decision.
'The sport of fitness'
The idea, born from the competitive energy of group classes, that fitness itself can be made competitive and measurable.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Chasing fitness trends, fashions, or popular approval. The creed treats these as distractions and says lusting for approval is chasing mediocrity or worse.
  • Defaulting to skill-less, low-skill programming because it's easy to coach. It delivers inferior results and makes the trainer a cheerleader rather than a coach.
  • Holding an athlete back to stay 'ahead' of them. The master trainer stays ahead by advancing his own knowledge, never by retarding the athlete's growth.
  • Jumping straight into group coaching without growing into it. The text says this almost always means substandard professional standards and trouble filling classes.
  • Working out alongside the class while trying to lead it. The section explicitly says this produces substandard training; the trainer must coach the group, not train in it.
  • Letting perceived attention drop as you add athletes. The pricing/scaling model only works if each client still feels fully attended to.
  • Treating training as just a transaction. The professional is obligated to know clients' lives and measure their performance, and the relationship (not advertising) is what drives growth.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A trainer has filled their schedule with one-on-one clients and demand keeps growing, but they're hitting a wall around five appointments a day and their energy and focus are slipping. Based on this section, what's the recommended next move?

  1. Raise one-on-one prices high enough to cut demand back down
  2. Keep adding one-on-one appointments and push through the fatigue
  3. Hire a brand-new trainer to take over the group classes from day one
  4. Immediately launch large group classes of 15 to maximize revenue
  5. Begin migrating toward group training, starting by doubling clients into one-on-two appointments and growing into group classes gradually
Show answer

Answer: E — Begin migrating toward group training, starting by doubling clients into one-on-two appointments and growing into group classes gradually. The text describes ~5 appointments/day as the realistic one-on-one ceiling before standards drop, and the only proven path to group-coaching skill is gradually migrating from one-on-one, starting with one-on-two appointments. Pushing through fatigue (B) erodes standards, jumping straight to big classes (D) or dropping a new trainer into them (C) usually means substandard training, and simply pricing out demand (A) abandons the goal of serving more people.

Scenario 2. A coach is debating whether to adopt a popular but low-skill program format because it's trendy and easy to run. How does the creed in this section frame that decision?

  1. Adopt it only if competitors are using it
  2. Follow the trend, popularity is good evidence the method works
  3. It doesn't matter what you program as long as clients are entertained
  4. Competency is determined by efficacy; trends and fashions are distractions, and skill-less programming delivers inferior results and makes a trainer a cheerleader
  5. Low-skill programming is fine because it's safer
Show answer

Answer: D — Competency is determined by efficacy; trends and fashions are distractions, and skill-less programming delivers inferior results and makes a trainer a cheerleader. The professional's competency is solely his efficacy; fitness trends and fashions are distractions, not attractions, and the section explicitly rejects skill-less/low-skill programming as delivering inferior results and turning trainers into cheerleaders. Following trends or competitors (B, A), entertaining clients (C), or assuming low-skill equals safe (E) all contradict the creed's standard of results.

Scenario 3. When deciding whether to expand CrossFit Santa Cruz, the financial projections were too uncertain to justify it on paper. What question did they use to make the call?

  1. 'What is the guaranteed return on investment?'
  2. 'Will it improve the quality of the programming and the training experience?'
  3. 'Will it let us advertise more aggressively?'
  4. 'Can we cut costs by expanding?'
  5. 'Will competitors expand first?'
Show answer

Answer: B — 'Will it improve the quality of the programming and the training experience?'. Because the variables were too numerous to convince an accountant, they asked instead, 'Will it improve the quality of the programming and the training experience?' The answer was a resounding yes, and the numbers tripled within six months. This reflects the business philosophy of pursuing excellence and letting the market bring the money, not chasing ROI, competitors, cost-cutting, or advertising (A, E, D, C).

Scenario 4. A trainer leads their group class by doing the workout right alongside the members, figuring it builds camaraderie. What does this section say about that?

  1. It's ideal, training with the group is the best way to motivate them
  2. It's required so the trainer understands the workout
  3. It's fine as long as the trainer is fit enough to keep up
  4. Participating in the class while trying to lead it produces substandard training; the trainer's job is to escalate attention, scrutiny, and coaching, not to work out
  5. It only matters for one-on-one sessions
Show answer

Answer: D — Participating in the class while trying to lead it produces substandard training; the trainer's job is to escalate attention, scrutiny, and coaching, not to work out. The text warns directly about the decline in standards 'for those trainers who are participating in their classes while trying to lead them,' calling the training 'always substandard.' Running a group well demands escalated attention, enthusiasm, voice projection, and 'in your face' presence for each athlete, none of which can happen while the trainer is doing the workout. So A, C, B, and E are wrong.

L1 · Section 25 of 27 Tier: ___

Credentials, License, and FAQ: What CF-L1 Actually Lets You Do

Open the official guide

Plain-English rules on what your CF-L1 lets you do with the CrossFit name and brand, the frequently asked questions, and the full ladder of CrossFit credentials.

Key ideas to understand

  • Passing the Level 1 course and exam isn't the finish line: you must agree to the CrossFit Level 1 Trainer Certificate License Agreement. This section summarizes it in plain English, but you're still responsible for everything in the full Agreement.
  • The single most important distinction: the CrossFit METHODOLOGY versus the CrossFit BRAND. The methodology (the training itself, free on CrossFit.com for over a decade) is free to use and follow. The brand name is NOT free.
  • What a CF-L1 CANNOT do: you cannot use the CrossFit brand name to advertise, market, promote, or solicit business or services in any way. Doing so breaches the Agreement and can make you liable for trademark infringement under Federal Law.
  • How to earn the right to advertise using the CrossFit name: become a licensed CrossFit affiliate. That means submitting an application, being accepted, and paying the annual affiliation fee. Affiliation is what unlocks the trademark for advertising.
  • What a CF-L1 CAN do with the name: use only the terms 'CrossFit Level 1 Trainer' or 'CF-L1 Trainer' on a resume, business card, or trainer bio on a website. That's it. You may NOT call yourself a 'Certified' CrossFit Trainer, that title is reserved for Level 3 and above.
  • What a CF-L1 can do without the brand: train yourself, teach friends for free, and introduce the methodology to others. You just can't use the CrossFit brand name or CrossFit's copyrighted material (like the Training Guide or Participant Handbook) to market your services.
  • You're also bound to uphold the highest standards of ethics and behavior; actions that reflect unfavorably on CrossFit, LLC are a breach of the Agreement.
  • CrossFit's legal department aggressively pursues unlicensed use of the brand name and copyrighted material worldwide. Questions about proper use go to legalintake@crossfit.com; suspected misuse is reported at crossfit.com/iptheft.
  • FAQ, teaching classes: you can teach CrossFit classes and advertise yourself as a CF-L1 ONLY if you're at a licensed affiliate. At a non-affiliate (like a commercial gym), you cannot use the CrossFit brand name to advertise your classes at all.
  • FAQ, advertising 'similar' training: as a CF-L1 you may use the methodology and train people on your own, but only affiliation lets you use the CrossFit trademark to describe your programming or advertise your services as 'CrossFit.'
  • FAQ, word of mouth: even telling clients you're 'doing CrossFit' verbally is NOT permitted without affiliating first. Word-of-mouth marketing of CrossFit training counts as use of the trademark.
  • FAQ, training off-site: a CF-L1 who trains clients at homes or commercial gyms still cannot use the trademark without affiliating, but can always state the credential on a business card, resume, or bio.
  • FAQ, 'Certified': you cannot call yourself a 'Certified' CrossFit Trainer; that's reserved for Level 3 and above. The correct descriptions are 'CrossFit Level 1 Trainer' or 'CF-L1 Trainer.'
  • FAQ, what the credential gets you: you're listed in the CrossFit Trainer Directory (a public database to find licensed trainers), and the CF-L1 is the prerequisite for more advanced courses, including the Level 2 Certificate Course.
  • The credential ladder, bottom to top: CF-L1 (Level 1 Trainer), CF-L2 (Level 2 Trainer), CF-L3 (Certified Level 3 Trainer), CF-L4 (Certified Level 4 Coach). Note 'Training' covers L1 and L2; 'Certification' covers L3 and L4.
  • Ladder requirements. CF-L1: no prerequisites; obtain by 100% participation in the Level 1 course and passing the test; maintain by reattending and passing the test every 5 years. CF-L2: prerequisite is a current CF-L1; obtain by 100% participation in the Level 2 course and meeting current certificate requisites; maintain by reattending and meeting requisites every 5 years.
  • CF-L3: prerequisites are a current CF-L2 plus CPR/AED; obtain by passing the ANSI-accredited CCFT examination; maintain every 3 years with 50 CEUs + 300 CrossFit training hours and current CPR/AED. CF-L4: prerequisite is a current CF-L3; obtain by passing a performance evaluation; maintain every 3 years with 50 CEUs + 300 CrossFit training hours and current CPR/AED.

Find & highlight0/10 found

Open the guide to this section and track these down — highlight each one, then check it off. Tap a clue to reveal what it teaches.

What you should get from it

This is the whole license in one line: the training is free to use, the name is protected and licensed.

What you should get from it

Holding a CF-L1 does NOT grant any right to advertise with the CrossFit name; misuse can mean Federal trademark liability.

What you should get from it

Affiliation, not certification, is what unlocks the right to market as 'CrossFit.'

What you should get from it

Your only permitted use of the brand is stating the credential itself, and only in those specific places.

What you should get from it

Never call yourself 'Certified' at L1; that word is gated to CF-L3 and up.

What you should get from it

Where you coach decides what you can say; only an affiliate lets you advertise classes as CrossFit.

What you should get from it

The trademark rule covers speech too, not just printed ads; even saying 'we do CrossFit' to clients requires affiliation.

What you should get from it

The credential's real perks are the public directory listing and eligibility to advance to L2.

What you should get from it

Four rungs: L1 and L2 are 'Training,' L3 and L4 are 'Certification,' each with its own prerequisites and maintenance.

What you should get from it

Lower rungs are renewed by reattending; the upper certified rungs require ongoing CEUs, training hours, and CPR/AED.

Words to know (plain English)

CrossFit methodology
The training approach and programming itself, free to use and follow on CrossFit.com; what you learned in the course.
CrossFit brand / trademark
The protected CrossFit name and marks. Not free, you can't use them to advertise without a license (affiliation).
License Agreement
The CrossFit Level 1 Trainer Certificate License Agreement you must accept after passing; it spells out what's permitted and prohibited.
Affiliate / affiliation
A licensed CrossFit gym. Affiliation (apply, get accepted, pay the annual fee) is what grants the right to advertise and market using the CrossFit name.
CF-L1 / CrossFit Level 1 Trainer
Your credential after passing the Level 1 course and exam; the only brand wording you may use, and only on a resume, card, or bio.
'Certified' CrossFit Trainer
A title reserved for Level 3 and above; a CF-L1 may NOT use the word 'Certified' to describe themselves.
Trademark infringement
Unauthorized use of a protected brand name; using 'CrossFit' to advertise without affiliating can make a CF-L1 liable under Federal Law.
CrossFit Trainer Directory
A public online database listing licensed CrossFit trainers; being listed is one perk of holding the CF-L1.
CCFT examination
The ANSI-accredited Certified CrossFit Trainer exam required to obtain the CF-L3 credential.
CEU (Continuing Education Unit)
Credit earned through approved learning; CF-L3 and CF-L4 require 50 CEUs (plus 300 training hours and CPR/AED) every 3 years to maintain.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Using the CrossFit name to advertise, market, promote, or solicit business as a CF-L1 (printed OR by word of mouth) breaches the Agreement and risks Federal trademark liability, affiliation is the only path to that right.
  • Calling yourself a 'Certified' CrossFit Trainer at the L1 level is prohibited; that title is reserved for L3 and above.
  • Using CrossFit copyrighted material (the Training Guide, Participant Handbook) to market your services is not allowed.
  • Advertising CrossFit classes from a non-affiliate location (e.g., a commercial gym) using the brand name is a violation.
  • Letting your credential lapse, L1/L2 require reattending every 5 years; L3/L4 require CEUs, training hours, and current CPR/AED every 3 years.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. You hold a CF-L1 and train private clients at a commercial gym (not an affiliate). A client asks you to put 'CrossFit classes here' on a flyer. What can you do?

  1. Call yourself a Certified CrossFit Trainer on the flyer instead
  2. Print the flyer; a CF-L1 lets you advertise CrossFit classes anywhere
  3. Decline to use the CrossFit name to advertise; you may only state 'CF-L1 Trainer' on a card, resume, or bio, and advertising classes as CrossFit requires affiliation
  4. Print it as long as you don't charge extra
  5. Just say it verbally to clients instead, that's allowed
Show answer

Answer: C — Decline to use the CrossFit name to advertise; you may only state 'CF-L1 Trainer' on a card, resume, or bio, and advertising classes as CrossFit requires affiliation. B CF-L1 cannot use the brand to advertise; only affiliation unlocks that, and your sole permitted brand use is stating the credential on a card/resume/bio. B and D both misuse the trademark; E is wrong because word-of-mouth marketing also requires affiliation; A adds a second violation since 'Certified' is reserved for L3+.

Scenario 2. You want to advance from CF-L2 to the Certified Level 3 (CF-L3) credential. What does this section say you need?

  1. A performance evaluation only
  2. 100% participation in the Level 1 course
  3. Nothing beyond holding a CF-L1
  4. A current CF-L2 plus CPR/AED, and pass the ANSI-accredited CCFT examination
  5. Just reattend the Level 2 course

Answer: D — A current CF-L2 plus CPR/AED, and pass the ANSI-accredited CCFT examination. Per the credentials ladder, CF-L3 requires a current CF-L2 and CPR/AED, and is obtained by passing the CCFT exam. E is just L2 maintenance; A is the CF-L4 requirement; B and C describe lower rungs.

Scenario 3. A friend who follows CrossFit.com workouts at home asks if they're breaking any rules by using the programming. What's the accurate answer?

  1. Only if they pay the affiliation fee
  2. Yes, all CrossFit use requires a license
  3. Only if they have a CF-L1
  4. Yes, they owe CEUs
  5. No, the methodology is free to use and follow; only using the CrossFit brand name to advertise or market requires a license
Show answer

Answer: E — No, the methodology is free to use and follow; only using the CrossFit brand name to advertise or market requires a license. The core distinction is methodology vs. brand: the methodology is free to use and follow, and it's the brand name (for advertising/marketing) that requires affiliation. B, C, A, and D all confuse using the free training with licensing the protected name.

L1 · Section 26 of 27 Tier: ___

The Nine Foundational Movements — Teach, See, Correct

Open the official guide

This is your movement cheat sheet for the nine foundational movements of the Level 1 Course — the points of performance to teach, the faults to see, the corrections (verbal and tactile) to fix them, and the teaching progressions that build the harder lifts step by step.

Key ideas to understand

  • Effective coaching is six capacities: teaching, seeing, correcting, group management, presence and attitude, and demonstration. This section drills the first three — teach, see, correct — on the nine foundational movements.
  • Know the nine in order: Air Squat, Front Squat, Overhead Squat, Shoulder Press, Push Press, Push Jerk, Deadlift, Sumo Deadlift High Pull, Medicine-Ball Clean. They're three families (squats, presses, pulls) that build on each other.
  • Teaching = knowing the points of performance (set-up, execution, finish). Seeing = spotting those points and any deviation in real time. Correcting = improving the client's mechanics to better hit the points of performance.
  • Each movement has at least Points of Performance and Common Faults and Corrections. Some also have a Teaching Progression that breaks a complex lift into simple steps. The progressions exist to develop the primary points of performance.
  • The movements stack: the air squat is the cornerstone and feeds the front and overhead squat; the shoulder press feeds the push press, which feeds the push jerk; the deadlift feeds the sumo deadlift high pull, which (with the front squat) feeds the medicine-ball clean.
  • AIR SQUAT — the cornerstone. Set-up: shoulder-width stance. Execution: hips descend back and down, lumbar curve maintained, knees in line with toes, hips descend lower than knees, heels down. Finish: full hip and knee extension.
  • AIR SQUAT faults: losing the neutral spine (lumbar flexion), weight shifting to the toes, improper line of action (hips don't travel back, knees shoot forward), not hitting depth, and knees caving in (not tracking the toes). Plus two named bad squats: the 'immature squat' (all points held but the athlete cantilevers forward onto the quads) and the 'multiple-fault squat' (can't hold lumbar curve, heels, knee tracking, and depth all at once).
  • AIR SQUAT corrections worth knowing by name: lift the chest / raise the arms on the descent (for lumbar flexion); exaggerate weight on heels by lifting the toes, tactile cue hips back and down (for toe shift); block the knees' forward travel by hand (for line of action); cue 'Lower!' and use a depth target (for depth); 'Push your knees out' / 'spread the ground apart' and an outside-knee target (for knees caving). And Squat Therapy — face a wall or racked bar with a depth target, heels to a box, chest close, squat slowly with control on the heels.
  • FRONT SQUAT — the air squat plus a loaded barbell in the front-rack position. New set-up points: loose fingertip grip, hands just outside shoulders, elbows high (upper arm parallel to the ground). All air-squat faults still apply, plus two new ones: improper rack (bar not in contact with the torso) and elbows dropping during the squat. Corrections: open/fingertip grip with the bar on the fingertips, 'Elbows high!', manually adjust the rack; for dropping elbows, have them move elbows away from your hands, cue 'Elbows up!' and lift the chest.
  • OVERHEAD SQUAT — the air squat with load locked out overhead. New set-up points: shoulders push up into the bar, arms extended, wide (pass-through) grip, armpits face forward; in execution the bar moves over the middle of the foot. New faults: inactive overhead position (flexed elbows / inactive shoulders) and the bar drifting forward of the frontal plane. Corrections: press the bar up, tactile cue elbows straight + shoulders up + armpits forward; press up and pull the bar back over midfoot or slightly behind the frontal plane.
  • SHOULDER PRESS — foundational to all overhead lifts; keys are a neutral spine, a straight bar path, and a correct overhead position. Set-up: hip-width stance, full grip, elbows slightly in front of the bar, hands just outside shoulders, bar rests on torso. Finish: full arm extension, spine neutral, legs extended, heels down, shoulders push up into the bar, bar over the middle of the foot.
  • SHOULDER PRESS faults: overextending the spine (ribs sticking out), bar finishing forward of the frontal plane, bent elbows / inactive shoulders, and the bar arcing around the face instead of straight up. Corrections: tighten the abs by pulling the rib cage down (then recheck overhead), wider grip until flexibility improves; press up and pull back, tactile push the bar back; 'Press up!' with a tactile lockout; pull the head back out of the bar's way, check elbows aren't too low at set-up, block the forward bar path with a piece of PVC.
  • PUSH PRESS — the shoulder press plus a vertical dip and rapid hip extension to add velocity. Same set-up, bar path, spine and overhead positions as the shoulder press. Execution: torso stays vertical as hips and knees flex in the dip; hips and legs extend, THEN the arms press; heels stay down until hips and knees extend. Teaching progression (4 steps): (1) dip and hold, (2) dip-drive slow, (3) dip-drive fast, (4) full push press.
  • PUSH PRESS faults: forward inclination of the chest in the dip, 'muted hips' (hips push forward during the dip instead of flexing), and pressing early (the press starts before the hip extends). Corrections: hold the dip and adjust them upright / shorter dip / 'Knees forward' / stand in front of them, and Dip Therapy (stand against a target, hips and shoulder blades touching, dip-drive keeping contact); tactile cue to create hip flexion in the dip and 'Push the hips back slightly'; for pressing early, go back to two dip-drives before the press and hold a hand at standing height for them to drive up and hit before pressing.
  • PUSH JERK — the push press plus a press UNDER the bar. Same set-up, bar path, spine/overhead, dip and drive. After hip extension, the athlete presses against the bar and receives it in a partial overhead squat, then stands to finish. Teaching progression (4 steps): (1) jump and land, hands at sides, stick the landing before standing; (2) jump and land, hands at shoulders, stick the landing; (3) jump and extend the arms after the hip opens, stick the landing with arms overhead; (4) full push jerk with PVC.
  • PUSH JERK faults (all shoulder-press and push-press faults still apply): lack of full hip extension, poor/inactive overhead position especially when receiving the bar, and lowering the bar before standing all the way up. Corrections: 'Jump higher' and a hand at standing height to hit during the drive, plus going back to steps 1–3 and squeezing glutes/quads before pressing under; press up on the bar in the receiving position before standing; keep the bar overhead until hips and knees are fully extended, with a tactile hand to hit before lowering.
  • DEADLIFT — foundational to all pulling lifts; the spine stays neutral the whole time and the bar stays close to the frontal plane. Set-up: hip-to-shoulder-width stance, full grip, hands just outside hips, shoulders slightly in front of or over the bar, arms straight with the bar on the shins, eyes on the horizon. Execution: lumbar curve maintained, hips and shoulders rise at the same rate until the bar passes the knee, THEN the hips open, bar over the middle of the foot, heels down. Finish: full hip and knee extension.
  • DEADLIFT faults: loss of lumbar curve (spinal flexion) — abort and drop the load; weight shifting to the toes; shoulders behind the bar at set-up; hips too high at set-up; hips not moving back to start the descent; bar losing contact with the legs; and hips rising before the chest (the stiff-legged deadlift) or shoulders rising without the hips so the bar travels around the knees. Corrections include: cue 'lift the chest' at a lighter load and don't relent; pull the hips back and settle on the heels; raise or lower the hips to set shoulders over/slightly in front of the bar; initiate the return by pushing hips back and delaying the knee bend until the bar is below the knees; 'Pull the bar in to your legs the whole time' with a tactile cue to engage the upper back; cue the chest and hips to rise in unison; 'Push the knees back as the chest rises.'
  • SUMO DEADLIFT HIGH PULL (SDLHP) — the deadlift with a wider stance and a narrower grip, plus added velocity and range of motion. A classic core-to-extremity movement: hips and legs accelerate the bar before the arms finish the pull. Set-up: slightly wider than shoulder-width stance, hands inside the legs with a full grip. Execution: lumbar curve maintained, hips and shoulders rise together until the bar passes the knee, then hips extend rapidly, shoulders shrug THEN the arms pull, elbows move high and outside, bar over midfoot. Finish: full hip and knee extension with the bar pulled under the chin. Teaching progression (4 steps): (1) sumo deadlift, (2) sumo deadlift-shrug slow, (3) sumo deadlift-shrug fast, (4) full SDLHP.
  • SDLHP faults (all deadlift faults still apply): pulling early (shrug or arm bend before the hips fully extend), pulling with the elbows low and inside, incorrect descent (hips flex before the arms extend), and shoulders rolling forward in the set-up or pull. Corrections: go back to the deadlift-shrug at a controllable speed, then two deadlift-shrugs for every one full rep, with a tactile cue to hit your hands with the shoulders before the arms pull; 'Elbows high!' with a tactile target for where elbows finish; slow the descent and extend the arms first; fix the shoulder position at set-up or top of pull, and widen the grip and/or reduce range of motion to keep shoulders in place.
  • MEDICINE-BALL CLEAN — builds on the deadlift and SDLHP; the new piece is the pull-under, which brings the ball to the front-rack (support) position. Set-up: shoulder-width stance, ball between the feet, palms on the ball, shoulders over the ball, eyes on the horizon. Execution: lumbar curve maintained, hips extend rapidly, shoulders then shrug, heels down until hips and knees extend, then the arms pull under to the bottom of a squat with the ball close to the body. Finish: full hip and knee extension with the ball at the rack. Teaching progression (5 steps): (1) deadlift, (2) deadlift-shrug fast, (3) front squat, (4) pull-under, (5) full medicine-ball clean.
  • MEDICINE-BALL CLEAN faults: lack of full hip extension; curling the ball; collapsing in the receiving position; receiving too high; tossing/flicking the ball without pulling under; and not standing up before lowering the weight. Corrections: two deadlift-shrugs per clean with a tactile head cue before pulling under; stand in front (or use a wall) to block the curl and 'Elbows high and outside!' (keep the laces facing out); lift the chest in the front squat and rehearse the pull-under with sound front-squat mechanics; hold the ball at the peak of the shrug to rehearse pulling under without raising it (and two pull-unders per clean); have them hold the ball with palms/fists only to feel the hand rotation; and keep the ball at the chest until the shoulder hits a target so they stand fully before lowering.

Words to know (plain English)

Points of performance
The specific positions and actions that define correct execution of a movement (set-up, execution, finish) — what a trainer teaches and watches for.
Teaching
Knowing the points of performance for a movement, including set-up and finish positions, well enough to convey them to a client.
Seeing
Assessing the points of performance — and any deviation from them — in real time as a client moves.
Correcting
Improving a client's mechanics so they better adhere to the points of performance, using verbal, visual, or tactile cues.
Teaching progression
A breakdown of a complex movement into simple steps, each developing a primary point of performance, so the full movement can be built up safely.
Lumbar curve
The natural inward curve of the lower spine that must be maintained (a neutral spine) under load to keep the back safe in squats, deadlifts, and pulls.
Front-rack position
Supporting a load on the front of the torso (bar resting on the torso, elbows high; for the med-ball clean, the ball at the chest) — the receiving/support position for front squats and cleans.
Frontal plane
The vertical plane running side-to-side through the body; loads in overhead and pulling movements should stay over the midfoot, in or just behind this plane.
Dip-drive
The vertical dip of the torso followed by rapid hip and leg extension that adds velocity to the push press and push jerk — hips fire before the arms.
Press under / pull-under
Actively driving the body down beneath the bar (push jerk) or ball (clean) to receive it in a partial squat or front rack, rather than pressing or muscling the load all the way up.
Muted hips
A push-press fault where the hips push forward during the dip instead of flexing, which kills the drive out of the bottom.
Immature squat
A squat in which all points of performance are technically held, but the athlete cantilevers forward excessively onto the quads to keep balance.
Multiple-fault squat
A squat in which the athlete can't simultaneously maintain the lumbar curve, keep weight on the heels, track the knees over the feet, and reach depth.
Stiff-legged deadlift (as a fault)
A deadlift fault where the hips rise before the chest, breaking the rule that hips and shoulders rise at the same rate until the bar passes the knee.
Squat Therapy
A corrective drill: the athlete faces a wall or racked bar with a depth target, heels to a box, chest close, and squats slowly with control and weight on the heels.
Tactile cue
A correction delivered by touch — guiding the bar, hips, elbows, or shoulders with a hand to show the athlete the correct position or path.
Core to extremity
The principle that force is generated from the center of the body outward to the limbs — seen clearly in the sumo deadlift high pull, where hips and legs drive the bar before the arms finish.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Loss of the lumbar curve (spinal flexion) under load in the deadlift is the line you don't cross: the prescribed correction is to ABORT the current lift and reduce the load until a neutral spine can be held — never coach through a rounding back.
  • Don't add load or velocity before the points of performance hold. The whole logic of the teaching progressions is that complex lifts get built from sound positions — regress the athlete (e.g., back to dip-drives or deadlift-shrugs) rather than push a faulty pattern faster or heavier.
  • Faults compound up the families. Because the front/overhead squat carry every air-squat fault, the push press/jerk carry every shoulder-press fault, and the SDLHP/med-ball clean carry every deadlift fault — fix the foundation movement first instead of chasing the symptom in the advanced lift.
  • 'Pressing early' (push press/jerk) and 'pulling early' (SDLHP/clean) are timing faults: the arms fire before the hips finish. Correct the sequence (hips, then shrug/press) — don't just cue harder arms.
  • Use tactile cues appropriately and safely — guide position with a light touch (bar path, elbow height, hips back), not by forcing a body into place; pair them with clear verbal cues and re-check the position afterward (e.g., recheck the overhead position after pulling the rib cage down).
  • This section is an introductory guide, not the complete book of corrections — don't treat its fault list as the only thing that can go wrong, and keep developing your eye beyond it.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. In the bottom of an air squat, a member's knees collapse inward (not tracking the toes). What's the best correction from this section?

  1. Tell them to shift their weight onto the toes
  2. Cue 'Push your knees out' or 'Spread the ground apart with your feet,' and use a target on the outside of the knee to reach toward
  3. Have them lift the chest and raise the arms on the descent
  4. Add load to force the knees into a stable position
  5. Cue 'Lower!' and have them squat to a deeper target
Show answer

Answer: B — Cue 'Push your knees out' or 'Spread the ground apart with your feet,' and use a target on the outside of the knee to reach toward. Knees caving is corrected with 'push your knees out' / 'spread the ground apart' and an outside-knee target. E addresses depth; C addresses a lost lumbar curve; D never fixes a movement fault with more load; A would create a different fault (weight on toes).

Scenario 2. Mid-set on moderately heavy deadlifts, you see your athlete's lower back round (loss of lumbar curve). What do you do?

  1. Abort the current lift and decrease the load to where the lumbar curve can be maintained, then cue 'lift the chest' at the lighter weight and don't relent
  2. Cue 'eyes up' and let them keep pulling the same weight
  3. Add weight so the heavier bar forces a tighter back
  4. Switch them immediately to the sumo deadlift high pull
  5. Let them finish the set, then mention it afterward
Show answer

Answer: A — Abort the current lift and decrease the load to where the lumbar curve can be maintained, then cue 'lift the chest' at the lighter weight and don't relent. The guide's explicit correction for spinal flexion is to abort and drop the load until the curve holds. E and B coach through a dangerous fault; C is backwards; D adds velocity and range of motion to a pattern that's already failing.

Scenario 3. On the push press, an athlete starts pressing the bar with the arms before the hips finish extending. Best correction?

  1. Tell them to press harder and faster with the arms
  2. Add load to slow the press down
  3. Cue a shorter dip
  4. Take them back in the teaching progression to perform two dip-drives before adding the press, and hold a hand at their standing height to drive up and hit before pressing
  5. Have them widen their grip
Show answer

Answer: D — Take them back in the teaching progression to perform two dip-drives before adding the press, and hold a hand at their standing height to drive up and hit before pressing. 'Pressing early' is a timing fault fixed by regressing to dip-drives (hips before arms) and giving a target to drive into before pressing. A reinforces the arm-first error; C addresses a forward-chest dip; B and E address different faults entirely.

Scenario 4. A new athlete keeps curling the medicine ball up with the arms instead of driving with the hips and pulling under. What's the best fix?

  1. Add a heavier ball so they can't curl it
  2. Cue 'Lower!' to get more depth in the catch
  3. Tell them to receive the ball higher
  4. Have them skip the pull-under entirely
  5. Stand in front of the athlete (or use a wall) to block the curl, cue 'Elbows high and outside,' and require the ball's laces to face out the whole movement
Show answer

Answer: E — Stand in front of the athlete (or use a wall) to block the curl, cue 'Elbows high and outside,' and require the ball's laces to face out the whole movement. Those are exactly the prescribed corrections for curling the ball — physically block it, cue elbows high and outside, and keep the laces facing out. B and C target other faults; A doesn't teach the correct hip-driven mechanics; D removes the defining skill of the clean.

Scenario 5. On the overhead squat, the bar drifts forward of the frontal plane as the athlete descends. Which correction matches the guide?

  1. Tell them to let the bar travel forward to counterbalance
  2. Cue the athlete to press the bar up and pull it back over the midfoot or slightly behind the frontal plane
  3. Block the knees' forward travel with your hand
  4. Cue 'Elbows high' as in the front squat
  5. Have them narrow their grip until it's shoulder-width
Show answer

Answer: B — Cue the athlete to press the bar up and pull it back over the midfoot or slightly behind the frontal plane. For a bar forward of the frontal plane, the guide says press up and pull the bar back over midfoot or slightly behind the plane. E works against the wide pass-through grip the overhead squat needs; A is the fault, not the fix; D is a front-squat cue; C is an air-squat line-of-action correction.

Scenario 6. On the sumo deadlift high pull, an athlete finishes the pull with the elbows low and inside. Best correction?

  1. Switch them to a conventional deadlift
  2. Cue 'Elbows high!' and give a tactile cue showing where the elbows should finish
  3. Widen the grip to shoulder-width
  4. Have them pull earlier, before the hips extend
  5. Tell them to keep the elbows pinned to the ribs
Show answer

Answer: B — Cue 'Elbows high!' and give a tactile cue showing where the elbows should finish. The fix for low/inside elbows is 'Elbows high!' plus a tactile target for the finish position. C changes the lift's narrow grip; D creates the 'pulling early' fault; A abandons the movement; E reinforces the exact error.

L1 · Section 27 of 27 Tier: ___

Four Additional Movements — Pull-up, Thruster, Muscle-up, Snatch

Open the official guide

Your coaching cheat sheet for the four extra Level 1 movements: the exact set-up points, the common faults, the fix for each fault, and the step-by-step teaching progression.

Key ideas to understand

  • These four movements — the pull-up, thruster, muscle-up, and snatch — are taught on top of the nine foundational movements for two reasons: to clean up the participants' own mechanics, AND to give you a teaching method for more complicated movements. They're built on top of the basics you already know.
  • Each movement is laid out the same three ways as the nine foundational movements: (1) Points of Performance (the set-up/execution/finish checkpoints), (2) Common Faults and Corrections, and (3) Teaching Progression. Read each movement through that same lens.
  • The neuroendocrine reminder up top still drives exercise selection: regardless of sport or goals, the criterion is neuroendocrine effect, and these high-value moves are the shortest path to results.
  • PULL-UP — the kipping pull-up is CrossFit's default. Why kip? The hips assist the upper-body pull, so the athlete does more work in less time (higher power). But CrossFit wants a strict pull-up FIRST to build baseline shoulder stability and strength before kipping.
  • Pull-up points of performance: hands just outside shoulder width, hang with arms fully extended, pull until the chin clears the bar, push away from the bar to start the descent, initiate the kip swing from the shoulders, and as the feet swing forward push the bar straight down with the arms — chest up, eyes forward — then return to full extension to begin the next rep.
  • Pull-up faults and fixes: (1) starting the swing with the legs → go back in the progression to the kip swing and drive it from the shoulders; (2) not pushing away after clearing the bar (dropping straight down instead of riding the arc) → pause at the top focusing on pushing away, do two kip swings between reps, or set a target behind to push toward; (3) losing midline stabilization by overextending the spine or exaggerating the swing → tighten the kip by keeping legs together and knees straight, or put a towel between the feet to hold a tight body.
  • Pull-up teaching progression (5 steps): 1) kip swings, 2) two kip swings and a kip, 3) two kip swings and a pull-up, 4) two kip swings + a pull-up + two kip swings, 5) multiple pull-ups without extra swings. The whole progression is built around owning the kip swing before stringing reps.
  • THRUSTER — it's a front squat plus a push press in one continuous movement. The athlete must move core-to-extremity: extend the hip FIRST, then press. Two key differences from a plain front squat: the thruster uses a FULL grip on the bar (not the loose fingertip grip) and a LOWER elbow position.
  • Thruster points of performance: elbows in front of the bar, bar on the front rack, hands just outside the shoulders, full grip, shoulder-width stance; hips descend back and down below the knees with the lumbar curve maintained, knees tracking the toes, elbows off the knees; hips and knees extend rapidly and THEN the arms press; heels stay down until the hips and knees extend; bar travels over the middle of the foot; finish at full hip, knee, and arm extension.
  • Thruster faults and fixes: most front-squat and push-press faults carry over, plus (1) pressing the bar before extending the hips → tactile cue, have them hit your hand before pressing; (2) descending into the squat before the bar is racked → take them back in the progression and pause in the rack position before squatting.
  • Thruster teaching progression (4 steps): 1) front squat, 2) push press (wide stance), 3) thruster pausing at the reset, 4) multiple thrusters with no pausing at any point. You build the two halves separately, then fuse them and remove the pause.
  • MUSCLE-UP — it's a pull-up and a dip combined into one movement: the athlete pulls from a hang to a position of support above the rings. The two keys that link the pull-up to the dip are the FALSE GRIP and the positioning of the rings during the transition. Because the rings are dynamic and unstable, CrossFit wants a STRICT muscle-up before any kipping muscle-up.
  • Muscle-up points of performance: rings about shoulder-width apart, false grip on the rings, hang with arms extended; pull the rings to the sternum as the torso leans back; move the chest over the rings while hands and elbows stay close to the body; finish at full arm extension in the support position.
  • Muscle-up faults and fixes: (1) losing the false grip → make sure the false grip is set before the rep, and allow bent arms while strength to hold it with straight elbows develops; (2) staying too vertical in the pull so the rings can't get into transition position → lean back so the rings pull to the chest; (3) letting the elbows flare during the pull or transition → keep the elbows close to the ribcage throughout; (4) not pulling the rings low enough before transitioning → cue lean-back-and-pull-to-the-sternum, or go back to the kneeling muscle-up drill at a challenging ring height.
  • Muscle-up teaching progression (5 steps): 1) ring support, 2) ring dip, 3) false grip, 4) kneeling muscle-ups (raise the rings or move the feet further forward to make it harder), 5) the muscle-up. Notice it teaches the support/dip and the false grip before ever attempting the full pull-to-support.
  • SNATCH — 'the world's fastest lift' — moves the barbell from the ground to overhead in one movement, received at the bottom of an overhead squat. Its complexity is exactly why it's so valuable for CrossFit athletes.
  • Snatch points of performance: hip-width stance; hands wide enough that the bar rests in the crease of the hips when hips and knees are extended; hook grip on the bar; shoulders slightly in front of the bar; eyes on the horizon; lumbar curve maintained; hips and shoulders rise at the SAME rate; then the hips extend rapidly; heels down until hips and knees extend; shoulders shrug, followed by a pull-under with the arms; the bar is received at the bottom of an overhead squat; finish at full hip, knee, and arm extension with the bar over the middle of the foot.
  • Snatch faults and fixes (most deadlift, sumo deadlift high pull, and medicine-ball clean faults also apply): (1) lack of hip extension → cue 'Jump higher!', hold your hand at standing head height and have them hit it on the drive, or do two snatch deadlift-shrugs per snatch; (2) not moving the elbows high and outside / swinging the bar around the body → tactile cue with the PVC, cue 'Elbows high and outside!', cue brushing the shirt with the bar; (3) shoulders rise without the hips → cue 'Push the knees back as your chest rises,' check the set-up isn't too low, tactile cue at hips and shoulders to lift together; (4) hips rise without the shoulders → cue 'Keep the chest lifted as you straighten your legs,' check the set-up isn't too high, tactile cue to lift in unison.
  • Snatch teaching progression (6 steps): 1) deadlift to mid-thigh, 2) deadlift-shrug, 3) muscle snatch, 4) overhead squat, 5) hang snatch, 6) snatch. The progression builds the pull, the shrug, the turnover, and the overhead receiving position in pieces before putting the full lift together from the floor.
  • Big-picture pattern across all four: each one is a combination of simpler movements you already coach (pull-up = strict pull + kip; thruster = front squat + push press; muscle-up = pull-up + dip; snatch = deadlift + shrug + overhead squat). The progressions all teach the pieces first, then assemble. Faults are corrected primarily by stepping BACK in the progression, not by adding cues alone.
