What you should get from it

These four exist to clean up the coach's own movement and to model how to teach harder skills — they sit on top of the nine foundational movements.

What you should get from it

Every movement in this section is organized the exact same way — read each one through those three headings.

What you should get from it

Kipping gives higher power (hips assist the pull), but strict comes first to build shoulder stability and strength.

What you should get from it

Each pull-up fault has a specific fix, and most send the athlete back to the kip swing or use a simple tactile aid (target, towel).

What you should get from it

The progression is built entirely around owning the kip swing before stringing reps together.

What you should get from it

The thruster is two known movements fused; the giveaways are the full grip, the lower elbows, and hip-extension-before-press.

What you should get from it

The signature thruster fault is breaking the core-to-extremity sequence — the fix is a tactile cue to extend the hip first.

What you should get from it

The muscle-up is a pull-up + dip; the false grip and ring position make the transition work, and strict comes before kipping because the rings are dynamic.

What you should get from it

Most muscle-up faults trace back to the transition — keep the false grip, lean back, keep elbows in, and pull the rings to the sternum.

What you should get from it

The support, dip, and false grip are taught before ever attempting the full pull-to-support — and the kneeling drill is the scalable rehearsal.

What you should get from it

The snatch takes the bar floor-to-overhead in one move into an overhead squat; the hips and shoulders must rise together off the floor.

What you should get from it

No hip extension means no power — the fixes all reinforce a violent stand/jump and the shrug.

What you should get from it

Both are set-up problems (too low vs. too high) fixed with chest/knee cues and a tactile cue to lift hips and shoulders in unison.

What you should get from it

The snatch is built in pieces — pull, shrug, turnover, overhead receiving — before assembling the full lift from the floor.

What you should get from it

You don't re-learn faults from scratch — the foundational-movement faults transfer, and these sections only add the new ones.

Words to know (plain English)

Points of performance
The specific position and execution checkpoints (set-up, execution, finish) that define correct movement and tell you exactly what to coach and watch for.
Teaching progression
The ordered series of smaller drills used to build a movement piece by piece. Also the place you send an athlete BACK to when a fault shows up.
Kipping pull-up
CrossFit's default pull-up, where a coordinated swing of the hips assists the upper-body pull so the athlete does more work in less time (higher power).
Strict pull-up
A pull-up with no kip — pure upper-body strength. CrossFit wants this first to build baseline shoulder stability and strength before kipping.
Kip swing
The shoulder-initiated swing that powers the kipping pull-up. The foundation of the entire pull-up progression.
Midline stabilization
Keeping the spine braced and neutral instead of overextending or exaggerating the swing — a key fault point on the pull-up.
Thruster
A front squat and a push press fused into one continuous movement, using a full grip and lower elbows, driven core-to-extremity (extend the hip, then press).
Core-to-extremity
Moving from the center of the body outward — extend the powerful hip first, then the arms press. Breaking this sequence is the signature thruster fault.
Front rack
The bar resting across the front of the shoulders with elbows up — the racked position the thruster squats from and presses out of.
Muscle-up
A pull-up and a dip combined: the athlete pulls from a hang to full support above the rings. The false grip and ring position make it work.
False grip
A grip set so the wrist rides on top of the ring, keeping the hand high enough to transition from the pull to the dip. Losing it is a core muscle-up fault.
Kneeling muscle-up
A scaled muscle-up drill done from the knees; raise the rings or move the feet forward to make it harder. The go-to rehearsal for the full muscle-up.
Snatch
'The world's fastest lift' — the barbell goes from the ground to overhead in one movement, received at the bottom of an overhead squat.
Hook grip
Gripping the bar by trapping the thumb under the first fingers — the secure grip used for the snatch.
Pull-under
After the shrug, actively pulling the body down under the bar to receive it overhead — a key part of the snatch turnover.
Muscle snatch
A snatch received without dropping under the bar (no squat) — a progression step that teaches the turnover and overhead path.

Red flags & common mistakes

  • Develop a STRICT pull-up before kipping (shoulder stability/strength) and a STRICT muscle-up before kipping (the rings are dynamic). Skipping straight to kipping is a safety and scope mistake.
  • On the thruster, never let the press start before the hips extend — that breaks the core-to-extremity pattern. Fix with a tactile cue, not just words.
  • Most faults are corrected by stepping BACK in the teaching progression, not by piling on more verbal cues. If a cue isn't landing, regress the drill.
  • On the snatch, the hips and shoulders must rise at the same rate off the floor — shoulders-rising-first or hips-rising-first are usually set-up errors (too low or too high), so check the set-up before anything else.
  • Don't re-teach faults you've already covered — front-squat and push-press faults carry into the thruster; deadlift, sumo deadlift high pull, and medicine-ball clean faults carry into the snatch. Layer the new ones on top.
  • The false grip and ring/transition position are the make-or-break of the muscle-up; if the athlete loses the false grip or stays too vertical, the transition fails.

Test yourself

Scenario 1. A member can grind out one ugly strict pull-up and immediately asks you to teach kipping so she can 'do more.' What's the best coaching move per this section?

  1. Build a solid strict pull-up first for shoulder stability and strength, then introduce the kip starting with the kip swing.
  2. Teach kipping right away since the kipping pull-up is CrossFit's default and produces higher power.
  3. Skip pull-ups entirely and put her on a muscle-up progression.
  4. Tell her kipping is unsafe and should never be taught.
  5. Let her kip with a towel between her feet to fix it.
Show answer

Answer: A — Build a solid strict pull-up first for shoulder stability and strength, then introduce the kip starting with the kip swing.. CrossFit makes the kipping pull-up the default BUT recommends developing a strict pull-up first to build baseline shoulder stability and strength, then teaching the kip beginning with the kip swing. B rushes past that baseline. C jumps to a harder movement she isn't ready for. E is a fix for a midline fault, not a substitute for the strict-first principle. D overstates it — kipping is taught, just after strict.

Scenario 2. During thrusters, an athlete keeps starting the arm press while still standing up out of the squat — arms and hips fire at the same time. Which correction fits this section best?

  1. Tell him to use a loose fingertip grip like a front squat.
  2. Add load so he's forced to use his legs.
  3. Use a tactile cue — have him hit your hand with the bar before pressing — to enforce hip extension first (core-to-extremity).
  4. Have him pause at the bottom of the squat longer.
  5. Switch him to a push jerk instead.
Show answer

Answer: C — Use a tactile cue — have him hit your hand with the bar before pressing — to enforce hip extension first (core-to-extremity).. The named fault is 'pressing the bar before extending the hips,' and the listed correction is a tactile cue: instruct the athlete to hit the hand before pressing, restoring the core-to-extremity sequence (extend the hip, then press). A is wrong — the thruster specifically uses a FULL grip, not the fingertip grip. B and D don't target the timing fault. E changes the movement instead of fixing it.

Scenario 3. An athlete keeps failing the muscle-up transition: he pulls but stays bolt-upright and the rings never get into position to come over. What does this section say to do?

  1. Cue him to keep the body vertical and pull straight down harder.
  2. Have him lean back so the rings can be pulled to the chest/sternum, getting them into transition position.
  3. Tell him to widen the rings well beyond shoulder width.
  4. Add a kip immediately to generate more momentum.
  5. Remove the false grip to make the pull easier.
Show answer

Answer: B — Have him lean back so the rings can be pulled to the chest/sternum, getting them into transition position.. The listed fault is 'keeping the body too vertical in the pull so the rings will not be in position for an efficient transition,' and the correction is to lean back so the rings can be pulled to the chest. A reinforces the exact fault. C contradicts the shoulder-width ring set-up. E removes the false grip, which is a key that links the pull to the dip. D pushes kipping before a strict muscle-up, which CrossFit says to develop first.

Scenario 4. On the snatch, an athlete's hips shoot up first off the floor while the chest drops, so the bar gets pulled with the back instead of the legs. Best fix from this section?

  1. Cue 'Keep the chest lifted as you straighten your legs,' confirm the set-up isn't too high, and give a tactile cue at the hips and shoulders to lift in unison.
  2. Cue 'Elbows high and outside!' through the turnover.
  3. Move straight to the full snatch from the floor to build speed.
  4. Cue 'Jump higher!' and add snatch deadlift-shrugs.
  5. Widen the grip so the bar sits lower at set-up.
Show answer

Answer: A — Cue 'Keep the chest lifted as you straighten your legs,' confirm the set-up isn't too high, and give a tactile cue at the hips and shoulders to lift in unison.. Hips rising without the shoulders is its own listed fault, fixed by cueing 'Keep the chest lifted as you straighten your legs,' checking the set-up isn't too high, and a tactile cue to lift hips and shoulders in unison. D addresses lack of hip extension, a different fault. B addresses the elbows/bar path, a different fault. E doesn't target the lift-off timing. C ignores the progression — when a fault appears you step back, not skip ahead.

Scenario 5. You're about to start a brand-new member on the snatch. Which sequence reflects the section's teaching progression?

  1. Muscle-up → thruster → snatch.
  2. Snatch from the floor → hang snatch → overhead squat → muscle snatch → deadlift-shrug → deadlift to mid-thigh.
  3. Front squat → push press → thruster → snatch.
  4. Overhead squat → snatch → deadlift → shrug.
  5. Deadlift to mid-thigh → deadlift-shrug → muscle snatch → overhead squat → hang snatch → snatch.
Show answer

Answer: E — Deadlift to mid-thigh → deadlift-shrug → muscle snatch → overhead squat → hang snatch → snatch.. The snatch teaching progression runs deadlift to mid-thigh, deadlift-shrug, muscle snatch, overhead squat, hang snatch, then the full snatch — building the pull, shrug, turnover, and overhead receiving position before assembling the lift from the floor. B reverses it (starting with the hardest version). D is incomplete and out of order. A and C are progressions for other movements (the muscle-up and the thruster), not the snatch.
